Convolutions of Goss and Pellarin L L -series
Wei-Cheng Huang, Matthew A. Papanikolas

Abstract
. We establish special value results of convolutions of Goss and Pellarin L L -series attached to Drinfeld modules that take values in Tate algebras. Applying the class module formula of Demeslay to certain rigid analytic twists of one Drinfeld module by another, we extend the special value formula for the Pellarin L L -function associated to the Carlitz module and the Anderson-Thakur function to Drinfeld modules of arbitrary rank and their rigid analytic trivializations. By way of the theory of Schur polynomials these identities take the form of specializations of convolutions of Rankin-Selberg type.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary 11M38; Secondary 11G09, 11M32

1.
Introduction

Let
\mathbb{F}_{q}
be a field with
q=p^{m}
elements for
p
a prime. For a variable
\theta
we let
A\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathbb{F}_{q}[\theta]
be a polynomial ring in
\theta
over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
, and let
K\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathbb{F}_{q}(\theta)
be its fraction field. We take
K_{\infty}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}={\mathbb{F}_{q}(\!(\theta^{-1})\!)}
for the completion of
K
at
\infty
, and let
\mathbb{C}_{\infty}
be the completion of an algebraic closure of
K_{\infty}
. We normalize the
\infty
-adic norm
{\lvert\,\cdot\,\rvert}_{\infty}
on
\mathbb{C}_{\infty}
so that
{\lvert\theta\rvert}_{\infty}=q
, and letting
\deg\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=-\ord_{\infty}=\log_{q}{\lvert\,\cdot\,\rvert}_{\infty}
, we see that
\deg a=\deg_{\theta}a
for any
a\in A
. Finally, we let
A_{+}
denote the monic elements of
A
.

For a variable
z
independent from
\theta
, we let
\mathbb{T}_{z}\subseteq{\mathbb{C}_{\infty}[\![z]\!]}
denote the Tate algebra of power series that converge on the closed unit disk of
\mathbb{C}_{\infty}
, and we let
\mathbb{T}_{z}(K_{\infty})\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathbb{T}_{z}\cap{K_{\infty}[\![z]\!]}
. We let
\widehat{\mathbb{L}}_{z}
(resp.
\mathbb{K}_{\infty}
) denote the completion of the fraction field of
\mathbb{T}_{z}
(resp.
\mathbb{T}_{z}(K_{\infty})
), and we note that
\mathbb{T}_{z}(K_{\infty})={\mathbb{F}_{q}[z](\!(\theta^{-1})\!)}
and
\mathbb{K}_{\infty}={\mathbb{F}_{q}(z)(\!(\theta^{-1})\!)}
. The Gauss norm
\lVert\,\cdot\,\rVert
on
\mathbb{T}_{z}
extends uniquely to
\widehat{\mathbb{L}}_{z}
. We let
\mathbb{A}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathbb{F}_{q}(z)[\theta]
.

1.1.
Motivation

In groundbreaking work Pellarin
[
61
]
introduced a new class of
L
-functions that take values in Tate algebras. In particular, he defined for
s\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
,

(1.1.1)

L(\mathbb{A},s)=\sum_{a\in A_{+}}\frac{a(z)}{a^{s}}\quad\in\mathbb{T}_{z}(K_{\infty}),

which for fixed
s
is in fact an entire function of
z
. By ordering the sum appropriately by degree,
L(\mathbb{A},s)
can be extended to all
s\in\mathbb{Z}
. This
L
-function possesses intriguing special value formulas, such as

(1.1.2)

L(\mathbb{A},1)=-\frac{\widetilde{\pi}}{(z-\theta)\omega_{z}},

where
\widetilde{\pi}\in\mathbb{C}_{\infty}
is the Carlitz period (see Example
2.4.9
) and

(1.1.3)

\omega_{z}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=(-\theta)^{1/(q-1)}\prod_{i=0}^{\infty}\biggl(1-\frac{z}{\theta^{q^{i}}}\biggr)^{-1}\quad\in\mathbb{T}_{z}^{\times}

is the Anderson-Thakur function defined in
[
3
]
. Subsequently, Anglès, Pellarin, and Tavares Ribeiro
[
8
]
considered classes of Drinfeld modules over
\widehat{\mathbb{L}}_{z}
defined by conjugating the Carlitz module by
\omega_{z}
or similar functions. In particular,
if we let
\mathbf{A}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathbb{F}_{q}(z)[t]
be the polynomial ring in a new variable
t
, they defined the
\mathbb{F}_{q}(z)
-algebra homomorphism
\mathbf{C}:\mathbf{A}\to\mathbb{A}[\tau]
so that

(1.1.4)

\mathbf{C}_{t}=\omega_{z}^{-1}\cdot\mathsf{C}_{t}\cdot\omega_{z}=\theta+(z-\theta)\tau.

Then
\mathbf{C}
is a twist of the Carlitz module
\mathsf{C}
(see Example
2.4.3
) by
\omega_{z}
, taking values in the twisted polynomial ring
\mathbb{A}[\tau]
in the
q
-th power Frobenius operator
\tau
on
\mathbb{C}_{\infty}
that is extended
\mathbb{F}_{q}(z)
-linearly to
\widehat{\mathbb{L}}_{z}
(see §
2.1.5
).

About the same time Taelman
[
65
,
66
,
67
]
developed a theory of special values of Goss
L
-functions attached to Drinfeld modules defined over finite extensions of
K
. Although at first unrelated to Pellarin’s
L
-functions, Anglès, Pellarin, and Tavares Ribeiro
[
8
]
used Demeslay’s extension
[
24
,
25
]
of Taelman’s class module formula (see Theorem
5.3.10
) to establish a connection. Demeslay’s formula here is an identity in
\mathbb{K}_{\infty}
,

(1.1.5)

\prod_{f}\frac{{[\mathbb{F}_{f}(z)]}_{\mathbb{A}}}{{[\mathbf{C}(\mathbb{F}_{f}(z))]}_{\mathbb{A}}}=\mathrm{Reg}_{\mathbf{C}}\cdot{[\mathrm{H}(\mathbf{C})]}_{\mathbb{A}}.

On the left-hand side the product is taken over all irreducible
f\in A_{+}
, for which we write
\mathbb{F}_{f}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=A/fA
, and for a finitely generated torsion
\mathbf{A}
-module
M
,
{[M]}_{\mathbb{A}}
denotes the
\mathbb{A}
-order
of
M
, i.e., the monic generator of the Fitting ideal of
M
in
\mathbf{A}
, coerced into
\mathbb{A}
. Moreover,

\prod_{f}\frac{{[\mathbb{F}_{f}(z)]}_{\mathbb{A}}}{{[\mathbf{C}(\mathbb{F}_{f}(z))]}_{\mathbb{A}}}=\prod_{f}\frac{f}{f-f(z)}=L(\mathbb{A},1).

The right-hand side of (
1.1.5
) includes the regulator of
\mathbf{C}
and the
\mathbb{A}
-order of the class module
\mathrm{H}(\mathbf{C})
. In this case
\mathrm{Reg}_{\mathbf{C}}
can be identified with the right-hand side of (
1.1.2
) and the class module is trivial (see also Example
4.1.21
and §
5.2
). Thus Demeslay’s identity bridges Taelman special
L
-value identities and the special value formulas of Pellarin.

Using this story about the Carlitz module as a guide, the goal of the present paper has been to address three questions that arise naturally in the context of Drinfeld modules of arbitrary rank defined over global function fields, especially defined over
A
itself.

•

What is a reasonable definition of a Pellarin
L
-series attached to a Drinfeld module over
A
?

•

To what extent can the twist
\mathbf{C}
of the Carlitz module by
\omega_{z}
be generalized in the context of Drinfeld modules of higher rank?

•

What does Demeslay’s class module formula reveal about special values of their
L
-functions?

Answers to these questions in the case that a Drinfeld module is conjugated by
\omega_{z}
have been obtained by Anglès and Tavares Ribeiro
[
13
]
and Gezmiş
[
35
]
(see Example
5.4.4
). Their work indicates that rather than this being a circumstance tied to a single Drinfeld module, that one could consider interactions between two Drinfeld modules.

Thus to answer these questions in full, for two Drinfeld modules
\phi
and
\psi
defined over
A
, we define a
t
-module
\mathbb{E}(\phi\times\psi)
that is the twist of
\phi
by the rigid anlyatic trivialization of
\psi
. Then its associated
L
-function includes a Rankin-Selberg type convolution of a Goss
L
-series and a Pellarin
L
-series (see Theorem
C
) and can be evaluated using Demeslay’s identity (see Theorem
A
and Corollaries
D
and
E
). We now summarize these results.

1.2.
Rigid analytic twists

Let
\mathsf{A}=\mathbb{F}_{q}[t]
, and let
\phi
,
\psi:\mathsf{A}\to A[\tau]
be Drinfeld modules defined over
A
by

(1.2.1)

\phi_{t}=\theta+\kappa_{1}\tau+\cdots+\kappa_{r}\tau^{r},\quad\psi_{t}=\theta+\eta_{1}\tau+\cdots+\eta_{\ell}\tau^{\ell},\quad\kappa_{r},\eta_{\ell}\in\mathbb{F}_{q}^{\times}.

Thus
\phi
has rank
r
and
\psi
has rank
\ell
, and moreover because their leading coefficients are in
\mathbb{F}_{q}^{\times}
, both
\phi
and
\psi
have everywhere good reduction. Using the theory of Anderson generating functions, we define a rigid analytic trivialization
\Upsilon_{\psi,z}\in\GL_{\ell}(\mathbb{T}_{z})
for
\psi
(see (
2.4.14
)) such that if

(1.2.2)

\Theta_{\psi,z}=\begin{pmatrix}0&\cdots&0&(z-\theta)/\eta_{\ell}\\
1&\cdots&0&-\eta_{1}/\eta_{\ell}\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots\\
0&\cdots&1&-\eta_{\ell-1}/\eta_{\ell}\end{pmatrix}\quad\in\Mat_{\ell}(\mathbb{A}),

then

\Upsilon_{\psi,z}^{(1)}=\Upsilon_{\psi,z}\Theta_{\psi,z},

where
\Upsilon_{\psi,z}^{(1)}
denotes the Frobenius twists of the entries of
\Upsilon_{\psi,z}
(see §
2.1.4
). We then define
\mathbb{E}=\mathbb{E}(\phi\times\psi):\mathbf{A}\to\Mat_{\ell}(\mathbb{A}[\tau])
to be the Anderson
t
-module determined by

(1.2.3)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{t}

\displaystyle=\Upsilon_{\psi,z}^{-1}\cdot\phi_{t}^{\oplus\ell}\cdot\Upsilon_{\psi,z}

\displaystyle=\theta\mathrm{I}_{\ell}+\kappa_{1}\Theta_{\psi,z}\tau+\kappa_{2}\Theta_{\psi,z}\Theta_{\psi,z}^{(1)}\tau^{2}+\cdots+\kappa_{r}\Theta_{\psi,z}\Theta_{\psi,z}^{(1)}\cdots\Theta_{\psi,z}^{(r-1)}\tau^{r},

where
\mathrm{I}_{\ell}
is the
\ell\times\ell
identity matrix.

Thus
\mathbb{E}(\phi\times\psi)
is the conjugation of the direct sum
\phi^{\oplus\ell}
by the rigid analytic trivialization
\Upsilon_{\psi,z}
. It induces an
\mathbf{A}
-module structure on
\widehat{\mathbb{L}}_{z}^{\ell}
, and as such it is a
t
-module over
\widehat{\mathbb{L}}_{z}
. It was shown in
[
33
]
that the induced exponential function
\Exp_{\phi}:\widehat{\mathbb{L}}_{z}\to\widehat{\mathbb{L}}_{z}
over
\widehat{\mathbb{L}}_{z}
is surjective, and it follows that
\Exp_{\mathbb{E}}(\mathbf{z}):\widehat{\mathbb{L}}_{z}^{\ell}\to\widehat{\mathbb{L}}_{z}^{\ell}
is also surjective. Moreover, if we let
\Lambda_{\phi}=\ker\Exp_{\phi}
and
\Lambda_{\mathbb{E}}=\ker\Exp_{\mathbb{E}}
, then

\Lambda_{\mathbb{E}}=\Upsilon_{\psi,z}^{-1}\,\Lambda_{\phi}^{\oplus\ell},

and
\Lambda_{\mathbb{E}}
is a free
\mathbb{A}
-module of rank
r\ell
. Furthermore, for
\nu\in\mathsf{A}
,
\nu\neq 0
, we find that the
\nu
-torsion submodule
\mathbb{E}[\nu]=\{\mathbf{x}\in\widehat{\mathbb{L}}_{z}^{\ell}\mid\mathbb{E}_{\nu}(\mathbf{x})=0\}
yields an isomorphism of
\mathbf{A}
-modules,
\mathbb{E}[\nu]\cong(\mathbf{A}/\nu\mathbf{A})^{r\ell}
. To that end if
\lambda\in\mathsf{A}_{+}
is irreducible, then we have a Tate module

T_{\lambda}(\mathbb{E})=\varprojlim\mathbb{E}[\lambda^{m}]\cong\mathbf{A}_{\lambda}^{r\ell},

where
\mathbf{A}_{\lambda}
is the
\lambda
-adic completion of
\mathbf{A}
. See §
4.1
for more details.

Demeslay’s class module formula (Theorem
5.3.10
) applies generally to Anderson
t
-modules over Tate algebras, and in particular to
\mathbb{E}
. In this case it states

(1.2.4)

\prod_{f}\frac{{[\Lie(\mkern 1.0mu\overline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathbb{E}})(\mathbb{F}_{f}(z))]}_{\mathbb{A}}}{{[\mkern 1.0mu\overline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathbb{E}}(\mathbb{F}_{f}(z))]}_{\mathbb{A}}}=\mathrm{Reg}_{\mathbb{E}}\cdot{\left[\mathrm{H}(\mathbb{E})\right]}_{\mathbb{A}}\quad\in\mathbb{K}_{\infty},

where as in (
1.1.5
) the product is taken over all irreducible
f\in A_{+}
. Here
\overline{\mathbb{E}}
denotes the reduction of
\mathbb{E}
modulo
f
,
\mathrm{Reg}_{\mathbb{E}}
is the regulator of
\mathbb{E}
, and
\mathrm{H}(\mathbb{E})
is its class module. Our initial task is to determine the factors in this product for each
f
, and then we associate it to the special value of an
L
-function.

1.3.
Characteristic polynomials of Frobenius

For our Drinfeld module
\phi
in (
1.2.1
), if we fix
f\in A_{+}
irreducible of degree
d
and let
\lambda\in\mathsf{A}_{+}
be irreducible with
\lambda(\theta)\neq f
, then by work of Gekeler, Hsia, Takahashi, and Yu
[
31
,
48
,
69
]
, the characteristic polynomial
P_{\phi,f}(X)=\Char(\tau^{d},T_{\lambda}(\mkern 2.5mu\overline{\mkern-2.5mu\phi}),X)=X^{r}+c_{r-1}X^{r-1}+\cdots+c_{0}\in A[X]
of
\tau^{d}
acting on
T_{\lambda}(\mkern 2.5mu\overline{\mkern-2.5mu\phi})
satisfies
c_{0}=(-1)^{r}\overline{\chi}_{\phi}(f)f
, where
\chi_{\phi}(a)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=((-1)^{r+1}\kappa_{r})^{\deg a}
and
\overline{\chi}_{\phi}=\chi_{\phi}^{-1}
, and moreover,

(1.3.1)

{\left[\mkern 2.5mu\overline{\mkern-2.5mu\phi}(\mathbb{F}_{f})\right]}_{A}=(-1)^{r}\chi_{\phi}(f)\cdot P_{\phi,f}(1).

See §
2.3
for more details.
Our first result is to establish an identity corresponding to (
1.3.1
) for
{[\mkern 1.0mu\overline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathbb{E}}(\mathbb{F}_{f}(z))]}_{\mathbb{A}}
. Letting
P_{\psi,f}^{\vee}(X)=\Char(\tau^{d},T_{\lambda}(\mkern 2.5mu\overline{\mkern-2.5mu\psi})^{\vee},X)\in K[X]
, where
T_{\lambda}(\mkern 2.5mu\overline{\mkern-2.5mu\psi})^{\vee}
is the dual of
T_{\lambda}(\mkern 2.5mu\overline{\mkern-2.5mu\psi})
, we let
P_{\psi,f(z)}^{\vee}(X)\in\mathbb{F}_{q}(z)[X]
denote
P_{\psi,f}^{\vee}(X)|_{\theta=z}
. We then define

(1.3.2)

\mathbf{P}_{f}(X)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\bigl(P_{\phi,f}\otimes P_{\psi,f(z)}^{\vee}\bigr)(X)\quad\in\mathbb{A}[X],

where if
P(X)=(X-\alpha_{1})\cdots(X-\alpha_{r})
and
Q(X)=(X-\beta_{1})\cdots(X-\beta_{\ell})
, then
(P\otimes Q)(X)=\prod_{i,j}(X-\alpha_{i}\beta_{j})
. The reason to study
\mathbf{P}_{f}(X)
is that if
\alpha_{f}\in\Gal(K^{\mathrm{sep}}/K)
is a Frobenius element for
f
, then (see Proposition
4.1.17
)

(1.3.3)

\mathbf{P}_{f}(X)=\Char(\alpha_{f},T_{\lambda}(\mathbb{E}(\phi\times\psi)),X).

This is a key ingredient for the following result (stated later as Theorem
4.2.2
).

Theorem A
.

Let
f\in\mathsf{A}_{+}
be irreducible. Then

{\left[\mkern 1.0mu\overline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathbb{E}}(\mathbb{F}_{f}(z))\right]}_{\mathbb{A}}=(-1)^{r\ell}\chi_{\phi}(f)^{\ell}\,\overline{\chi}_{\psi}(f)^{r}\cdot\mathbf{P}_{f}(1).

Unfortunately the techniques of
[
8
,
13
,
24
,
35
]
used to evaluate such
\mathbb{A}
-orders in the case where one twists by the Carlitz module (i.e.,
\psi=\mathsf{C}
) relied on the fact that
\mathsf{C}
is rank
1
. In order to account for
\psi
having higher rank, we develop general results about Anderson
t
-modules in finite characteristic that we apply to
\mathbb{E}
and prove Theorem
A
.

The main line of our argument is to adapt constructions of Poonen
[
63
]
of Galois equivariant and non-degenerate pairings on torsion submodules of Drinfeld modules and their adjoints over finite fields to higher dimensions and to more general fields. This takes up the bulk of §
3
. What we produce is a bit more general than what we require, though we anticipate it will be useful for future work (e.g., see Corollary
3.7.8
).

For
n\geqslant 0
, let
Z=\{z_{1},\dots,z_{n}\}
denote a set of variables (if
n=0
, then
Z=\emptyset
), and let
{\mathbb{F}_{q}\langle Z\rangle}
denote either
\mathbb{F}_{q}(Z)
or
{\mathbb{F}_{q}(\!(Z)\!)}
. We let
\mathcal{A}={\mathbb{F}_{q}\langle Z\rangle}[t]
. For fixed
f\in A_{+}
irreducible of degree
d
, we have a structure map
\iota:\mathcal{A}\to{\mathbb{F}_{f}\langle Z\rangle}
that extends the map
\mathsf{A}\to\mathbb{F}_{f}
sending
t
to a root of
f
. We consider an Anderson
t
-module
\mathcal{E}:\mathcal{A}\to\Mat_{\ell}({\mathbb{F}_{f}\langle Z\rangle}[\tau])
over
{\mathbb{F}_{f}\langle Z\rangle}
together with its adjoint
\mathcal{E}^{*}:\mathcal{A}\to\Mat_{\ell}({\mathbb{F}_{f}\langle Z\rangle}[\sigma])
(see §
3.1
), and for
\nu\in\mathcal{A}
, following Poonen we define an
{\mathbb{F}_{q}\langle Z\rangle}
-bilinear pairing (see Proposition
3.6.5
)

\langle\cdot\mathbin{,}\cdot\rangle_{\nu}:\mathcal{E}[\nu]\times\mathcal{E}^{*}[\nu]\to{\mathbb{F}_{q}\langle Z\rangle}

that is
\Gal(\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{f}/\mathbb{F}_{f})
-equivariant. If the torsion modules
\mathcal{E}[\nu]
and
\mathcal{E}^{*}[\nu]
have maximal dimension over
{\mathbb{F}_{q}\langle Z\rangle}
, then the pairing is non-degenerate.

For
\lambda\in\mathsf{A}_{+}
irreducible with
\lambda(\theta)\neq f
, we obtain a pairing (see Theorem
3.6.19
)

[\cdot\mathbin{,}\cdot]_{\lambda}:T_{\lambda}(\mathcal{E})\times T_{\lambda}(\mathcal{E}^{*})\to\mathcal{A}_{\lambda}

that is
\mathcal{A}_{\lambda}
-bilinear and
\Gal(\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{f}/\mathbb{F}_{f})
-equivariant. Moreover, under certain conditions (see Definition
3.6.17
), which are satisfied in the case
\mathcal{E}=\mathbb{E}(\phi\times\psi)
, the pairing
[\cdot\mathbin{,}\cdot]_{\lambda}
is non-degenerate. The main result in this part of the paper (Theorem
3.7.4
) is the following.

Theorem B
.

Let
\mathcal{E}:\mathcal{A}\to\Mat_{\ell}({\mathbb{F}_{f}\langle Z\rangle}[\tau])
be a
t
-module defined over
{\mathbb{F}_{f}\langle Z\rangle}
. Let
\lambda\in\mathsf{A}_{+}
be irreducible with
\lambda(\theta)\neq f
such that Definition
3.6.17
(a)–(c) are satisfied. Then

\bigl[\mathcal{E}({\mathbb{F}_{f}\langle Z\rangle})\bigr]_{\mathcal{A}}=\gamma\cdot\Char(\tau^{d},T_{\lambda}(\mathcal{E}),1),

where
\gamma\in{\mathbb{F}_{q}\langle Z\rangle}^{\times}
uniquely forces the right-hand expression to be monic in
t
.

When
f\neq\theta
, Theorem
A
follows from Theorem
B
by using (
1.3.3
). It is here that we need to work over
{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{f}(\!(z)\!)}
as well as
\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{f}(z)
, since the entries of
\Upsilon_{\psi,z}
reduce to elements of
{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{f}(\!(z)\!)}
modulo primes different from
\theta
. For
f=\theta
we employ a separate direct argument.

1.4.
Convolution
L
-functions and special values

For the Drinfeld module
\phi
from (
1.2.1
), Goss defined two
L
-functions,

L(\phi^{\vee},s)=\prod_{f}Q_{\phi,f}^{\vee}\bigl(f^{-s}\bigr)^{-1},\quad L(\phi,s)=\prod_{f}Q_{\phi,f}\bigl(f^{-s}\bigr)^{-1},

where
Q_{\phi,f}(X)
and
Q_{\phi,f}^{\vee}(X)
are the reciprocal polynomials of
P_{\phi,f}(X)
and
P_{\phi,f}^{\vee}(X)
respectively. These give rise to multiplicative functions
\mu_{\phi}
,
\nu_{\phi}:A_{+}\to A
such that

\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\mu_{\phi}(f^{m})X^{m}=Q_{f}^{\vee}(fX)^{-1},\quad\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\nu_{\phi}(f^{m})X^{m}=Q_{f}(X)^{-1}.

We similarly define
\mu_{\psi}
and
\nu_{\psi}
. See §
2.3
for details. In a similar fashion we define

L(\mathbb{E}^{\vee},s)=L(\mathbb{E}(\phi\times\psi)^{\vee},s)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\prod_{f}\mathbf{Q}_{f}^{\vee}\bigl(f^{-s}\bigr)^{-1},\quad s\geqslant 0,

where
\mathbf{Q}_{f}^{\vee}(X)
is the reciprocal polynomial of
\mathbf{P}_{f}^{\vee}(X)=(P_{\phi,f}^{\vee}\otimes P_{\psi,f(z)})(X)\in K[z][X]
. This product for
L(\mathbb{E}^{\vee},s)
converges in
\mathbb{T}_{z}(K_{\infty})
for integers
s\geqslant 0
. Moreover, it follows from Theorem
A
that (see Proposition
5.4.3
)

(1.4.1)

L(\mathbb{E}^{\vee},0)=\prod_{f}\frac{{\left[\mathbb{F}_{f}(z)^{\ell}\right]}_{\mathbb{A}}}{{\left[\mkern 1.0mu\overline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathbb{E}}(\mathbb{F}_{f}(z))\right]}_{\mathbb{A}}},

and thus Demeslay’s identity (
1.2.4
) implies that
L(\mathbb{E}^{\vee},0)=\mathrm{Reg}_{\mathbb{E}}\cdot\mathrm{H}(\mathbb{E})
.

On the other hand, we can express
L(\mathbb{E}^{\vee},s)
in an alternative form as the convolution of
L
-series for
\phi
and
\psi
, following the situation for Maass forms on
\GL_{n}
(see
[
16
,
38
]
). For fixed
f\in A_{+}
irreducible, using Cauchy’s identity (see Theorem
2.5.13
) we can write
\mathbf{Q}_{f}^{\vee}(X)^{-1}
in terms of Schur polynomials evaluated at the roots of
P_{\phi,f}^{\vee}(X)
and
P_{\psi,f(z)}(X)
. In particular if
\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{r}\in\mkern 2.5mu\overline{\mkern-2.5muK}
are the roots of
P_{\phi,f}^{\vee}(X)
, then for
k_{1},\dots,k_{r}\geqslant 0
, we define

(1.4.2)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\phi,\theta}\bigl(f^{k_{1}},\dots,f^{k_{r-1}}\bigr)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=S_{k_{1},\dots,k_{r-1}}(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{r})\cdot f^{k_{1}+\cdots+k_{r-1}}

(1.4.3)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\phi,\theta}\bigl(f^{k_{1}},\dots,f^{k_{r-1}}\bigr)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=S_{k_{1},\dots,k_{r-1}}\bigl(\alpha_{1}^{-1},\dots,\alpha_{r}^{-1}\bigr),

where
S_{k_{1},\dots,k_{r-1}}(x_{1},\dots,x_{r})
is the Schur polynomial defined in (
2.5.7
). We can extend
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\phi,\theta}
and
\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\phi,\theta}
to functions on
(A_{+})^{r-1}
multiplicatively, and then we find that for
a_{1},\dots,a_{r-1}\in A_{+}
, we have
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\phi,\theta}(a_{1},\dots,a_{r-1})\in A
and
\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\phi,\theta}(a_{1},\dots,a_{r-1})\in A
, and

\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\phi,\theta}(a_{1},\dots,a_{r-1})=\chi_{\phi}(a_{1}\cdots a_{r-1})\cdot\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\phi,\theta}(a_{r-1},\dots,a_{1}).

Furthermore, for any
a\in A_{+}
,

\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\phi,\theta}(a,1,\dots,1)=\mu_{\phi}(a),\quad\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\phi,\theta}(a,1,\dots,1)=\nu_{\phi}(a).

The functions
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\phi,\theta}
,
\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\phi,\theta}:(A_{+})^{r-1}\to A
satisfy various relations induced by relations on Schur polynomials. See §
6.1
for details.

Returning to the situation of Drinfeld modules
\phi
and
\psi
, we set
\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\psi,z}(a_{1},\dots,a_{r})=\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\psi,\theta}(a_{1},\dots,a_{r})|_{\theta=z}\in\mathbb{F}_{q}[z]
. When
r
,
\ell\geqslant 2
, we define an
L
-function
L(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\phi,\theta}\times\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\psi,z},s)
as follows. If
r=\ell
, then

(1.4.4)

L(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\phi,\theta}\times\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\psi,z},s)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\sum_{a_{1},\dots,a_{r-1}\in A_{+}}\frac{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\phi,\theta}(a_{1},\dots,a_{r-1})\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\psi,z}(a_{1},\dots,a_{r-1})}{a_{1}\cdots a_{r-1}(a_{1}a_{2}^{2}\cdots a_{r-1}^{r-1})^{s}}.

If
r<\ell
, then

(1.4.5)

L(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\phi,\theta}\times\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\psi,z},s)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\sum_{a_{1},\ldots,a_{r}\in A_{+}}\frac{\chi_{\phi}(a_{r})\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\phi,\theta}(a_{1},\dots,a_{r-1})\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\psi,z}(a_{1},\dots,a_{r},1,\ldots,1)}{a_{1}\cdots a_{r}(a_{1}a_{2}^{2}\cdots a_{r}^{r})^{s}},

and if
r>\ell
, then

(1.4.6)

L(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\phi,\theta}\times\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\psi,z},s)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\\
\sum_{a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\in A_{+}}\frac{\overline{\chi}_{\psi}(a_{\ell})a_{\ell}(z)\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\phi,\theta}(a_{1},\dots,a_{\ell},1,\dots,1)\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\psi,z}(a_{1},\dots,a_{\ell-1})}{a_{1}\cdots a_{\ell}(a_{1}a_{2}^{2}\cdots a_{\ell}^{\ell})^{s}}.

The different versions are governed by the different applications of Cauchy’s identity (Theorem
2.5.13
, Corollary
2.5.14
) that are needed. See §
6.2
–
6.3
for more details.

As
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\phi,\theta}(a_{1},\dots,a_{r-1})\in A
and
\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\psi,z}(a_{1},\dots,a_{r-1})\in\mathbb{F}_{q}[z]
, we interpret
L(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\phi,\theta}\times\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\psi,z},s)
as a convolution of Goss and Pellarin
L
-series. Now
L(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\phi,\theta}\times\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\psi,z},s)
is related to
L(\mathbb{E}(\phi\times\psi)^{\vee},s)
by the following result (stated later as Theorems
6.2.3
and
6.3.5
), where we let
L(\mathbb{A},\chi_{\phi}\overline{\chi}_{\psi},s)=\sum_{a\in A_{+}}\chi_{\phi}(a)\overline{\chi}_{\psi}(a)a(z)\cdot a^{-s}
be a twist of
L(\mathbb{A},s)
.

Theorem C
.

Let
\phi
,
\psi:\mathsf{A}\to A[\tau]
be Drinfeld modules of ranks
r
and
\ell
respectively with everywhere good reduction as defined in (
1.2.1
). Assume that
r
,
\ell\geqslant 2
.

(a)

If
r=\ell
, then

L(\mathbb{E}(\phi\times\psi)^{\vee},s)=L(\mathbb{A},\chi_{\phi}\overline{\chi}_{\psi},rs+1)\cdot L(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\phi,\theta}\times\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\psi,z},s).

(b)

If
r\neq\ell
, then

L(\mathbb{E}(\phi\times\psi)^{\vee},s)=L(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\phi,\theta}\times\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\psi,z},s).

Taking
s=0
in Theorem
C
provides special value identities for
L(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\phi,\theta}\times\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\psi,z},0)
.
If
r=\ell
and
\kappa_{r}=\eta_{r}
, then
L(\mathbb{A},\chi_{\phi}\overline{\chi}_{\psi},s)=L(\mathbb{A},s)
, and (
1.1.2
) and (
1.2.4
) imply the following corollary (stated as Corollary
6.2.4
). Under the condition that
\lVert\Upsilon_{\psi,z}\rVert
is less than the radius of convergence of
\Log_{\phi}(\mathbf{z})
, part (b) emerges because we can evaluate
\mathrm{Reg}_{\mathbb{E}}
exactly and
\mathrm{H}(\mathbb{E})
is trivial. For the case where
\kappa_{r}\neq\eta_{r}
, see Corollary
6.2.4
(c).

Corollary D
.

Let
\phi
,
\psi:\mathsf{A}\to A[\tau]
be Drinfeld modules both of rank
r\geqslant 2
with everywhere good reduction, as defined in (
1.2.1
).

(a)

Then

\displaystyle L(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\phi,\theta}\times\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\psi,z},0)

\displaystyle=\sum_{a_{1}\in A_{+}}\cdots\sum_{a_{r-1}\in A_{+}}\frac{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\phi,\theta}(a_{1},\dots,a_{r-1})\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\psi,z}(a_{1},\dots,a_{r-1})}{a_{1}\cdots a_{r-1}}

\displaystyle=(\theta-z)\cdot\frac{\omega_{z}}{\widetilde{\pi}}\cdot\mathrm{Reg}_{\mathbb{E}}\cdot{[\mathrm{H}(\mathbb{E})]}_{\mathbb{A}}.

(b)

If
\lVert\Upsilon_{\psi,z}\rVert<R_{\phi}
, where
R_{\phi}
is the radius of convergence of
\Log_{\phi}(\mathbf{z})
in (
2.2.5
), then

L(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\phi,\theta}\times\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\psi,z},0)=(\theta-z)\cdot\frac{\omega_{z}}{\widetilde{\pi}}\cdot\det\Bigl(\Upsilon_{\psi,z}^{-1}\Log_{\phi}\bigl(\Upsilon_{\psi,z}\bigr)\Bigr).

In the case that
r\neq\ell
, we obtain the following (stated later as Corollay
6.3.6
).

Corollary E
.

Let
\phi
,
\psi:\mathsf{A}\to A[\tau]
be Drinfeld modules of ranks
r
and
\ell
respectively with everywhere good reduction, as defined in (
1.2.1
). Assume that
r
,
\ell\geqslant 2
and that
r\neq\ell
.

(a)

If
r<\ell
, then

\displaystyle L(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\phi,\theta}\times\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\psi,z},0)

\displaystyle=\sum_{a_{1},\ldots,a_{r}\in A_{+}}\frac{\chi_{\phi}(a_{r})\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\phi,\theta}(a_{1},\dots,a_{r-1})\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\psi,z}(a_{1},\dots,a_{r},1,\dots,1)}{a_{1}\cdots a_{r}}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Reg}_{\mathbb{E}}\cdot{[\mathrm{H}(\mathbb{E})]}_{\mathbb{A}}.

(b)

If
r>\ell
, then

\displaystyle L(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\phi,\theta}\times\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\psi,z},0)

\displaystyle=\sum_{a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\in A_{+}}\frac{\overline{\chi}_{\psi}(a_{\ell})a_{\ell}(z)\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\phi,\theta}(a_{1},\dots,a_{\ell},1,\ldots,1)\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\psi,z}(a_{1},\dots,a_{\ell-1})}{a_{1}\cdots a_{\ell}}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Reg}_{\mathbb{E}}\cdot{[\mathrm{H}(\mathbb{E})]}_{\mathbb{A}}.

(c)

If
\lVert\Upsilon_{\psi,z}\rVert<R_{\phi}
, where
R_{\phi}
is the radius of convergence of
\Log_{\phi}(\mathbf{z})
in (
2.2.5
), then

L(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\phi,\theta}\times\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\psi,z},0)=\det\Bigl(\Upsilon_{\psi,z}^{-1}\Log_{\phi}\bigl(\Upsilon_{\psi,z}\bigr)\Bigr).

Similar results hold when
r=1
or
\ell=1
. The cases where
\phi
or
\psi
are the Carlitz module are worked out in Examples
4.1.21
,
5.4.4
, and
5.4.7
, and we observe that Corollaries
D
and
E
degenerate to these cases. Example
4.1.21
is Pellarin’s original case in
[
61
]
, and Example
5.4.4
was worked out by Anglès, Gezmiş, and Tavares Ribeiro
[
13
,
35
]
.

Remark 1.4.7
.

Throughout we have restricted our attention to cases of everywhere good reduction. This was partly to provide a more simplified and unified treatment of what was to the authors quite complicated to parcel out among various constituent identities. However, (
1.2.2
) and (
1.2.3
) indicate that there are delicate issues to resolve in determining appropriate Euler factors at bad primes (especially primes dividing
\eta_{\ell}
).

Remark 1.4.8
.

In many works on Pellarin
L
-series (e.g.,
[
5
,
10
,
8
,
13
,
24
,
35
,
58
]
), one considers
L
-functions in several
z
-variables, say
z_{1},\dots z_{n}
. The present techniques should apply, but it would entail several tensor products of polynomials as in (
1.3.2
) and applications of Cauchy’s identity (Theorem
2.5.13
), which were beyond the scope of this paper.

One might wonder if the present framework can be adapted to convolutions of Goss
L
-functions with values in
\mathbb{C}_{\infty}
, say for tensor products of Drinfeld modules defined over
A
, and whether their special values can be related to class module formulas for
t
-modules from
[
28
,
6
,
7
]
. This will be the subject of forthcoming work of the first author.

1.4.9.
Outline

After summarizing preliminary material in §
2
, we consider Anderson
t
-modules over Tate algebras and over “
\tau
-perfect” fields of finite characteristic in §
3
. Especially in §
3
we extend Poonen pairings to
t
-modules over more general fields of finite characteristic so as to find identities for
\mathbb{A}
-orders of
t
-modules in terms of characteristic polynomials of Frobenius. In §
4
we introduce the rigid analytic twists
\mathbb{E}(\phi\times\psi)
associated to two Drinfeld modules over
A
, as well as determine identities for their
\mathbb{A}
-orders over fields of finite characteristic. In §
5
we review the theories of Goss and Pellarin
L
-series, as well as Demeslay’s class module formula. We introduce the
L
-function of
E(\phi\times\psi)
and demonstrate how we can apply Demeslay’s formula to it. As a stepping stone to the next section we consider the cases where one of
\phi
or
\psi
is the Carlitz module. Finally, in §
6
we introduce the convolution
L
-series
L(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\phi,\theta}\times\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\psi,z},s)
, relate it to
L(\mathbb{E}(\phi\times\psi)^{\vee},s)
and Pellarin’s
L
-series, and investigate special value identities. We analyze a few examples, including one where
\lVert\Upsilon_{\psi,z}\rVert\geqslant R_{\phi}
, at the end of §
6
.

2.
Preliminaries

2.1.
Notation

We will use the following notation throughout.

A

=

\mathbb{F}_{q}[\theta]
, polynomial ring in variable
\theta
over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
.

A_{+}

=

the monic elements of
A
.

K

=

\mathbb{F}_{q}(\theta)
, the fraction field of
A
.

K_{\infty}

=

{\mathbb{F}_{q}(\!(\theta^{-1})\!)}
, the completion of
K
at
\infty
.

\mathbb{C}_{\infty}

=

the completion of an algebraic closure
\mkern 2.5mu\overline{\mkern-2.5muK}_{\infty}
of
K_{\infty}
.

{\lvert\,\cdot\,\rvert}_{\infty}
;
\deg

=

\infty
-adic norm on
\mathbb{C}_{\infty}
, extended to the sup norm on a finite dimensional
\mathbb{C}_{\infty}
-vector space;
\deg=-\ord_{\infty}=\log_{q}{\lvert\,\cdot\,\rvert}_{\infty}
.

\mathbb{F}_{f}

=

A/fA
for
f\in A_{+}
irreducible.

\mathsf{A}

=

\mathbb{F}_{q}[t]
, for a variable
t
independent from
\theta
.

\mathbb{T}_{t}
;
\mathbb{L}_{t}

=

Tate algebra in
t

=\{\sum a_{i}t^{i}\in{\mathbb{C}_{\infty}[\![t]\!]}\mid{\lvert a_{i}\rvert}_{\infty}\to 0\}=
completion of
\mathbb{C}_{\infty}[t]
with respect to Gauss norm; and
\mathbb{L}_{t}
its fraction field.

\widehat{\mathbb{L}}_{t}

=

completion of
\mathbb{L}_{t}
with respect to Gauss norm.

\mathbb{T}_{t}(K_{\infty})
;
\mathbb{L}_{t}(K_{\infty})

=

\mathbb{T}_{t}\cap{K_{\infty}[\![t]\!]}={\mathbb{F}_{q}[t](\!(\theta^{-1})\!)}
; and
\mathbb{L}_{t}(K_{\infty})
its fraction field.

\widehat{\mathbb{L}}_{t}(K_{\infty})

=

completion of
\mathbb{L}_{t}(K_{\infty})
, or equivalently
{\mathbb{F}_{q}(t)(\!(\theta^{-1})\!)}
.

\mathbb{A}

=

\mathbb{F}_{q}(z)[\theta]
, for a third variable
z
independent from
\theta
,
t
.

\mathbb{K}

=

\mathbb{F}_{q}(z,\theta)
, the fraction field of
\mathbb{A}
.

\mathbb{K}_{\infty}

=

{\mathbb{F}_{q}(z)(\!(\theta^{-1})\!)}
, the completion of
\mathbb{K}
at
\infty
, or equivalently
\widehat{\mathbb{L}}_{z}(K_{\infty})
.

\mathbb{T}_{z}
,
\mathbb{T}_{z}(K_{\infty})
,
\widehat{\mathbb{L}}_{z}
,
\widehat{\mathbb{L}}_{z}(K_{\infty})

=

same as above, with
t
replaced by
z
.

\mathbf{A}

=

\mathbb{F}_{q}(z)[t]
.

{F\langle Z\rangle}

=

F(Z)
or
{F(\!(Z)\!)}
, where
Z=\{z_{1},\dots,z_{n}\}
,
n\geqslant 0
.

\mathcal{A}

=

{\mathbb{F}_{q}\langle Z\rangle}[t]
, for
Z=\{z_{1},\dots,z_{n}\}
,
n\geqslant 0
.

\Mat_{k\times\ell}(R)

=

for a ring
R
, the left
R
-module of
k\times\ell
matrices over
R
.

\Mat_{k}(R)
;
R^{k}

=

\Mat_{k\times k}(R)
;
\Mat_{k\times 1}(R)
.

B^{{\mathsf{T}}}

=

the transpose of a matrix
B
.

\Char(B,X)

=

the monic characteristic polynomial in
X
of a square matrix
B
.

\Char(\alpha,V,X)

=

the monic characteristic polynomial of a linear map
\alpha:V\to V
.

2.1.1.
Rings of scalars, functions, and operators

For a variable
t
independent from
\theta
we let
\mathsf{A}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathbb{F}_{q}[t]
. We let
\mathbb{T}_{t}
denote the standard
Tate algebra
,
\mathbb{T}_{t}\subseteq{\mathbb{C}_{\infty}[\![t]\!]}
,
consisting of power series that converge on the closed unit disk of
\mathbb{C}_{\infty}
, and we take

(2.1.2)

\mathbb{T}_{t}(K_{\infty})\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathbb{T}_{t}\cap{K_{\infty}[\![t]\!]}={\mathbb{F}_{q}[t](\!(\theta^{-1})\!)}.

We let
\lVert\,\cdot\,\rVert
denote the Gauss norm on
\mathbb{T}_{t}
, such that
\lVert\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}a_{i}t^{i}\rVert=\max_{i}\{{\lvert a_{i}\rvert}_{\infty}\}
,
under which
\mathbb{T}_{t}
is a complete normed
\mathbb{C}_{\infty}
-vector space, and likewise
\mathbb{T}_{t}(K_{\infty})
is a complete normed
K_{\infty}
-vector space. We extend the degree map on
\mathbb{C}_{\infty}
to
\mathbb{T}_{t}
by taking

\deg=\log_{q}\lVert\,\cdot\,\rVert
. We let
\mathbb{L}_{t}
(respectively
\mathbb{L}_{t}(K_{\infty})
) denote the fraction field of
\mathbb{T}_{t}
(respectively
\mathbb{T}_{t}(K_{\infty})
), to which we extend the Gauss norm, and we take
\widehat{\mathbb{L}}_{t}
(respectively
\widehat{\mathbb{L}}_{t}(K_{\infty})
) for its completion.
We note the following properties. With respect to
\lVert\,\cdot\,\rVert
,

•

\mathbb{T}_{t}
is the completion of
\mathbb{C}_{\infty}[t]
and
\mathbb{T}_{t}(K_{\infty})
is the completion of
K_{\infty}[t]
,

•

and
\widehat{\mathbb{L}}_{t}
is the completion of
\mathbb{C}_{\infty}(t)
and
\widehat{\mathbb{L}}_{t}(K_{\infty})
is the completion of
K_{\infty}(t)
.

•

Also,
\widehat{\mathbb{L}}_{t}(K_{\infty})={\mathbb{F}_{q}(t)(\!(\theta^{-1})\!)}
.

We make any finite dimensional
\widehat{\mathbb{L}}_{t}
-vector space a complete normed vector space by taking the sup norm. For more information on Tate algebras the reader is directed to
[
30
]
.

Having defined several rings so far in terms of the variable
t
, we make also identical copies,
\mathbb{T}_{z}
,
\mathbb{T}_{z}(K_{\infty})
, etc., with a new variable
z
. We fix also

\mathbb{A}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathbb{F}_{q}(z)[\theta],\quad\mathbb{K}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathbb{F}_{q}(z,\theta),\quad\mathbb{K}_{\infty}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}={\mathbb{F}_{q}(z)(\!(\theta^{-1})\!)}=\widehat{\mathbb{L}}_{z}(K_{\infty}).

The Gauss norm and degree on these new rings are also denoted
\lVert\,\cdot\,\rVert
and
\deg
.

We finally let
\mathbf{A}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathbb{F}_{q}(z)[t]
. The reader has undoubtedly noticed that we now have four similar polynomial rings to keep track of,
A\cong\mathsf{A}
and
\mathbb{A}\cong\mathbf{A}
, but each will have its own use in due course. A fifth ring “
\mathcal{A}
” we also appear in §
3
.

Remark 2.1.3
.

From the point of view of the present paper, the rings in terms of
t
are rings of functions and operators, whereas the rings in terms of
z
are rings of scalars. As such the rings (
A
,
\mathbb{A}
) are scalars and (
\mathsf{A}
,
\mathbf{A}
) are operators. We note that the roles of
t
and
z
here would be
\theta
and
t_{1},\dots,t_{s}
in
[
8
,
24
,
25
]
, and
z
and
t_{1},\dots,t_{s}
in
[
33
]
.

2.1.4.
Frobenius operators

We take
\tau:\mathbb{C}_{\infty}\to\mathbb{C}_{\infty}
for the
q
-th power Frobenius automorphism
, which we extend to
{\mathbb{C}_{\infty}(\!(t)\!)}
and
{\mathbb{C}_{\infty}(\!(z)\!)}
(and their compositum) by requiring it to commute with
t
and
z
. For
g=\sum c_{i}t^{i}\in{\mathbb{C}_{\infty}(\!(t)\!)}
, we define the
n
-th Frobenius twist
,

g^{(n)}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\tau^{n}(g)=\sum c_{i}^{q^{n}}t^{i},\quad\forall\,n\in\mathbb{Z},

and likewise for
g\in{\mathbb{C}_{\infty}(\!(z)\!)}
. Then
\tau
induces
\mathbb{F}_{q}(t)
-linear automorphisms of
\mathbb{T}_{t}
,
\mathbb{L}_{t}
,
\widehat{\mathbb{L}}_{t}
, etc., and
\mathbb{F}_{q}(z)
-linear automorphisms of their
z
-counterparts, and the fixed rings of
\tau
are

\mathbb{T}_{t}^{\tau}=\mathbb{F}_{q}[t],\quad\mathbb{L}_{t}^{\tau}=\mathbb{F}_{q}(t),\quad\widehat{\mathbb{L}}_{t}^{\tau}=\mathbb{F}_{q}(t),

(e.g., see
[
24
]
*Lem. 2.2
[
57
]
*Lem. 3.3.2). Furthermore,
\tau
restricts to (non-invertible) endomorphisms of
\mathbb{T}_{t}(K_{\infty})
,
\mathbb{L}_{t}(K_{\infty})
,
\widehat{\mathbb{L}}_{t}(K_{\infty})
, etc.

2.1.5.
Twisted polynomials

Let
R
be any commutative
\mathbb{F}_{q}
-algebra, and let
\tau:R\to R
be an injective
\mathbb{F}_{q}
-algebra endomorphism. Let
R^{\tau}
be the
\mathbb{F}_{q}
-subalgebra of
R
of elements fixed by
\tau
. For
n\in\mathbb{Z}
for which
\tau^{n}
is defined on
R
and a matrix
B=(b_{ij})
with entries in
R
, we let
B^{(n)}
be defined by twisting each entry. That is,
(b_{ij})^{(n)}=(b_{ij}^{(n)})
. For
\ell\geqslant 1
we let
\Mat_{\ell}(R)[\tau]=\Mat_{\ell}(R[\tau])
be the ring of
twisted polynomials
in
\tau
with coefficients in
\Mat_{\ell}(R)
, subject to the relation
\tau B=B^{(1)}\tau
for
B\in\Mat_{\ell}(R)
.
In this way,
R^{\ell}
is a left
\Mat_{\ell}(R)[\tau]
-module, where if
\beta=B_{0}+B_{1}\tau+\dots+B_{m}\tau^{m}\in\Mat_{\ell}(R)[\tau]
and
\mathbf{x}\in R^{\ell}
, then

(2.1.6)

\beta(\mathbf{x})=B_{0}\mathbf{x}+B_{1}\mathbf{x}^{(1)}+\cdots+B_{m}\mathbf{x}^{(m)}.

More generally (
2.1.6
) extends to
\beta\in\Mat_{k\times\ell}(R[\tau])
and
\mathbf{x}\in\Mat_{\ell\times n}(R)
, thus defining

(2.1.7)

(\beta,\mathbf{x})\mapsto\beta(\mathbf{x}):\Mat_{k\times\ell}(R[\tau])\times\Mat_{\ell\times n}(R)\to\Mat_{k\times n}(R),

which is
R^{\tau}
-bilinear and
R
-linear in the first entry. Furthermore,

(2.1.8)

(\beta_{1}\beta_{2})(\mathbf{x})=\beta_{1}(\beta_{2}(\mathbf{x})),

for all
\beta_{1}
,
\beta_{2}
matrices over
R[\tau]
and
\mathbf{x}
over
R
of the appropriate sizes.

If furthermore
\tau
is an automorphism of
R
, then we set
\sigma\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\tau^{-1}
and form the twisted polynomial ring
\Mat_{\ell}(R)[\sigma]
, subject to
\sigma B=B^{(-1)}\sigma
for
B\in\Mat_{\ell}(R)
.
Then
R^{\ell}
is a left
\Mat_{\ell}(R)[\sigma]
-module, where for
\gamma=C_{0}+C_{1}\sigma+\dots+C_{m}\sigma^{m}\in\Mat_{\ell}(R)[\sigma]
and
\mathbf{x}\in R^{\ell}
,

(2.1.9)

\gamma(\mathbf{x})=C_{0}\mathbf{x}+C_{1}\mathbf{x}^{(-1)}+\cdots+C_{m}\mathbf{x}^{(-m)}.

As above this evaluation operation extends to

(2.1.10)

(\gamma,\mathbf{x})\mapsto\gamma(\mathbf{x}):\Mat_{k\times\ell}(R[\sigma])\times\Mat_{\ell\times n}(R)\to\Mat_{k\times n}(R),

which is
R^{\tau}
-bilinear and
R
-linear in the first entry. It is also associative as in
(
2.1.8
).

For
\beta\in\Mat_{\ell}(R)[\tau]
(or
\gamma\in\Mat_{\ell}(R)[\sigma]
), we write
\partial\beta
(or
\partial\gamma
) for the constant term with respect to
\tau
(or
\sigma
). We have natural inclusions of
\mathbb{F}_{q}
-algebras,

\Mat_{\ell}(R)[\tau]\subseteq{\Mat_{\ell}(R)[\![\tau]\!]},\quad\Mat_{\ell}(R)[\sigma]\subseteq{\Mat_{\ell}(R)[\![\sigma]\!]},

into
twisted power series
rings, the latter when
\tau
is an automorphism.

2.1.11.
Ore anti-involution

We assume that
\tau:R\to R
is an automorphism, and recall the anti-isomorphism
*:R[\tau]\to R[\sigma]
of
\mathbb{F}_{q}
-algebras originally defined by Ore
[
55
]
(see also
[
44
]
*§1.7
[
54
]
*§2.3
[
63
]
), given by

\biggl(\sum_{i=0}^{\ell}b_{i}\tau^{i}\biggr)^{*}=\sum_{i=0}^{\ell}b_{i}^{(-i)}\sigma^{i}.

One verifies that
(\alpha\beta)^{*}=\beta^{*}\alpha^{*}
for
\alpha
,
\beta\in R[\tau]
. For
B=(\beta_{ij})\in\Mat_{k\times\ell}(R[\tau])
, we set

B^{*}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\bigl(\beta_{ij}^{*}\bigr)^{{\mathsf{T}}}\in\Mat_{\ell\times k}(R[\sigma]),

which then satisfies

(2.1.12)

(BC)^{*}=C^{*}B^{*}\in\Mat_{m\times k}(R[\sigma]),\quad B\in\Mat_{k\times\ell}(R[\tau]),\ C\in\Mat_{\ell\times m}(R[\tau]).

The inverse of
*:\Mat_{k\times\ell}(R[\tau])\to\Mat_{\ell\times k}(R[\sigma])
is also denoted by “
*
.”

2.1.13.
Division algorithms

When
R
is a field and
\tau:R\to R
is an automorphism,
R[\tau]
and
R[\sigma]
possess both left and right division algorithms (see
[
23
]
*§7.3
[
44
]
*§1.6
[
56
]
). For
\alpha
,
\beta\in R[\tau]
with
\beta\neq 0
, there exist unique
\eta
,
\eta^{\prime}
,
\delta
,
\delta^{\prime}\in R[\tau]
so that

\alpha=\beta\eta+\delta,\quad\alpha=\eta^{\prime}\beta+\delta^{\prime},\quad\deg_{\tau}\delta<\deg_{\tau}\beta,\ \deg_{\tau}\delta^{\prime}<\deg_{\tau}\beta.

In this way
R[\tau]
is both a left and right principal ideal domain. The right division algorithm does not require
\tau
to be an automorphism, but it is required for the left division algorithm. By applying the
*
-anti-involution, similar statements hold for
R[\sigma]
.

Proposition 2.1.14

(cf. Anderson
[
4
]
*Props. 1.4.2, 1.4.4)
.

Let
R
be a field such that
\tau:R\to R
is an automorphism. Let
B\in\Mat_{\ell\times k}(R[\tau])
.

(a)

There exist
U\in\GL_{\ell}(R[\tau])
and
V\in\GL_{k}(R[\tau])
so that the entries of
D=UBV
vanish off its diagonal.

(b)

If
\ell\geqslant k
and if the diagonal entries of
D
are
\delta_{1},\dots,\delta_{k}\in R[\tau]
, then

\dim_{R}\biggl(\frac{\Mat_{1\times k}(R[\tau])}{\Mat_{1\times\ell}(R[\tau])B}\biggr)=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\dim_{R}\biggl(\frac{R[\tau]}{R[\tau]\delta_{i}}\biggr).

With appropriate changes, similar results hold for matrices over
R[\sigma]
.

The proof of this proposition follows exactly as the proofs of
[
4
]
*Props. 1.4.2, 1.4.4, relying on the left and right division algorithms for
R[\tau]
and
R[\sigma]
, and generally follows from standard arguments for modules over skew polynomial rings
[
23
]
*§7.2–7.3.

2.1.15.
Orders of finite
F[x]
-modules

For
F[x]
a polynomial ring in one variable over a field
F
, we say that an
F[x]
-module is
finite
if it is finitely generated and torsion.
Now fix a finite
F[x]
-module
M
. Then there are monic polynomials
f_{1},\dots,f_{\ell}\in F[x]
so that

M\cong F[x]/(f_{1})\oplus\dots\oplus F[x]/(f_{\ell}).

We set
{[M]}_{F[x]}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=f_{1}\cdots f_{\ell}\in F[x]
, which is a generator of the Fitting ideal of
M
, and we call
{[M]}_{F[x]}
the
F[x]
-order
of
M
. If
m_{x}:M\to M
is left-multiplication by
x
, then

{[M]}_{F[x]}=\Char(m_{x},M,X)|_{X=x},

where
\Char(m_{x},M,X)\in F[X]
is the characteristic polynomial of
m_{x}
as an
F
-linear map.
For a variable
y
independent from
x
, but
M
still an
F[x]
-module, we will write

{[M]}_{F[y]}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}={[M]}_{F[x]}|_{x=y}=\Char(m_{x},M,y).

This will be of particular use for us when
M
is an
\mathsf{A}
-module (or
\mathbf{A}
-module), where

{[M]}_{A}={[M]}_{\mathsf{A}}|_{t=\theta}=\Char(m_{t},M,\theta)\in A,\ \textup{(or ${[M]}_{\mathbb{A}}={[M]}_{\mathbf{A}}|_{t=\theta}=\Char(m_{t},M,\theta)\in\mathbb{A}$),}

coercing
A
-orders and
\mathbb{A}
-orders to be elements of our scalar fields.

2.2.
Drinfeld modules, Anderson
t
-modules, and their adjoints

Given a field
F\supseteq\mathbb{F}_{q}
and an
\mathbb{F}_{q}
-algebra map
\iota:\mathsf{A}\to F
, we call
F
an
\mathsf{A}
-field
. The kernel of
\iota
is the
characteristic
of
F
, and if
\iota
is injective then the characteristic is
generic
. If
F\subseteq\mathbb{C}_{\infty}
has generic characteristic, then we always assume that
\iota(t)=\theta
. Otherwise,
\iota(t)=\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}\mkern 2.5mu\overline{\mkern-2.5mu\theta}\in F
.

2.2.1.
Drinfeld modules and Anderson
t
-modules

A
Drinfeld module
over
F
is defined by an
\mathbb{F}_{q}
-algebra homomorphism
\phi:\mathsf{A}\to F[\tau]
such that

(2.2.2)

\phi_{t}=\mkern 2.5mu\overline{\mkern-2.5mu\theta}+\kappa_{1}\tau+\dots+\kappa_{r}\tau^{r},\quad\kappa_{r}\neq 0.

We say that
\phi
has
rank
r
. We then make
F
into an
\mathsf{A}
-module by setting

t\cdot x\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\phi_{t}(x)=\mkern 2.5mu\overline{\mkern-2.5mu\theta}x+\kappa_{1}x^{q}+\cdots+\kappa_{r}x^{q^{r}},\quad x\in F.

Similarly an
Anderson
t
-module of dimension
\ell
over
F
is defined by an
\mathbb{F}_{q}
-algebra homomorphism
\psi:\mathsf{A}\to\Mat_{\ell}(F)[\tau]
such that

(2.2.3)

\psi_{t}=\partial\psi_{t}+E_{1}\tau+\dots+E_{w}\tau^{w},\quad E_{i}\in\Mat_{\ell}(F),

where
\partial\psi_{t}-\mkern 2.5mu\overline{\mkern-2.5mu\theta}\cdot\mathrm{I}_{\ell}
is nilpotent. A Drinfeld module is then
a
t
-module of dimension
1
.
We write
\psi(F)
for
F^{\ell}
with the
\mathsf{A}
-module structure given by
a\cdot\mathbf{x}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\psi_{a}(\mathbf{x})
through (
2.1.6
). Similarly, we write
\Lie(\psi)(F)
for
F^{\ell}
with
F[t]
-module structure defined by
\partial\psi_{a}
for
a\in\mathsf{A}
. For
a\in\mathsf{A}
, the
a
-torsion submodule of
\psi(\mkern 2.5mu\overline{\mkern-2.5muF})
is denoted

\psi[a]\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\{\mathbf{x}\in\smash{\mkern 2.5mu\overline{\mkern-2.5muF}}^{\ell}\mid\psi_{a}(\mathbf{x})=0\}.

Given
t
-modules
\phi:\mathsf{A}\to\Mat_{k}(F)[\tau]
,
\psi:\mathsf{A}\to\Mat_{\ell}(F)[\tau]
, a morphism
\eta:\phi\to\psi
is a matrix
\eta\in\Mat_{\ell\times k}(F[\tau])
such that

\eta\phi_{a}=\psi_{a}\eta
for all
a\in\mathsf{A}
.
Moreover,
\eta
induces an
\mathsf{A}
-module homomorphism
\eta:\phi(F)\to\psi(F)
, and we have a functor
\psi\mapsto\psi(F)
from the category of
t
-modules to
\mathsf{A}
-modules. We also have an induced map of
F[t]
-modules,
\partial\psi:\Lie(\phi)(F)\to\Lie(\psi)(F)
.

Anderson defined
t
-modules in
[
4
]
, and following his language we sometimes abbreviate “Anderson
t
-module” by “
t
-module.” For more information about Drinfeld modules and
t
-modules see
[
44
,
71
]
.

2.2.4.
Exponential and logarithm series

Suppose now that
F\subseteq\mathbb{C}_{\infty}
has generic characteristic and that
\psi
is defined over
F
. Then there is a twisted power series
\Exp_{\psi}\in{\Mat_{\ell}(F)[\![\tau]\!]}
, called the
exponential series
of
\psi
, such that

\Exp_{\psi}=\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}B_{i}\tau^{i},\quad B_{0}=\mathrm{I}_{\ell},\ B_{i}\in\Mat_{\ell}(F),

and for all
a\in\mathsf{A}
,
\Exp_{\psi}{}\cdot\partial\psi_{a}=\psi_{a}\cdot\Exp_{\psi}
.
This functional identity for
a=t
induces a recursive relation that uniquely determines
\Exp_{\psi}
. That the coefficient matrices have entries in
F
is due to Anderson
[
4
]
*Prop. 2.1.4, Lem. 2.1.6. In fact if
\psi
is defined over
H
with
K\subseteq H\subseteq F
, then
B_{i}\in\Mat_{\ell}(H)
even if
H
is not perfect (see
[
54
]
*Rem. 2.2.3 for further discussion). The exponential series induces an
\mathbb{F}_{q}
-linear and entire function,

\Exp_{\psi}:\mathbb{C}_{\infty}^{\ell}\to\mathbb{C}_{\infty}^{\ell},\quad\Exp_{\psi}(\mathbf{z})=\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}B_{i}\mathbf{z}^{(i)},\quad\mathbf{z}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=(z_{1},\dots,z_{\ell})^{{\mathsf{T}}},

called the
exponential function
of
\psi
. That
\Exp_{\psi}
converges everywhere is equivalent to

\lim_{i\to\infty}{\lvert B_{i}\rvert}_{\infty}^{1/q^{i}}=0\Leftrightarrow\lim_{i\to\infty}\deg(B_{i})/q^{i}=-\infty
.
We also identify the exponential function with the
\mathbb{F}_{q}
-linear formal power series
\Exp_{\psi}(\mathbf{z})\in{\mathbb{C}_{\infty}[\![\mathbf{z}]\!]}^{\ell}
. The functional equation for
\Exp_{\psi}
induces the identities,

\Exp_{\psi}(\partial\psi_{a}\mathbf{z})=\psi_{a}\bigl(\Exp_{\psi}(\mathbf{z})\bigr),\quad\forall\,a\in\mathsf{A}.

The exponential function of
\psi
is always surjective for Drinfeld modules, but it may not be surjective when
\ell\geqslant 2
. We say that
\psi
is
uniformizable
if
\Exp_{\psi}:\mathbb{C}_{\infty}^{\ell}\to\mathbb{C}_{\infty}^{\ell}
is surjective. The kernel of
\Exp_{\psi}\subseteq\mathbb{C}_{\infty}^{\ell}
,

\Lambda_{\psi}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\ker\Exp_{\psi},

is a finitely generated and discrete
\partial\psi(\mathsf{A})
-submodule of
\mathbb{C}_{\infty}^{\ell}
called the
period lattice
of
\psi
.
Thus if
\psi
is uniformizable, then we obtain an exact sequence of
\mathsf{A}
-modules,

0\to\Lambda_{\psi}\to\mathbb{C}_{\infty}^{\ell}\xrightarrow{\Exp_{\psi}}\psi(\mathbb{C}_{\infty})\to 0.

As an element of
{\Mat_{\ell}(F)[\![\tau]\!]}
the series
\Exp_{\psi}
is invertible, and we let
\Log_{\psi}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\Exp_{\psi}^{-1}\in{\Mat_{\ell}(F)[\![\tau]\!]}
be the
logarithm series
of
\psi
, satisfying

\Log_{\psi}=\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}C_{i}\tau^{i},\quad C_{0}=\mathrm{I}_{\ell},\ C_{i}\in\Mat_{\ell}(F).

Together with the
logarithm function
,
\Log_{\psi}(\mathbf{z})=\sum_{i\geqslant 0}C_{i}\mathbf{z}^{(i)}\in{\mathbb{C}_{\infty}[\![\mathbf{z}]\!]}^{\ell}
, we have

\partial\psi_{a}\cdot\Log_{\psi}=\Log_{\psi}{}\cdot\psi_{a}
and
\partial\psi_{a}(\Log_{\psi}(\mathbf{z}))=\Log_{\psi}(\psi_{a}(\mathbf{z}))
, for all
a\in\mathsf{A}
.
In general
\Log_{\psi}(\mathbf{z})
converges only on an open polydisc in
\mathbb{C}_{\infty}^{\ell}
. For example, if
\phi:\mathsf{A}\to\mathbb{C}_{\infty}[\tau]
is a Drinfeld module as in (
2.2.2
), then
\Log_{\phi}(z)
converges on the open disk of radius
R_{\phi}
, where

(2.2.5)

R_{\phi}={\lvert\theta\rvert}_{\infty}^{-\max\{(\deg\kappa_{i}-q^{i})/(q^{i}-1)\,\mid\,1\leqslant i\leqslant r,\,\kappa_{i}\neq 0\}}

(see
[
27
]
*Rem. 6.11
[
49
]
*Cor. 4.5).

2.2.6.
Adjoints of
t
-modules

Assume now that
F
is a perfect
\mathsf{A}
-field and that
\psi:\mathsf{A}\to\Mat_{\ell}(F)[\tau]
is an Anderson
t
-module over
F
defined as in (
2.2.3
). The
adjoint
of
\psi
is defined to be the
\mathbb{F}_{q}
-algebra homomorphism
\psi^{*}:\mathsf{A}\to\Mat_{\ell}(F)[\sigma]
defined by

\psi_{a}^{*}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=(\psi_{a})^{*},\quad\forall\,a\in\mathsf{A}.

Since for
a
,
b\in\mathsf{A}
we have
\psi_{ab}=\psi_{a}\psi_{b}=\psi_{b}\psi_{a}
, (
2.1.12
) implies that
\psi^{*}
respects multiplication, which is the nontrivial part of checking that
\psi^{*}
is an
\mathbb{F}_{q}
-algebra homomorphism. From (
2.2.3
), we have

\psi^{*}_{t}=(\psi_{t})^{*}=(\partial\psi_{t})^{{\mathsf{T}}}+\bigl(E_{1}^{(-1)}\bigr)^{{\mathsf{T}}}\sigma+\cdots+\bigl(E_{w}^{(-w)}\bigr)^{{\mathsf{T}}}\sigma^{w},

and so for any
\mathbf{x}\in F^{\ell}
, we have
\psi^{*}_{t}(\mathbf{x})=(\partial\psi_{t})^{{\mathsf{T}}}\mathbf{x}+(E_{1}^{(-1)})^{{\mathsf{T}}}\mathbf{x}^{(-1)}+\cdots+(E_{w}^{(-w)})^{{\mathsf{T}}}\mathbf{x}^{(-w)}
.
In this way the map
\psi^{*}
induces an
\mathsf{A}
-module structure on
F^{\ell}
, which we denote
\psi^{*}(F)
. Similarly we denote
\Lie(\psi^{*})(F)=F^{\ell}
with an
F[t]
-module structure induced by
\partial\psi_{a}^{{\mathsf{T}}}
for
a\in\mathsf{A}
. For
a\in\mathsf{A}
, the
a
-torsion submodule of
\psi^{*}(\mkern 2.5mu\overline{\mkern-2.5muF})
is denoted

\psi^{*}[a]\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\{\mathbf{x}\in\smash{\mkern 2.5mu\overline{\mkern-2.5muF}}^{\ell}\mid\psi^{*}_{a}(\mathbf{x})=0\}.

If
\eta:\phi\to\psi
is a morphism of
t
-modules as above, then
\eta^{*}\in\Mat_{k\times\ell}(F)[\sigma]
provides a morphism
\eta^{*}:\psi^{*}\to\phi^{*}
such that
\eta^{*}\psi_{a}^{*}=\phi_{a}^{*}\eta^{*}
for all
a\in\mathsf{A}
(and vice versa). Furthermore,
\partial\eta^{*}:\Lie(\psi^{*})(F)\to\Lie(\phi^{*})(F)
is an
F[t]
-module homomorphism. Adjoints of Drinfeld modules were investigated extensively by Goss
[
44
]
*§4.14 and Poonen
[
63
]
, and we will explore further properties for adjoints of Anderson modules in §
3.6
.

2.3.
Tate modules and characteristic polynomials for Drinfeld modules

We fix a Drinfeld module
\phi:\mathsf{A}\to A[\tau]
of rank
r
in generic characteristic, given by

\phi_{t}=\theta+\kappa_{1}\tau+\cdots+\kappa_{r}\tau^{r},\quad\kappa_{i}\in A,\ \kappa_{r}\neq 0.

Letting
f\in A_{+}
be irreducible of degree
d
, the reduction of
\phi
modulo
f
is a Drinfeld module
\mkern 2.5mu\overline{\mkern-2.5mu\phi}:\mathsf{A}\to\mathbb{F}_{f}[\tau]
of rank
r_{0}\leqslant r
, where
\mathbb{F}_{f}=A/fA
. Then
\phi
has
good reduction
modulo
f
if
r_{0}=r
or equivalently if
f\nmid\kappa_{r}
.

For
\lambda\in\mathsf{A}_{+}
irreducible, we form the
\lambda
-adic
Tate modules
,

T_{\lambda}(\phi)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\varprojlim\phi[\lambda^{m}],\quad T_{\lambda}(\mkern 2.5mu\overline{\mkern-2.5mu\phi})\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\varprojlim\mkern 2.5mu\overline{\mkern-2.5mu\phi}[\lambda^{m}].

As an
\mathsf{A}_{\lambda}
-module,
T_{\lambda}(\phi)\cong\mathsf{A}_{\lambda}^{r}
, and if
\lambda(\theta)\neq f
, then likewise
T_{\lambda}(\mkern 2.5mu\overline{\mkern-2.5mu\phi})\cong\mathsf{A}_{\lambda}^{r_{0}}
. Fixing henceforth that
\lambda(\theta)\neq f
, we set
P_{f}(X)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\Char(\tau^{d},T_{\lambda}(\mkern 2.5mu\overline{\mkern-2.5mu\phi}),X)|_{t=\theta}
to be the characteristic polynomial of the
q^{d}
-th power Frobenius acting on
T_{\lambda}(\mkern 2.5mu\overline{\mkern-2.5mu\phi})
but, for convenience, with coefficients forced into
A
(rather than
\mathsf{A}
). Thus we have

(2.3.1)

P_{f}(X)=X^{r_{0}}+c_{r_{0}-1}X^{r_{0}-1}+\cdots+c_{0}\in A[X].

Takahashi
[
69
]
*Prop. 3 showed that the coefficients in
A
and are independent of the choice of
\lambda
(see also Gekeler
[
31
]
*Cor. 3.4).
We note that if
\phi
has good reduction modulo
\lambda
and if
\alpha_{f}\in\Gal(K^{\mathrm{sep}}/K)
is a Frobenius element, then (e.g., see
[
41
]
*§3
[
44
]
*§8.6)

\Char(\tau^{d},T_{\lambda}(\mkern 2.5mu\overline{\mkern-2.5mu\phi}),X)=\Char(\alpha_{f},T_{\lambda}(\phi),X)\in\mathsf{A}[X].

2.3.2.
Properties of
P_{f}(X)

The following results are due to Gekeler
[
31
]
*Thm. 5.1 and Takahashi
[
69
]
*Lem. 2, Prop. 3.

•

We have
c_{0}=c_{f}^{-1}f
for some
c_{f}\in\mathbb{F}_{q}^{\times}
.

•

The ideal
(P_{f}(1))\subseteq A
is an Euler-Poincaré characteristic for
\mkern 2.5mu\overline{\mkern-2.5mu\phi}(\mathbb{F}_{f})
.

•

The roots
\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{r_{0}}
of
P_{f}(x)
in
\mkern 2.5mu\overline{\mkern-2.5muK}
satisfy
\deg_{\theta}\gamma_{i}=d/r_{0}
.

Extending these a little further, for
1\leqslant j\leqslant r_{0}
, we have
\deg_{\theta}c_{r_{0}-j}\leqslant jd/r_{0}
. Additionally,

(2.3.3)

\bigl[\mkern 2.5mu\overline{\mkern-2.5mu\phi}(\mathbb{F}_{f})\bigr]_{A}=c_{f}P_{f}(1)

by
[
18
]
*Cor. 3.2. Here we use the convention from §
2.1.15
that
{[\mkern 2.5mu\overline{\mkern-2.5mu\phi}(\mathbb{F}_{f})]}_{A}={[\mkern 2.5mu\overline{\mkern-2.5mu\phi}(\mathbb{F}_{f})]}_{\mathsf{A}}|_{t=\theta}
. Following the exposition in
[
18
]
*§3, we let
P_{f}^{\vee}(X)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\Char(\tau^{d},T_{\lambda}(\mkern 2.5mu\overline{\mkern-2.5mu\phi})^{\vee},X)|_{t=\theta}
be the characteristic polynomial in
K[X]
of
\tau^{d}
acting on the the dual space of
T_{\lambda}(\mkern 2.5mu\overline{\mkern-2.5mu\phi})
. We let
Q_{f}(X)=X^{r_{0}}P_{f}(1/X)
and
Q_{f}^{\vee}(X)=X^{r_{0}}P_{f}^{\vee}(1/X)
be the reciprocal polynomials of
P_{f}(X)
and
P_{f}^{\vee}(X)
, and consider
Q_{f}^{\vee}(fX)=1+c_{f}c_{1}X+c_{f}c_{2}fX^{2}+\cdots+c_{f}c_{r_{0}-1}f^{r_{0}-2}X^{r_{0}-1}+c_{f}f^{r_{0}-1}X^{r_{0}}
. To denote the dependence on
\phi
, we write
P_{\phi,f}(X)
,
Q_{\phi,f}(X)
, etc.

By varying
f
, we use
Q_{f}^{\vee}(fX)
and
Q_{f}(X)
to define multiplicative functions
\mu_{\phi}
,
\nu_{\phi}:A_{+}\to A
such that on powers of a given
f
,

(2.3.4)

\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\mu_{\phi}(f^{m})X^{m}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\frac{1}{Q_{f}^{\vee}(fX)},\quad\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\nu_{\phi}(f^{m})X^{m}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\frac{1}{Q_{f}(X)}.

2.3.5.
Everywhere good reduction

Hsia and Yu
[
48
]
have determined precise formulas for
c_{f}
in terms of the
(q-1)
-st power residue symbol. Of particular interest presently is the case that
\phi
has everywhere good reduction, i.e., when
\kappa_{r}\in\mathbb{F}_{q}^{\times}
. In this case, Hsia and Yu
[
48
]
*Thm. 3.2, Eqs. (2) & (8) showed that
c_{f}=(-1)^{r+d(r+1)}\kappa_{r}^{d}
.
This prompts the definition of a completely multiplicative function
\chi_{\phi}:A_{+}\to\mathbb{F}_{q}^{\times}
,

(2.3.6)

\chi_{\phi}(a)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\bigl((-1)^{r+1}\kappa_{r}\bigr)^{\deg_{\theta}a},

for which we see that
c_{f}=(-1)^{r}\chi_{\phi}(f)
. Letting
\overline{\chi}_{\phi}:A_{+}\to\mathbb{F}_{q}^{\times}
be the multiplicative inverse of
\chi_{\phi}
, we see that

(2.3.7)

\displaystyle P_{f}(X)

\displaystyle=X^{r}+c_{r-1}X^{r-1}+\cdots+c_{1}X+(-1)^{r}\overline{\chi}_{\phi}(f)\cdot f,

\displaystyle P_{f}^{\vee}(X)

\displaystyle=X^{r}+\frac{(-1)^{r}\chi_{\phi}(f)c_{1}}{f}X^{r-1}+\cdots+\frac{(-1)^{r}\chi_{\phi}(f)c_{r-1}}{f}X+\frac{(-1)^{r}\chi_{\phi}(f)}{f},

and likewise

(2.3.8)

\displaystyle Q_{f}(X)

\displaystyle=1+c_{r-1}X+\cdots+c_{1}X^{r-1}+(-1)^{r}\overline{\chi}_{\phi}(f)\cdot fX^{r},

\displaystyle Q_{f}^{\vee}(fX)

\displaystyle=\begin{aligned} 1+(-1)^{r}\chi_{\phi}(f)c_{1}X+\cdots+(-1)^{r}&\chi_{\phi}(f)c_{r-1}f^{r-2}X^{r-1}\\
&{}+(-1)^{r}\chi_{\phi}(f)f^{r-1}X^{r}.\end{aligned}

Moreover,

(2.3.9)

\mu_{\phi}(f)=(-1)^{r+1}\chi_{\phi}(f)c_{1},\quad\nu_{\phi}(f)=-c_{r-1}.

We record the induced recursive relations (cf.
[
18
]
*Lem. 3.5) on
\mu_{\phi}
and
\nu_{\phi}
, where taking
m+r\geqslant 1
and using the convention that
\mu_{\phi}(b)=\nu_{\phi}(b)=0
if
b\in K\setminus A_{+}
,

(2.3.10)

\displaystyle\mu_{\phi}(f^{m+r})

\displaystyle=\begin{aligned} \mu_{\phi}(f)\mu_{\phi}(f^{m+r-1})-(-1)^{r}\chi_{\phi}(f)\sum_{j=2}^{r-1}&c_{j}f^{j-1}\mu_{\phi}(f^{m+r-j})\\
&{}-(-1)^{r}\chi_{\phi}(f)f^{r-1}\mu_{\phi}(f^{m}),\end{aligned}

(2.3.11)

\displaystyle\nu_{\phi}(f^{m+r})

\displaystyle=\nu_{\phi}(f)\nu_{\phi}(f^{m+r-1})-\sum_{j=2}^{r-1}c_{r-j}\nu_{\phi}(f^{m+r-j})-(-1)^{r}\overline{\chi}_{\phi}(f)f\nu_{\phi}(f^{m}).

Later we may write “
\mu_{\phi,\theta}(a)
” and “
\nu_{\phi,\theta}(a)
” for
\mu_{\phi}(a)
and
\nu_{\phi}(a)
to emphasize that they take values in
A
, and use
\mu_{\phi,z}(a)=\mu_{\theta}(a)|_{\theta=z}
and similarly
\nu_{\phi,z}(a)
to switch to values in
\mathbb{F}_{q}[z]
. We use
\mu_{\phi}
and
\nu_{\phi}
to define Goss
L
-functions
L(\phi^{\vee},s-1)
and
L(\phi,s)
in §
5.1
.

2.4.
t
-motives and dual
t
-motives

We recall here basic information about
t
-motives and dual
t
-motives attached to
t
-modules, which will be extended to Anderson
t
-modules over
\tau
-perfect fields and expanded on in §
3
. For this section we fix a perfect
\mathsf{A}
-field
F
and
t
-module
\psi:\mathsf{A}\to\Mat_{\ell}(F)[\tau]
as in (
2.2.3
). Recall that
\mkern 2.5mu\overline{\mkern-2.5mu\theta}=\iota(t)\in F
.

2.4.1.
t
-motive of
\psi

We let
\mathcal{M}_{\psi}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\Mat_{1\times\ell}(F[\tau])
, and make
\mathcal{M}_{\psi}
into a left
F[t,\tau]
-module by using the inherent structure as a left
F[\tau]
-module and setting

a\cdot m\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=m\psi_{a},\quad m\in\mathcal{M}_{\psi},\ a\in\mathsf{A}.

Then
\mathcal{M}_{\psi}
is called the
t
-motive
of
\psi
. We note that for any
m\in\mathcal{M}_{\psi}
,

(t-\mkern 2.5mu\overline{\mkern-2.5mu\theta})^{\ell}\cdot m\in\tau\mathcal{M}_{\psi},

since
\partial\psi_{t}-\mkern 2.5mu\overline{\mkern-2.5mu\theta}\mathrm{I}_{\ell}
is nilpotent (and
F
is perfect). If we need to emphasize the dependence on the base field
F
, we write

\mathcal{M}_{\psi}(F)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathcal{M}_{\psi}=\Mat_{1\times\ell}(F[\tau]).

A morphism
\eta:\phi\to\psi
of
t
-modules over
F
of dimensions
k
and
\ell
, defined as in §
2.2
, induces a morphism of left
F[t,\tau]
-modules
\eta^{\dagger}:\mathcal{M}_{\psi}\to\mathcal{M}_{\phi}
, given by
\eta^{\dagger}(m)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=m\eta
for
m\in\mathcal{M}_{\psi}
. The functor from
t
-modules over
F
to
t
-motives over
F
is fully faithful, and so every left
F[t,\tau]
-module homomorphism
\mathcal{M}_{\psi}\to\mathcal{M}_{\phi}
arises in this way.

By construction
\mathcal{M}_{\psi}
is free of rank
\ell
as a left
F[\tau]
-module, and we say
\ell
is the
dimension
of
\mathcal{M}_{\psi}
. If
\mathcal{M}_{\psi}
is further free of finite rank over
F[t]
, then
\mathcal{M}_{\psi}
is said to be
abelian
and
r=\rank_{F[t]}\mathcal{M}_{\psi}
is the
rank
of
\mathcal{M}_{\psi}
. We will say that
\psi
is abelian or has rank
r
if
\mathcal{M}_{\psi}
possesses the corresponding properties. The
t
-motives in Anderson’s original definition in
[
4
]
are abelian, as will be most of the
t
-motives in this paper, but for example, see
[
15
]

[
44
]
*Ch. 5
[
47
]

[
54
]
*Ch. 2–4) for
t
-motives in this wider context.

2.4.2.
Dual
t
-motive of
\psi

We let
\mathcal{N}_{\psi}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\Mat_{1\times\ell}(F[\sigma])
, and similar to the case of
t
-motives, we define a left
F[t,\sigma]
-module structure on
\mathcal{N}_{\psi}
by setting

a\cdot n\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=n\psi_{a}^{*},\quad n\in\mathcal{N}_{\psi},\ a\in\mathsf{A}.

The module
\mathcal{N}_{\psi}
is the
dual
t
-motive
of
\psi
. As in the case of
t
-motives, for any
n\in\mathcal{N}_{\psi}
we have
(t-\mkern 2.5mu\overline{\mkern-2.5mu\theta})^{\ell}\cdot n\in\sigma\mathcal{N}_{\psi}
. Also if we need to emphasize the dependence on
F
, we write

\mathcal{N}_{\psi}(F)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathcal{N}_{\psi}=\Mat_{1\times\ell}(F[\sigma]).

Again for a morphism
\eta:\phi\to\psi
of
t
-modules of dimensions
k
and
\ell
, we obtain a morphism of left
F[t,\sigma]
-modules,
\eta^{\ddagger}:\mathcal{N}_{\phi}\to\mathcal{N}_{\psi}
, given by
\eta^{\ddagger}(n)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=n\eta^{*}
for
n\in\mathcal{N}_{\phi}
. Also, every morphism of left
F[t,\sigma]
-modules
\mathcal{N}_{\phi}\to\mathcal{N}_{\psi}
arises in this way.

The dual
t
-motive
\mathcal{N}_{\psi}
is free of rank
\ell
as a left
F[\sigma]
-module, and
\ell
is the
dimension
of
\mathcal{N}_{\psi}
. If
\mathcal{N}_{\psi}
is free of finite rank over
F[t]
, then we say
\mathcal{N}_{\psi}
is
\mathsf{A}
-finite
, and we call
r=\rank_{F[t]}(\mathcal{N}_{\psi})
the
rank
of
\mathcal{N}_{\psi}
. It has been shown by Maurischat
[
52
]
that for a
t
-module
\psi
, the
t
-motive
\mathcal{M}_{\psi}
is abelian if and only if the dual
t
-motive
\mathcal{N}_{\psi}
is
\mathsf{A}
-finite. In this case the rank of
\mathcal{M}_{\psi}
is the same as the rank of
\mathcal{N}_{\psi}
. We will say that
\psi
is
\mathsf{A}
-finite or has rank
r
if
\mathcal{N}_{\psi}
has those properties.
Dual
t
-motives were initially introduced in
[
2
]
over fields of generic characteristic. See
[
15
]

[
47
]

[
52
]

[
54
]
*Ch. 2–4, for more information.

We call
\mathbf{m}=(m_{1},\dots,m_{r})^{{\mathsf{T}}}\in\Mat_{r\times 1}(\mathcal{M}_{\psi}(F))
a
basis
of
\mathcal{M}_{\psi}(F)
if
m_{1},\dots,m_{r}
form an
F[t]
-basis of
\mathcal{M}_{\psi}(F)
. Likewise
\mathbf{n}=(n_{1},\dots,n_{r})^{{\mathsf{T}}}\in\Mat_{r\times 1}(\mathcal{N}_{\psi}(F))
is a
basis
of
\mathcal{N}_{\psi}(F)
if
n_{1},\dots,n_{r}
form an
F[t]
-basis of
\mathcal{N}_{\psi}(F)
. We then define
\Gamma
,
\Phi\in\Mat_{r}(F[t])
so that

\tau\mathbf{m}=\Gamma\mathbf{m},\quad\sigma\mathbf{n}=\Phi\mathbf{n}.

It follows that
\det\Gamma=c(t-\theta)^{\ell}
,
\det\Phi=c^{\prime}(t-\theta)^{\ell}
,
where
c
,
c^{\prime}\in F^{\times}
(e.g., see
[
54
]
*§3.2). Then
\Gamma

represents multiplication by
\tau
on
\mathcal{M}_{\psi}
and
\Phi

represents multiplication by
\sigma
on
\mathcal{N}_{\psi}
.

Example 2.4.3
.

Carlitz module.
The Carlitz module
\mathsf{C}:\mathsf{A}\to F[\tau]
over
F
is defined by

\mathsf{C}_{t}=\mkern 2.5mu\overline{\mkern-2.5mu\theta}+\tau,

and it has dimension
1
and rank
1
. Then
\mathbf{m}=\{1\}
is an
F[t]
-basis for
\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{C}}=F[\tau]
, and
\mathbf{n}=\{1\}
is an
F[t]
-basis for
\mathcal{N}_{\mathsf{C}}=F[\sigma]
. One finds that
\tau\cdot 1=(t-\theta)\cdot 1
in
\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{C}}
and
\sigma\cdot 1=(t-\theta)\cdot 1
in
\mathcal{N}_{\mathsf{C}}
, so
\Gamma=\Phi=t-\theta
.

Example 2.4.4
.

Drinfeld modules.
Let
\phi:\mathsf{A}\to F[\tau]
be a Drinfeld module over
F
of rank
r
defined as in (
2.2.2
). Then
\mathbf{m}=(1,\tau,\dots,\tau^{r-1})^{{\mathsf{T}}}
is a basis for
\mathcal{M}_{\psi}
and
\mathbf{n}=(1,\sigma,\dots,\sigma^{r-1})^{{\mathsf{T}}}
is a basis for
\mathcal{N}_{\psi}
. Furthermore,
\tau\mathbf{m}=\Gamma\mathbf{m}
and
\sigma\mathbf{n}=\Phi\mathbf{n}
, where

(2.4.5)

\Gamma=\begin{pmatrix}0&1&\cdots&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
0&0&\cdots&1\\
(t-\mkern 2.5mu\overline{\mkern-2.5mu\theta})/\kappa_{r}&-\kappa_{1}/\kappa_{r}&\cdots&-\kappa_{r-1}/\kappa_{r}\end{pmatrix},

and
\Phi
occurs similarly. See
[
21
]
*§3.3–3.4
[
54
]
*Ex. 3.5.14, Ex. 4.6.7
[
60
]
*§4.2 for details.

2.4.6.
Rigid analytic trivializations

We now specialize to the situation that
\psi:\mathsf{A}\to\Mat_{\ell}(F)
is an Anderson
t
-module defined over a perfect field
F
with
K\subseteq F\subseteq\mathbb{C}_{\infty}
of generic characteristic. We further assume that
\psi
is abelian of rank
r
, equivalently
\mathsf{A}
-finite of rank
r
by
[
52
]
. If
\Gamma
represents multiplication by
\tau
on
\mathcal{M}_{\psi}
, we set

\Theta\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\Gamma^{{\mathsf{T}}}\in\Mat_{r}(F[t]).

Then we say that
\mathcal{M}_{\psi}
is
rigid analytically trivial
if there exists
\Upsilon\in\GL_{r}(\mathbb{T}_{t})
so that

(2.4.7)

\Upsilon^{(1)}=\Upsilon\Theta.

By
[
4
]
*Thm. 4,
\psi
is uniformizable if and only if
\mathcal{M}_{\psi}
is rigid analytically trivial.

Remark 2.4.8
.

Definitions of rigid analytic trivializations using the dual
t
-motive
\mathcal{N}_{\psi}
have been investigated extensively in the context of transcendence theory (e.g., see
[
2
]

[
22
]

[
21
]

[
53
]

[
54
]
*Ch. 3–4
[
57
]

[
60
]
). However, for our purposes the rigid analytic trivialization for
\mathcal{M}_{\psi}
is more convenient. Moreover, the two types of rigid analytic trivializations are related by
[
47
]
*Thm. 2.5.13 (see
[
21
]
*§3.4
[
54
]
*§4.4–4.6 for additional discussion).

Example 2.4.9
.

Carlitz module.
The Carlitz exponential
\Exp_{\mathsf{C}}=\sum_{i\geqslant 0}D_{i}^{-1}\tau^{i}
and logarithm
\Log_{\mathsf{C}}=\sum_{i\geqslant 0}L_{i}^{-1}\tau^{i}
are defined for
D_{i}
,
L_{i}\in A
(see
[
44
]
*Ch. 3
[
71
]
*Ch. 2). Its period lattice
\Lambda_{\mathsf{C}}=A\widetilde{\pi}
is generated by the
Carlitz period
(see
[
17
]
*Thm. 5.1),

\widetilde{\pi}=-(-\theta)^{q/(q-1)}\prod_{i=1}^{\infty}\Bigl(1-\theta^{1-q^{i}}\Bigr)^{-1}\in K_{\infty}\bigl((-\theta)^{1/(q-1)}\bigr),

for a fixed choice of
(-\theta)^{1/(q-1)}
. The radius of convergence of
\Log_{\mathsf{C}}
is
R_{\mathsf{C}}=\lvert\widetilde{\pi}\rvert=\lvert\theta\rvert^{q/(q-1)}
. The rigid analytic trivialization for
\mathsf{C}
is the
Anderson-Thakur function

[
3
]
*§2.5,

(2.4.10)

\omega\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=(-\theta)^{1/(q-1)}\prod_{i=0}^{\infty}\biggl(1-\frac{t}{\theta^{q^{i}}}\biggr)^{-1}=\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\exp_{\mathsf{C}}\biggl(\frac{\widetilde{\pi}}{\theta^{m+1}}\biggr)t^{m}\in\mathbb{T}_{t}^{\times}.

The functional equation
\omega^{(1)}=(t-\theta)\omega
implies that

(2.4.11)

\frac{\omega^{(i)}}{\omega}=\bigl(t-\theta^{q^{i-1}}\bigr)\cdots\bigl(t-\theta^{q}\bigr)(t-\theta)\in A[t],\quad i\geqslant 0,

where we use the convention that the empty product is
1
so that the identity holds for
i=0
. Furthermore, we recover the Carlitz period by taking a residue at
t=\theta
,

\Res_{t=\theta}\omega=(t-\theta)\omega|_{t=\theta}=-\widetilde{\pi}.

For additional properties and generalizations of
\omega
, see
[
11
,
8
,
61
,
62
]
.

Example 2.4.12
.

Drinfeld modules.
For a Drinfeld module
\phi:\mathsf{A}\to F[\tau]
of rank
r
defined as in (
2.2.2
) over a perfect
\mathsf{A}
-field
F
of generic characteristic with
K\subseteq F\subseteq\mathbb{C}_{\infty}
, we fix generators
\pi_{1},\dots,\pi_{r}\in\Lambda_{\phi}
. For
i=1,\dots,r
, we define the
Anderson generating function
,

g_{i}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\Exp_{\phi}\biggl(\frac{\pi_{i}}{\theta^{m+1}}\biggr)t^{m}\in\mathbb{T}_{t},

which satisfies
\phi_{t}(g_{i})=\theta g_{i}+\kappa_{1}g_{i}^{(1)}+\cdots+\kappa_{r}g_{i}^{(r)}=tg_{i}
.
The Anderson-Thakur function
\omega
is then the Anderson generating function for
\widetilde{\pi}
by (
2.4.10
). By Example
2.4.4
,

(2.4.13)

\Theta=\Gamma^{{\mathsf{T}}}=\begin{pmatrix}0&\cdots&0&(t-\theta)/\kappa_{r}\\
1&\cdots&0&-\kappa_{1}/\kappa_{r}\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots\\
0&\cdots&1&-\kappa_{r-1}/\kappa_{r}\end{pmatrix},

and one finds that

(2.4.14)

\Upsilon\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\begin{pmatrix}g_{1}&g_{1}^{(1)}&\cdots&g_{1}^{(r-1)}\\
g_{2}&g_{2}^{(1)}&\cdots&g_{2}^{(r-1)}\\
\vdots&\vdots&&\vdots\\
g_{r}&g_{r}^{(1)}&\cdots&g_{r}^{(r-1)}\end{pmatrix}\in\GL_{r}(\mathbb{T}_{t})

satisfies
\Upsilon^{(1)}=\Upsilon\Theta
. Thus
\Upsilon
is a rigid analytic trivialization for
\mathcal{M}_{\phi}
, originally determined by Pellarin
[
60
]
*§4.2 (see also
[
42
]
*§2.6 for rank
2
). That
\Upsilon
is invertible in
\Mat_{r}(\mathbb{T}_{t})
takes some effort, but see
[
20
]
*§2.5
[
33
]
*Prop. 6.2.4
[
60
]
*§4.2.3 for more details. From the theory of Anderson generating functions, one knows that each
g_{i}
has a meromorphic continuation to
\mathbb{C}_{\infty}
, with simple poles at
t=\theta
,
\theta^{q}
,
\theta^{q^{2}},\ldots
. We find that

\Res_{t=\theta}g_{i}=(t-\theta)g_{i}|_{t=\theta}=-\pi_{i},\quad g_{i}^{(j)}(\theta)=\eta_{ij},\quad 1\leqslant i\leqslant r,\ 1\leqslant j\leqslant r-1,

where
\eta_{ij}
is a quasi-period for
\phi
. See
[
20
]
*§2.5
[
21
]
*§3.4
[
54
]
*Ex. 4.6.7
[
60
]
*§4.2. Anderson generating functions originally appeared in
[
4
]
*§3.2, and they have been studied extensively for Drinfeld modules in
[
3
,
20
,
21
,
27
,
42
,
46
,
49
,
50
,
60
,
61
]
.
About our notation, our use of
\Upsilon
in (
2.4.14
) coincides with that in
[
20
,
21
,
33
]
, but would be
\Upsilon^{{\mathsf{T}}}
in
[
49
]
;
(\Upsilon^{{\mathsf{T}}})^{(-1)}
in
[
54
]
*Eq. (4.3.7); and
\widehat{\Psi}^{{\mathsf{T}}}
in
[
60
]
*§4.2.

2.5.
Schur polynomials

We review properties of symmetric polynomials and especially Schur polynomials. For more details on symmetric polynomials see
[
1
]
*Ch. 8
[
64
]
*Ch. 7.
Letting
\mathbf{x}=\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\}
be independent variables, the
elementary symmetric polynomials

\{e_{i}\}_{i=0}^{n}=\{e_{n,i}\}_{i=0}^{n}\subseteq\mathbb{Z}[\mathbf{x}]
are defined by

(2.5.1)

\sum_{i=0}^{n}e_{i}(\mathbf{x})T^{i}=(1+x_{1}T)(1+x_{2}T)\cdots(1+x_{n}T).

We adopt the convention that
e_{i}=0
if
i<0
or
i>n
.
The
complete homogeneous symmetric polynomials

\{h_{i}\}_{i\geqslant 0}=\{h_{n,i}\}_{i\geqslant 0}\subseteq\mathbb{Z}[x_{1},\dots,x_{n}]
are defined by

(2.5.2)

\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}h_{i}(\mathbf{x})T^{i}=\frac{1}{(1-x_{1}T)(1-x_{2}T)\cdots(1-x_{n}T)},

and similarly if
i<0
then we take
h_{i}=0
. Then
h_{i}
consists of the sum of all monomials in
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}
of degree
i
. The
Vandermonde determinant
is

(2.5.3)

V(\mathbf{x})=\prod_{1\leqslant i<j\leqslant n}(x_{i}-x_{j}).

When nonzero we have
\deg e_{i}=i
and
\deg h_{i}=i
, and
\deg V=\binom{n}{2}
.

Definition 2.5.4
.

For polynomials
P(T)=(T-x_{1})\cdots(T-x_{k})
and
Q(T)=(T-y_{1})\cdots(T-y_{\ell})
, we set

(P\otimes Q)(T)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leqslant i\leqslant k\\
1\leqslant j\leqslant\ell\end{subarray}}(T-x_{i}y_{j}).

Letting
B_{m}
be the coefficient of
T^{m}
in
(P\otimes Q)(T)
, we find that
B_{m}
is symmetric in both
x_{1},\dots,x_{k}
and
y_{1},\dots,y_{\ell}
, its total degree in
x_{1},\dots,x_{k}
is
k\ell-m
, and its total degree in
y_{1},\dots,y_{\ell}
is also
k\ell-m
. As such,

B_{m}\in\mathbb{Z}[e_{k,1}(\mathbf{x}),\ldots,e_{k,k\ell-m}(\mathbf{x});e_{\ell,1}(\mathbf{y}),\ldots,e_{\ell,k\ell-m}(\mathbf{y})]
.
The coefficients of
(P\otimes Q)(T)
and its inverse
(P\otimes Q)(T)^{-1}
are expressible in terms of Schur polynomials (see Theorem
2.5.13
and Corollary
2.5.14
for
(P\otimes Q)(T)^{-1}
).

2.5.5.
Schur polynomials

Let
\lambda
denote an integer partition
\lambda_{1}\geqslant\cdots\geqslant\lambda_{n}\geqslant 0
of length
n
, where
\lambda_{i}=0
is allowed. We set

(2.5.6)

s_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x})=s_{\lambda_{1}\cdots\lambda_{n}}(\mathbf{x})\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=V(\mathbf{x})^{-1}\cdot\det\begin{pmatrix}x_{1}^{\lambda_{1}+n-1}&\cdots&x_{n}^{\lambda_{n}+n-1}\\
\vdots&&\vdots\\
x_{1}^{\lambda_{n-1}+1}&\cdots&x_{n}^{\lambda_{n-1}+1}\\
x_{1}^{\lambda_{n}}&\cdots&x_{n}^{\lambda_{n}}\end{pmatrix}

We have the following properties (see
[
1
]
*§8.3
[
64
]
*§7.15).

•

s_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x})
is a symmetric polynomial in
\mathbb{Z}[x_{1},\dots,x_{n}]
.

•

\deg s_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x})=\lambda_{1}+\dots+\lambda_{n}
.

•

For
0\leqslant i\leqslant n
we have
s_{\underbrace{\scriptstyle 1\,\,\cdots\,\,1}_{i}\underbrace{\scriptstyle 0\,\,\cdots\,\,0}_{n-i}}(\mathbf{x})=e_{i}(\mathbf{x})
.

•

For
i\geqslant 0
we have
s_{i\underbrace{\scriptstyle 0\,\,\cdots\,\,0}_{n-1}}(\mathbf{x})=h_{i}(\mathbf{x})
.

The polynomial
s_{\lambda}
is called the
Schur polynomial for
\lambda
. Following the exposition of Bump and Goldfeld
[
16
,
38
]
, when
n\geqslant 2
(which we now assume), we consider the subset of Schur polynomials where
\lambda_{n}=0
as follows. For integers
k_{1},\dots,k_{n-1}\geqslant 0
, form

\lambda:k_{1}+\cdots+k_{n-1}\geqslant k_{2}+\cdots+k_{n-1}\geqslant\cdots\geqslant k_{n-1}\geqslant 0\geqslant 0.

We set
S_{k_{1},\dots,k_{n-1}}(\mathbf{x})
to be the the Schur polynomial
s_{\lambda}
, i.e.,

(2.5.7)

S_{k_{1},\dots,k_{n-1}}(\mathbf{x})\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=V(\mathbf{x})^{-1}\cdot\det\begin{pmatrix}x_{1}^{k_{1}+\cdots+k_{n-1}+n-1}&\cdots&x_{n}^{k_{1}+\cdots+k_{n-1}+n-1}\\
x_{1}^{k_{2}+\cdots+k_{n-1}+n-2}&\cdots&x_{n}^{k_{2}+\cdots+k_{n-1}+n-2}\\
\vdots&&\vdots\\
x_{1}^{k_{n-1}+1}&\cdots&x_{n}^{k_{n-1}+1}\\
1&\cdots&1\end{pmatrix}.

The degree of
S_{k_{1},\dots,k_{n-1}}(\mathbf{x})
is
k_{1}+2k_{2}+\cdots+(n-1)k_{n-1}
.

Lemma 2.5.8
.

Let
\lambda:\lambda_{1}\geqslant\cdots\geqslant\lambda_{n}\geqslant 0
be an integer partition. Then

s_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x})=(x_{1}\cdots x_{n})^{\lambda_{n}}\cdot S_{\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2},\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{3},\ldots,\lambda_{n-1}-\lambda_{n}}(\mathbf{x}).

Proof.

By pulling
x_{i}^{\lambda_{n}}
out of the
i
-th column in (
2.5.6
) we see that
s_{\lambda}=(x_{1}\cdots x_{n})^{\lambda_{n}}\cdot s_{\lambda^{\prime}}
, where
\lambda^{\prime}
is the partition
\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{n}\geqslant\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{n}\geqslant\cdots\geqslant\lambda_{n-1}-\lambda_{n}\geqslant 0\geqslant 0
. The identity then follows from (
2.5.7
).
∎

As a result, we see from the properties of
s_{\lambda}
above that

(2.5.9)

\displaystyle S_{\underbrace{\scriptstyle 0,\,\ldots\,,0,1,0,\,\ldots\,,0}_{\textup{$i$-th place}}}(\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle=e_{i}(\mathbf{x}),\qquad

\displaystyle 1\leqslant i\leqslant n-1,

(2.5.10)

\displaystyle S_{i,\underbrace{\scriptstyle 0,\,\cdots\,,0}_{n-2}}(\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle=h_{i}(\mathbf{x}),\qquad

\displaystyle i\geqslant 0.

Lemma 2.5.11
.

For
k_{1},\dots,k_{n-1}\geqslant 0
, we have

(x_{1}\cdots x_{n})^{k_{1}+\dots+k_{n-1}}\cdot S_{k_{1},\dots,k_{n-1}}\bigl(x_{1}^{-1},\dots,x_{n}^{-1}\bigr)=S_{k_{n-1},\dots,k_{1}}(\mathbf{x}).

Proof.

Substituting
x_{1}\leftarrow x_{1}^{-1},\dots,x_{n}\leftarrow x_{n}^{-1}
into (
2.5.7
) and using the multilinearity of the determinant, we have

\begin{aligned} (x_{1}\cdots x_{n}&)^{k_{1}+\dots+k_{n-1}}\\
&{}\cdot S_{k_{1},\dots,k_{n-1}}\bigl(x_{1}^{-1},\dots,x_{n}^{-1}\bigr)\end{aligned}=\frac{\det\begin{pmatrix}1&\cdots&1\\
x_{1}^{k_{1}+1}&\cdots&x_{n}^{k_{1}+1}\\
\vdots&&\vdots\\
x_{1}^{k_{1}+\cdots+k_{n-2}+n-2}&\cdots&x_{n}^{k_{1}+\cdots+k_{n-2}+n-2}\\
x_{1}^{k_{1}+\cdots+k_{n-1}+n-1}&\cdots&x_{n}^{k_{1}+\cdots+k_{n-1}+n-1}\end{pmatrix}}{\displaystyle(x_{1}\cdots x_{n})^{n-1}\prod_{1\leqslant i<j\leqslant n}\bigl(x_{i}^{-1}-x_{j}^{-1}\bigr)}.

By rearranging the rows, we check that the numerator on the right is
(-1)^{\binom{n}{2}}
times the determinant in (
2.5.7
) with
(k_{1},\dots,k_{n-1})\leftarrow(k_{n-1},\dots,k_{1})
. Likewise the denominator is
(-1)^{\binom{n}{2}}
times the
V(\mathbf{x})
term of (
2.5.7
).
∎

2.5.12.
Cauchy’s identities

In order to work with
(P\otimes Q)(T)^{-1}
, which arises as part of the Euler product for one of the main
L
-series
L(\mathbb{E}(\phi\times\psi)^{\vee},s)
we consider in this paper (see §
5
–
6
), we use the following identities expressed in terms of Schur polynomials.

Theorem 2.5.13

(Cauchy’s Identity, see
[
1
]
*Cor. 8.16
[
16
]
*§2.2
[
64
]
*Thm. 7.12.1)
.

For variables
\mathbf{x}=\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\}
and
\mathbf{y}=\{y_{1},\dots,y_{n}\}
, let
X=x_{1}\cdots x_{n}
and
Y=y_{1}\cdots y_{n}
. Then as power series in
{\mathbb{Z}[\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}][\![T]\!]}
,

\prod_{1\leqslant i,j\leqslant n}(1-x_{i}y_{j}T)^{-1}=(1-XYT^{n})^{-1}\underset{k=(k_{1},\dots,k_{n-1})}{\sum_{k_{1}=0}^{\infty}\cdots\sum_{k_{n-1}=0}^{\infty}}S_{k}(\mathbf{x})S_{k}(\mathbf{y})T^{k_{1}+2k_{2}+\cdots+(n-1)k_{n-1}}.

If instead we have
\mathbf{x}=\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\}
and
\mathbf{y}=\{y_{1},\dots,y_{\ell}\}
with
n<\ell
, then Cauchy’s identity reduces to the following result by setting
x_{n+1}=\cdots=x_{\ell}=0
and simplifying.

Corollary 2.5.14

(Bump
[
16
]
*§2.2)
.

For variables
\mathbf{x}=\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\}
and
\mathbf{y}=\{y_{1},\dots,y_{\ell}\}
with
n<\ell
, let
X=x_{1}\cdots x_{n}
. Then as power series in
{\mathbb{Z}[\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}][\![T]\!]}
,

\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leqslant i\leqslant n\\
1\leqslant j\leqslant\ell\end{subarray}}(1-x_{i}y_{j}T)^{-1}=\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}k=(k_{1},\dots,k_{n-1})\\
k^{\prime}=(k_{1},\dots,k_{n},0\dots,0)\end{subarray}}{\sum_{k_{1}=0}^{\infty}\cdots\sum_{k_{n}=0}^{\infty}}S_{k}(\mathbf{x})S_{k^{\prime}}(\mathbf{y})X^{k_{n}}T^{k_{1}+2k_{2}+\cdots+nk_{n}}.

2.5.15.
Pieri’s rules

In general products of Schur polynomials can be expressed as linear combinations of Schur polynomials using the Littlewood-Richardson rule
[
64
]
*§A1.3. Pieri’s rule and its dual are special cases, which we state here in terms of the polynomials
S_{k_{1},\dots,k_{n-1}}(\mathbf{x})
. For
k
,
k_{1},\dots,k_{n-1}\geqslant 0
,
Pieri’s rule

[
64
]
*Thm. 7.15.7 is

(2.5.16)

h_{k}(\mathbf{x})\cdot S_{k_{1},\dots,k_{n-1}}(\mathbf{x})\\
=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m_{0}+\cdots+m_{n-1}=k\\
m_{1}\leqslant k_{1},\,\ldots,\,m_{n-1}\leqslant k_{n-1}\end{subarray}}S_{k_{1}+m_{0}-m_{1},\,k_{2}+m_{1}-m_{2},\,\ldots,\,k_{n-1}+m_{n-2}-m_{n-1}}(\mathbf{x})X^{m_{n-1}},

where
X=x_{1}\cdots x_{n}
. For the
dual Pieri rule

[
64
]
*p. 340, we define for
k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}\geqslant 0
,

\mathcal{I}_{k_{1},\dots,k_{n-1}}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\Bigl\{(m_{0},\dots,m_{n-1})\in\{0,1\}^{n}\Bigm|k_{j}=0\Rightarrow\bigl(m_{j}=1\Rightarrow m_{j-1}=1\bigr)\Bigr\}.

Then for
0\leqslant k\leqslant n-1
,

(2.5.17)

e_{k}(\mathbf{x})\cdot S_{k_{1},\dots,k_{n-1}}(\mathbf{x})\\
=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m_{0}+\cdots+m_{n-1}=k\\
(m_{0},\ldots,m_{n-1})\,\in\,\mathcal{I}_{k_{1},\dots,k_{n-1}}\end{subarray}}S_{k_{1}+m_{0}-m_{1},\,k_{2}+m_{1}-m_{2},\,\ldots,\,k_{n-1}+m_{n-2}-m_{n-1}}(\mathbf{x})X^{m_{n-1}}.

2.5.18.
Jacobi-Trudi identity

We can also express Schur polynomials in terms of complete homogeneous symmetric polynomials by the
Jacobi-Trudi identity

[
1
]
*Thm. 8.7
[
64
]
*Thm. 7.16.1.
We state it here for
S_{k_{1},\dots,k_{n-1}}(\mathbf{x})
. For
k_{1},\dots,k_{n-1}\geqslant 0
, we have

(2.5.19)

S_{k_{1},\dots,k_{n-1}}(\mathbf{x})=\det\Bigl(h_{k_{i}+\cdots+k_{n-1}-i+j}(\mathbf{x})\Bigr)_{i,j=1}^{n-1},

where
