\displaystyle=\theta+\kappa_{1}\tau+\cdots+\kappa_{r}\tau^{r},\quad

\displaystyle\kappa_{i}\in A,\ \kappa_{r}\in\mathbb{F}_{q}^{\times},

(4.1.2)

\displaystyle\psi_{t}

\displaystyle=\theta+\eta_{1}\tau+\cdots+\eta_{\ell}\tau^{\ell},\quad

\displaystyle\eta_{i}\in A,\ \eta_{\ell}\in\mathbb{F}_{q}^{\times}.

We let
\pi_{1},\dots,\pi_{r}\in\Lambda_{\phi}
and
\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{\ell}\in\Lambda_{\psi}
be
A
-bases of their respective period lattices. As in (
2.4.5
), (
2.4.13
), and (
2.4.14
), we construct

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\phi}

\displaystyle\in\Mat_{r}(A[t]),\quad

\displaystyle\Theta_{\phi}=\Gamma_{\phi}^{{\mathsf{T}}}

\displaystyle\in\Mat_{r}(A[t]),\quad

\displaystyle\Upsilon_{\phi}\in\GL_{r}(\mathbb{T}_{t}),

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\psi}

\displaystyle\in\Mat_{\ell}(A[t]),\quad

\displaystyle\Theta_{\psi}=\Gamma_{\psi}^{{\mathsf{T}}}

\displaystyle\in\Mat_{\ell}(A[t]),\quad

\displaystyle\Upsilon_{\psi}\in\GL_{\ell}(\mathbb{T}_{t}).

We set

(4.1.3)

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\psi,z}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\Gamma_{\psi}|_{t=z}\in\Mat_{\ell}(A[z]),\quad\Theta_{\psi,z}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\Theta_{\psi}|_{t=z}\in\Mat_{\ell}(A[z]),

\displaystyle\Upsilon_{\psi,z}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\Upsilon_{\psi}|_{t=z}\in\GL_{\ell}(\mathbb{T}_{z}).

We note that the identity
\Upsilon_{\psi,z}^{(1)}=\Upsilon_{\psi,z}\Theta_{\psi,z}
implies

(4.1.4)

\Upsilon_{\psi,z}^{-1}\Upsilon_{\psi,z}^{(j)}=\Theta_{\psi,z}\Theta_{\psi,z}^{(1)}\cdots\Theta_{\psi,z}^{(j-1)}\in\Mat_{\ell}(A[z]),\quad j\geqslant 0,

where we use the convention that the empty product is the identity matrix.
We define
\phi^{\oplus\ell}:\mathbf{A}\to\Mat_{\ell}(\mathbb{A}[\tau])
to be the
\ell
-fold direct sum of
\phi
, which we consider to be a constant
t
-module defined over
\mathbb{A}
. We then conjugate
\phi^{\oplus\ell}
by
\Upsilon_{\psi,z}
to form the
t
-module

(4.1.5)

\mathbb{E}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathbb{E}(\phi\times\psi):\mathbf{A}\to\Mat_{\ell}(\mathbb{A}[\tau])

such that

(4.1.6)

\mathbb{E}_{t}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\Upsilon_{\psi,z}^{-1}\cdot\phi^{\oplus\ell}_{t}\cdot\Upsilon_{\psi,z}=\theta\mathrm{I}_{\ell}+\kappa_{1}\Theta_{\psi,z}\tau+\cdots+\kappa_{r}\Theta_{\psi,z}\Theta_{\psi,z}^{(1)}\cdots\Theta_{\psi,z}^{(r-1)}\tau^{r}.

We consider
\mathbb{E}
to be
t
-module over
\widehat{\mathbb{L}}_{z}
as in §
3.4
, and moreover,
\Upsilon_{\psi,z}:\mathbb{E}\to\phi^{\oplus\ell}
is a
t
-module isomorphism. We recount some fundamental properties below.

Remark 4.1.7
.

We note that
\mathbb{E}(\phi\times\psi)
is different from
\mathbb{E}(\psi\times\phi)
. Indeed the former has dimension
\ell
and the latter
r
, and generally they are not isomorphic when
\phi\not\cong\psi
.

4.1.8.
Abelianness and
\mathbf{A}
-finiteness

The Drinfeld module
\phi
is abelian and
\mathsf{A}
-finite, and naturally its extension to a Drinfeld module over
\widehat{\mathbb{L}}_{z}
is also abelian and
\mathbf{A}
-finite. By taking direct sums
\phi^{\oplus\ell}
is also abelian and
\mathbf{A}
-finite. Since
\mathbb{E}_{t}
is isomorphic to
\phi^{\oplus\ell}
over
\widehat{\mathbb{L}}_{z}
, it follows that
\mathbb{E}_{t}
is also abelian and
\mathbf{A}
-finite.

Alternatively, since
\Theta_{\psi,z}\in\GL_{r}(\widehat{\mathbb{L}}_{z})
, it follows that
\mathbb{E}_{t}
is strictly pure in the sense of
[
54
]
*Ex. 3.5.17, Ex. 4.6.19 and is abelian and
\mathbf{A}
-finite through the discussions there (see also
[
47
]
*§2.5.2, p. 112). There we see that if
\mathbf{s}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{s}_{\ell}
denote the standard basis vectors of
\Mat_{1\times\ell}(\widehat{\mathbb{L}}_{z})
, then

\displaystyle\{\tau^{j}\mathbf{s}_{i}:1\leqslant i\leqslant\ell,\ 0\leqslant j\leqslant r-1\}\subseteq\Mat_{1\times\ell}(\widehat{\mathbb{L}}_{z}[\tau])=\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{E}}(\widehat{\mathbb{L}}_{z}),

\displaystyle\{\sigma^{j}\mathbf{s}_{i}:1\leqslant i\leqslant\ell,\ 0\leqslant j\leqslant r-1\}\subseteq\Mat_{1\times\ell}(\widehat{\mathbb{L}}_{z}[\sigma])=\mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{E}}(\widehat{\mathbb{L}}_{z}),

are
\widehat{\mathbb{L}}_{z}[t]
-bases of
\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{E}}(\widehat{\mathbb{L}}_{z})
and
\mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{E}}(\widehat{\mathbb{L}}_{z})
.
We note that
\mathbb{E}
has dimension
\ell
and rank
r\ell
.

4.1.9.
Exponentials, logarithms, and period lattices

Suppose that
\Exp_{\phi}=\sum_{i\geqslant 0}B_{i}\tau^{i}
,
\Log_{\phi}=\sum_{i\geqslant 0}C_{i}\tau^{i}\in{K[\![\tau]\!]}
are the exponential and logarithm series of
\phi
. Then we have

(4.1.10)

\displaystyle\Exp_{\mathbb{E}}

\displaystyle=\Upsilon_{\psi,z}^{-1}\cdot\Exp_{\phi^{\oplus\ell}}\cdot\Upsilon_{\psi,z}=\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}B_{i}\Theta_{\psi,z}\Theta_{\psi,z}^{(1)}\cdots\Theta_{\psi,z}^{(i-1)}\tau^{i},

\displaystyle\Log_{\mathbb{E}}

\displaystyle=\Upsilon_{\psi,z}^{-1}\cdot\Log_{\phi^{\oplus\ell}}\cdot\Upsilon_{\psi,z}=\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}C_{i}\Theta_{\psi,z}\Theta_{\psi,z}^{(1)}\cdots\Theta_{\psi,z}^{(i-1)}\tau^{i},

both of which are in
{\Mat_{\ell}(K[z])[\![\tau]\!]}
.

We let
\Lambda_{\phi}\subseteq\widehat{\mathbb{L}}_{z}
be the kernel of
\Exp_{\phi}:\widehat{\mathbb{L}}_{z}\to\widehat{\mathbb{L}}_{z}
, and we fix generators
\pi_{1},\dots,\pi_{r}\in\mathbb{C}_{\infty}
so that
\Lambda_{\phi}=\mathbb{A}\pi_{1}+\cdots+\mathbb{A}\pi_{r}
as in Theorem
3.4.9
(c). Because
\phi^{\oplus\ell}
is simply a direct sum, the following properties then follow from Theorem
3.4.9
.

•

\Exp_{\phi^{\oplus\ell}}:\widehat{\mathbb{L}}_{z}^{\ell}\to\widehat{\mathbb{L}}_{z}^{\ell}
is surjective.

•

\Lambda_{\phi^{\oplus\ell}}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\ker\Exp_{\phi^{\oplus\ell}}\subseteq\widehat{\mathbb{L}}_{z}^{\ell}
is free of rank
r\ell
over
\mathbb{A}
, and
\Lambda_{\phi^{\oplus\ell}}=\Lambda_{\phi}^{\oplus\ell}
.

From (
4.1.10
) the following also hold.

•

\Exp_{\mathbb{E}}:\widehat{\mathbb{L}}_{z}^{\ell}\to\widehat{\mathbb{L}}_{z}^{\ell}
is surjective.

•

\Lambda_{\mathbb{E}}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\ker\Exp_{\mathbb{E}}\subseteq\widehat{\mathbb{L}}_{z}^{\ell}
satisfies

(4.1.11)

\Lambda_{\mathbb{E}}=\Upsilon_{\psi,z}^{-1}\,\Lambda_{\phi}^{\oplus\ell}.

Notably we see that
\mathbb{E}
is uniformizable, and for
\nu\in\mathsf{A}
,
\nu\neq 0
, we have isomorphisms of
\mathbf{A}
-modules,

(4.1.12)

\mathbb{E}[\nu]\cong\Lambda_{\mathbb{E}}/\nu\Lambda_{\mathbb{E}}\cong\bigl(\mathbf{A}/\nu\mathbf{A}\bigr)^{r\ell},

where
\mathbf{A}
operates on
\Lambda_{\mathbb{E}}\subseteq\Lie(\mathbb{E})(\widehat{\mathbb{L}}_{z})
through scalar multiplication by
\mathbb{A}
. In particular, by Corollary
3.3.8
,
\mathbb{E}[\nu]
has full dimension. Furthermore,

(4.1.13)

\mathbb{E}[\nu]=\Upsilon_{\psi,z}^{-1}\cdot\phi^{\oplus\ell}[\nu],

which also makes these isomorphisms explicit.

4.1.14.
Adjoint of
\mathbb{E}

The adjoint
\mathbb{E}^{*}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathbb{E}^{*}(\phi\times\psi):\mathbf{A}\to\Mat_{\ell}(\mathbb{H}[\tau])
of
\mathbb{E}
is defined over
\mathbb{H}=K^{\mathrm{perf}}(z)
, where
K^{\mathrm{perf}}
is the perfection of
K
. By (
4.1.6
),

(4.1.15)

\mathbb{E}^{*}_{t}=\Upsilon_{\psi,z}^{{\mathsf{T}}}\cdot\smash{\bigl(\phi_{t}^{*}\bigr)}^{\oplus\ell}\cdot\bigl(\Upsilon_{\psi,z}^{{\mathsf{T}}}\bigr)^{-1}.

Now for
\nu\in\mathsf{A}
,
\nu\neq 0
, we have that
\phi^{*}[\nu]\cong(\mathsf{A}/\nu\mathsf{A})^{r}
(see
[
44
]
*§4.14), and so it follows from the considerations in §
4.1.9
that over
\widehat{\mathbb{L}}_{z}
we have
(\phi^{\oplus\ell})^{*}[\nu]\cong(\mathbf{A}/\nu\mathbf{A})^{r\ell}
. Thus by Corollary
3.3.8
,
(\phi^{\oplus\ell})^{*}[\nu]
has full dimension. Through the isomorphism
\Upsilon_{\psi,z}:\mathbb{E}\to\phi^{\oplus\ell}
, it follows that
\mathbb{E}^{*}[\nu]
has full dimension. Furthermore, to make things explicit,

\mathbb{E}^{*}[\nu]=\Upsilon_{\psi,z}^{{\mathsf{T}}}\cdot\bigl(\phi^{\oplus\ell}\bigr){}^{*}[\nu].

4.1.16.
Characteristic polynomials of Frobenius

Let
f\in A_{+}
be irreducible of degree
d
, and let
\lambda\in\mathsf{A}_{+}
be irreducible so that
\lambda(\theta)\neq f
. Let

\rho_{\phi,\lambda}:\Gal(K^{\mathrm{sep}}/K)\to\Aut(T_{\lambda}(\phi))\cong\GL_{r}(\mathsf{A}_{\lambda})

be the Galois representation associated
T_{\lambda}(\phi)
, and similarly define
\rho_{\psi,\lambda}:\Gal(K^{\mathrm{sep}}/K)\to\Aut(T_{\lambda}(\psi))
for
\psi
. As outlined in §
2.3
, if
\alpha_{f}\in\Gal(K^{\mathrm{sep}}/K)
is a Frobenius element for
f
, then because
\phi
and
\psi
have good reduction at
f
,

\Char(\alpha_{f},T_{\lambda}(\phi),X)|_{t=\theta}=P_{\phi,f}(X),\quad\Char(\alpha_{f},T_{\lambda}(\psi),X)|_{t=\theta}=P_{\psi,f}(X),

both of which lie in
A[X]
. Now
\lambda
is also irreducible in
\mathbf{A}=\mathbb{F}_{q}(z)[t]
, and from (
4.1.12
),

T_{\lambda}(\mathbb{E})=\varprojlim\mathbb{E}[\lambda^{m}]\cong\mathbf{A}_{\lambda}^{r\ell},

which induces another Galois representation

\rho_{\mathbb{E},\lambda}:\Gal(K^{\mathrm{sep}}/K)\to\Aut(T_{\lambda}(\mathbb{E}))\cong\GL_{r\ell}(\mathbf{A}_{\lambda}).

We have the following result that will be fundamental for analyzing
L(\mathbb{E}^{\vee},0)
in §
5
–§
6
. Recall the notation
(P\otimes Q)(X)
from Definition
2.5.4
.

Proposition 4.1.17
.

Let
f\in A_{+}
be irreducible,
f\neq\theta
, and let
\lambda\in\mathsf{A}_{+}
be irreducible so that
\lambda(\theta)\neq f
. For the
t
-module
\mathbb{E}=\mathbb{E}(\phi\times\psi)
, let

\displaystyle P_{\phi,f}(X)

\displaystyle=\Char(\tau^{d},T_{\lambda}(\mkern 2.5mu\overline{\mkern-2.5mu\phi}),X)|_{t=\theta}\in A[X],

\displaystyle P_{\psi,f(z)}^{\vee}(X)

\displaystyle=\Char(\tau^{d},T_{\lambda}(\mkern 2.5mu\overline{\mkern-2.5mu\psi})^{\vee},X)|_{t=z}\in\mathbb{F}_{q}(z)[X],

as in (
2.3.7
). Then

\Char(\alpha_{f},T_{\lambda}(\mathbb{E}(\phi\times\psi)),X)|_{t=\theta}=\bigl(P_{\phi,f}\otimes P_{\psi,f(z)}^{\vee}\bigr)(X)\in\mathbb{A}[X].

Proof.

By (
4.1.13
), for
\alpha\in\Gal(K^{\mathrm{sep}}/K)
and
(\mathbf{v}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{v}_{\ell})^{{\mathsf{T}}}\in T_{\lambda}(\phi^{\oplus\ell})
, we have

(4.1.18)

\alpha\left(\Upsilon_{\psi,z}^{-1}\cdot\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{v}_{1}\\
\vdots\\
\mathbf{v}_{\ell}\end{pmatrix}\right)=\alpha(\Upsilon_{\psi,z})^{-1}\cdot\begin{pmatrix}\alpha(\mathbf{v}_{1})\\
\vdots\\
\alpha(\mathbf{v}_{\ell})\end{pmatrix}.

As in §
2.3
, the characteristic polynomial of
\alpha_{f}
acting on
T_{\lambda}(\phi)
coincides with
P_{\phi,f}(X)
and is independent of the choice of
\lambda
, so we can take
\lambda=t
. If we write
\rho_{\psi,z}(\alpha)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\rho_{\psi,t}(\alpha)|_{t=z}\in\GL_{\ell}({\mathbb{F}_{q}[\![z]\!]})
, then we see from
[
21
]
*Cor. 3.2.4 that

\alpha(\Upsilon_{\psi,z})=\rho_{\psi,z}(\alpha)\cdot\Upsilon_{\psi,z},

having chosen a basis of
T_{t}(\psi)
appropriately as in
[
21
]
*§3.2. Continuing with (
4.1.18
),

\rho_{\mathbb{E},t}(\alpha)=\left(\begin{array}[]{c|c|c}&&\\[-10.0pt]
(\rho_{\psi,z}(\alpha)^{-1})_{11}\cdot\rho_{\phi,t}(\alpha)&\phantom{\cdots}\cdots&(\rho_{\psi,z}(\alpha)^{-1})_{1\ell}\cdot\rho_{\phi,t}(\alpha)\\[5.0pt]
\hline\cr&&\\[-10.0pt]
\vdots&&\vdots\\[5.0pt]
\hline\cr&&\\[-5.0pt]
(\rho_{\psi,z}(\alpha)^{-1})_{\ell 1}\cdot\rho_{\phi,t}(\alpha)&\cdots&(\rho_{\psi,z}(\alpha)^{-1})_{\ell\ell}\cdot\rho_{\phi,t}(\alpha)\end{array}\right),

which we take in
\GL_{r\ell}({\mathbb{F}_{q}[\![t,z]\!]})
. Thus we have an isomorphism,

\rho_{\mathbb{E},t}\cong\rho_{\psi,z}^{-1}\otimes\rho_{\phi,t},

of representations over
{\mathbb{F}_{q}[\![t,z]\!]}
. From this we see that

\displaystyle\Char(\alpha_{f},T_{t}(\mathbb{E}),X)

\displaystyle=\Char(\alpha_{f},T_{t}(\psi)^{\vee},X)|_{t=z}\otimes\Char(\alpha_{f},T_{t}(\phi),X)

\displaystyle=P_{\psi,f(z)}^{\vee}(X)\otimes P_{\phi,f}(X).\qed

Definition 4.1.19
.

For
f\in A_{+}
irreducible (including
f=\theta
) and
\mathbb{E}=\mathbb{E}(\phi\times\psi)
as above, Proposition
4.1.17
prompts us to define

\mathbf{P}_{f}(X)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\bigl(P_{\phi,f}\otimes P_{\psi,f(z)}^{\vee}\bigr)(X)\in\mathbb{A}[X],\quad\mathbf{P}_{f}^{\vee}(X)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\bigl(P_{\phi,f}^{\vee}\otimes P_{\psi,f(z)}\bigr)(X)\in K[z][X].

If we need to emphasize the dependence on
\phi
and
\psi
, we will write
\mathbf{P}_{\phi\times\psi,f}(X)=\mathbf{P}_{f}(X)
.
We will see in §
4.2
that, for
f\neq\theta
,
\mathbf{P}_{f}(X)
coincides with the characteristic polynomial of
\tau^{d}
acting on the
\lambda
-adic Tate module of
\mathbb{E}
modulo
f
, and
\mathbf{P}_{f}^{\vee}(X)
arises from
\tau^{d}
acting on the dual. When
f=\theta
, its role in determining the
\mathbb{A}
-order of
\mathbb{E}(\mathbb{F}_{\theta}(z))
is checked directly (see Theorem
4.2.2
). We do gain a connection between
\mathbb{E}(\phi\times\psi)
and
\mathbb{E}(\psi\times\phi)
, in that

(4.1.20)

\mathbf{P}_{\psi\times\phi,f}(X)=\mathbf{P}_{\phi\times\psi,f}^{\vee}(X)\big|_{z\leftrightarrow\theta}\,,\quad\mathbf{P}_{\psi\times\phi,f}^{\vee}(X)=\mathbf{P}_{\phi\times\psi,f}(X)\big|_{z\leftrightarrow\theta}\,,

where “
z\leftrightarrow\theta
” indicates that the roles of
z
and
\theta
have been swapped.

Example 4.1.21
.

Twisting a Drinfeld module by the Carlitz module
. Let
\mathbb{E}=\mathbb{E}(\mathsf{C}\times\mathsf{C})
, where
\mathsf{C}
is the Carlitz module. Then
\mathbb{E}
has dimension
1
and rank
1
, and by (
4.1.6
),

\mathbb{E}_{t}=\omega_{z}^{-1}\cdot\mathsf{C}_{t}\cdot\omega_{z}=\theta+(z-\theta)\tau.

Thus
\mathbb{E}
was studied extensively by Anglès, Pellarin, and Tavares Ribeiro
[
11
,
8
]
, as well as its multivariable versions. In particular,
\mathbb{E}[\nu]=\omega_{z}^{-1}\mathsf{C}[\nu]
for each
\nu\in\mathsf{A}
. We know that
P_{\mathsf{C},f}(X)=X-f
(e.g., see
[
31
]
*Ex. 5.11), and so by Proposition
4.1.17
,

(4.1.22)

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}_{\mathsf{C}\times\mathsf{C},f}(X)

\displaystyle=(X-f)\otimes\biggl(X-\frac{1}{f(z)}\biggr)=X-\frac{f}{f(z)},

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}_{\mathsf{C}\times\mathsf{C},f}^{\vee}(X)

\displaystyle=\biggl(X-\frac{1}{f}\biggr)\otimes(X-f(z))=X-\frac{f(z)}{f}.

For
\phi
a Drinfeld module as in (
4.1.1
), we can also form
\mathbb{E}=\mathbb{E}(\phi\times\mathsf{C})
, given by

\mathbb{E}_{t}=\omega_{z}^{-1}\cdot\phi_{t}\cdot\omega_{z}=\theta+\kappa_{1}(z-\theta)\tau+\cdots+\kappa_{r}(z-\theta)\cdots(z-\theta^{q^{r-1}})\tau^{r}.

Then
\mathbb{E}
is a deformation of
\phi
defined by Anglès and Tavares Ribeiro
[
13
]
*§3 and also investigated by Gezmiş
[
35
]
. A short calculation shows

(4.1.23)

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}_{\phi\times\mathsf{C},f}(X)

\displaystyle=P_{\phi,f}(X)\otimes\biggl(X-\frac{1}{f(z)}\biggr)=\frac{P_{\phi,f}(f(z)X)}{f(z)^{r}},

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}_{\phi\times\mathsf{C},f}^{\vee}(X)

\displaystyle=P_{\phi,f}^{\vee}(X)\otimes(X-f(z))=f(z)^{r}P_{\phi,f}^{\vee}\biggl(\frac{X}{f(z)}\biggr).

Example 4.1.24
.

Twisting the Carlitz module by a Drinfeld module
. Letting
\phi:\mathsf{A}\to A[\tau]
be a Drinfeld module of rank
r
defined as in (
4.1.1
), we take
\mathbb{E}=\mathbb{E}(\mathsf{C}\times\phi)
, given by

\mathbb{E}_{t}=\Upsilon_{\phi,z}^{-1}\cdot\mathsf{C}_{t}\cdot\Upsilon_{\phi,z}=\theta\mathrm{I}_{r}+\Theta_{\phi,z}\tau^{r}\in\Mat_{r}(\mathbb{A}[\tau]).

It follows from (
4.1.20
) and (
4.1.23
) that

(4.1.25)

\mathbf{P}_{\mathsf{C}\times\phi,f}(X)=f^{r}P_{\phi,f(z)}^{\vee}\biggl(\frac{X}{f}\biggr),\quad\mathbf{P}_{\mathsf{C}\times\phi,f}^{\vee}(X)=\frac{P_{\phi,f(z)}(fX)}{f^{r}}.

4.2.
Reduction modulo
f
and
\mathbb{A}
-orders

We continue with the notation of the previous section, and in particular have Drinfeld modules
\phi
and
\psi
defined as in (
4.1.1
) and (
4.1.2
) and
\mathbb{E}=\mathbb{E}(\phi\times\psi)
as in (
4.1.6
). We fix
f\in A_{+}
irreducible of degree
d
. We let

\mkern 2.5mu\overline{\mkern-2.5mu\phi}:\mathsf{A}\to\mathbb{F}_{f}[\tau]
,
\mkern 2.5mu\overline{\mkern-2.5mu\psi}:\mathsf{A}\to\mathbb{F}_{f}[\tau]
,
denote the reductions modulo
f
. As both have everywhere good reduction, we see that
\mkern 2.5mu\overline{\mkern-2.5mu\phi}
has rank
r
and
\mkern 2.5mu\overline{\mkern-2.5mu\psi}
rank
\ell
. Likewise, the entries of coefficients of
\mathbb{E}_{t}
are all in
\mathbb{A}
, so we can form the reduction with entries in
\mathbb{A}/f\mathbb{A}\cong\mathbb{F}_{f}(z)
,

\overline{\mathbb{E}}:\mathbf{A}\to\Mat_{\ell}(\mathbb{F}_{f}(z)[\tau]).

In general “
\overline{\beta}
” will denote reduction of an element or object
\beta
modulo
f
, and so,

(4.2.1)

\overline{\mathbb{E}}_{t}=\mkern 2.5mu\overline{\mkern-2.5mu\theta}\mathrm{I}_{\ell}+\overline{\kappa}_{1}\overline{\Theta}_{\psi,z}\tau+\cdots+\overline{\kappa}_{r}\overline{\Theta}_{\psi,z}\overline{\Theta}_{\psi,z}^{(1)}\cdots\overline{\Theta}_{\psi,z}^{(r-1)}\tau^{r},

where
\overline{\kappa}_{r}=\kappa_{r}\in\mathbb{F}_{q}^{\times}
. We note from (
2.4.5
) that
\det\overline{\Theta}_{\psi,z}=(-1)^{\ell}(z-\mkern 2.5mu\overline{\mkern-2.5mu\theta})/\eta_{\ell}\neq 0
, and so
\overline{\Theta}_{\psi,z}\in\GL_{\ell}(\mathbb{F}_{f}(z))
. Thus as in §
4.1.8
we see that
\mathcal{M}_{\overline{\mathbb{E}}}(\mathbb{F}_{f}(z))
and
\mathcal{N}_{\overline{\mathbb{E}}}(\mathbb{F}_{f}(z))
both have rank
r\ell
as
\mathbb{F}_{f}(z)[t]
-modules. In this sense,
\mathbb{E}
also has everywhere good reduction.

Our main result in this section is the following theorem for determining
{[\overline{\mathbb{E}}(\mathbb{F}_{f}(z))]}_{\mathbb{A}}={[\overline{\mathbb{E}}(\mathbb{F}_{f}(z))]}_{\mathbf{A}}\big|_{t=\theta}
in terms of the value

\mathbf{P}_{f}(1)
, where
\mathbf{P}_{f}(X)\in\mathbb{A}[X]
is taken from Definition
4.1.19
. Define completely multiplicative functions
\chi_{\phi}
,
\chi_{\psi}:\mathsf{A}_{+}\to\mathbb{F}_{q}^{\times}
as in (
2.3.6
).

Theorem 4.2.2
.

Let
f\in\mathsf{A}_{+}
be irreducible. For
\overline{\mathbb{E}}:\mathbf{A}\to\Mat_{\ell}(\mathbb{F}_{f}(z)[\tau])
defined above,

{\left[\mkern 1.0mu\overline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathbb{E}}(\mathbb{F}_{f}(z))\right]}_{\mathbb{A}}=(-1)^{r\ell}\chi_{\phi}(f)^{\ell}\,\overline{\chi}_{\psi}(f)^{r}\cdot f(z)^{r}\cdot\mathbf{P}_{f}(1)=\frac{\mathbf{P}_{f}(1)}{\mathbf{P}_{f}(0)}\cdot f^{\ell}.

We recall that
\overline{\chi}_{\psi}
is the multiplicative inverse of
\chi_{\psi}
and
not
the reduction modulo
f
. This conflict of notation is isolated to the characters
\chi_{\phi}
and
\chi_{\psi}
and should not cause much confusion. The proof of this theorem takes the rest of the section and is split into the two cases where
f\neq\theta
and
f=\theta
.

Assume for the time being that
f\neq\theta
. Let
K_{f}^{\mathrm{nr}}
be the maximal unramified and separable extension of
K_{f}
, and let
K^{\mathrm{nr}}_{f}\supseteq O^{\mathrm{nr}}_{f}\supseteq M^{\mathrm{nr}}_{f}
be its subring of
f
-integral elements and its maximal ideal. Because
\phi
and
\psi
have everywhere good reduction, we see from
[
69
]
*Thm. 1 (see also
[
44
]
*Thm. 4.10.5) that

\phi[t^{m}],\ \psi[t^{m}]\subseteq O^{\mathrm{nr}}_{f},\quad\forall\,m\geqslant 1.

Moreover, the natural reduction maps

(4.2.3)

\phi(O^{\mathrm{nr}}_{f})[t^{m}]=\phi[t^{m}]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mkern 2.5mu\overline{\mkern-2.5mu\phi}[t^{m}],\quad\psi(O^{\mathrm{nr}}_{f})[t^{m}]=\psi[t^{m}]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mkern 2.5mu\overline{\mkern-2.5mu\psi}[t^{m}],\quad\forall\,m\geqslant 1,

are
\mathsf{A}
-module isomorphisms (e.g., see
[
69
]
*§2).
Thus for each
i=1,\dots,\ell
, the Anderson generating functions
g_{1},\dots,g_{\ell}
associated to
\psi
as in Example
2.4.12
each satisfy
g_{i}\in{O^{\mathrm{nr}}_{f}[\![t]\!]}
. Furthermore, since
\det\Upsilon_{\psi}^{(1)}=c(t-\theta)\det\Upsilon_{\psi}
, it follows that
\det\Upsilon_{\psi}=c^{\prime}\omega
for some
c^{\prime}\in\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
(e.g., see
[
27
]
*§7
[
33
]
*Eq. (6.3.2)
[
54
]
*Prop. 4.3.10). By the formulation
\omega=\sum_{m\geqslant 0}\exp_{\mathsf{C}}(\widetilde{\pi}/\theta^{m+1})t^{m}
in (
2.4.10
), it follows that
\omega\in{O^{\mathrm{nr}}_{f}[\![t]\!]}^{\times}
, since
\exp_{\mathsf{C}}(\widetilde{\pi}/\theta)=(-\theta)^{1/(q-1)}\in(O^{\mathrm{nr}}_{f})^{\times}
. It follows that

\Upsilon_{\psi,z}\in\GL_{\ell}\bigl({O^{\mathrm{nr}}_{f}[\![z]\!]}\bigr),

and therefore from (
4.1.13
),

(4.2.4)

\mathbb{E}[t^{m}]=\Upsilon_{\psi,z}^{-1}\cdot\phi^{\oplus\ell}[t^{m}]\subseteq\mathbb{E}\bigl({O^{\mathrm{nr}}_{f}(\!(z)\!)}\bigr).

By (
4.2.3
), for each
m\geqslant 1
we have an isomorphism
\phi^{\oplus\ell}({O^{\mathrm{nr}}_{f}(\!(z)\!)})[t^{m}]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\smash{\mkern 2.5mu\overline{\mkern-2.5mu\phi}}^{\oplus\ell}({\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{f}(\!(z)\!)})[t^{m}]
of
{\mathbb{F}_{q}(\!(z)\!)}[t]
-modules, and the following diagram of
{\mathbb{F}_{q}(\!(z)\!)}[t]
-modules commutes:

(4.2.5)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{E}\bigl({O^{\mathrm{nr}}_{f}(\!(z)\!)}\bigr)[t^{m}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\mathbb{E}}\bigl({\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{f}(\!(z)\!)}\bigr)[t^{m}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi^{\oplus\ell}\bigl({O^{\mathrm{nr}}_{f}(\!(z)\!)}\bigr)[t^{m}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\smash{\mkern 2.5mu\overline{\mkern-2.5mu\phi}}^{\oplus\ell}\bigl({\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{f}(\!(z)\!)}\bigr)[t^{m}].}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Upsilon_{\psi,z}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\Upsilon}_{\psi,z}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

The left-hand column is an isomorphism from (
4.1.13
). The map
\overline{\Upsilon}_{\psi,z}:\overline{\mathbb{E}}({\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{f}(\!(z)\!)})\to\smash{\mkern 2.5mu\overline{\mkern-2.5mu\phi}}^{\oplus\ell}({\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{f}(\!(z)\!)})
is an isomorphism since
\Upsilon_{\psi,z}\in\GL_{\ell}({O^{\mathrm{nr}}_{f}[\![z]\!]})
, and so the inverse restricted to
t^{m}
-torsion,
\smash{\overline{\Upsilon}}_{\psi,z}^{-1}:\smash{\mkern 2.5mu\overline{\mkern-2.5mu\phi}}^{\oplus\ell}({\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{f}(\!(z)\!)})[t^{m}]\to\overline{\mathbb{E}}({\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{f}(\!(z)\!)})[t^{m}]
, is also an isomorphism. Taking
\mathcal{A}={\mathbb{F}_{q}(\!(z)\!)}[t]
in Corollary
3.3.8
we see that

\dim_{{\mathbb{F}_{q}(\!(z)\!)}}\smash{\mkern 2.5mu\overline{\mkern-2.5mu\phi}}^{\oplus\ell}({\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{f}(\!(z)\!)})[t^{m}]=r\ell m,

and so also
\dim_{{\mathbb{F}_{q}(\!(z)\!)}}\overline{\mathbb{E}}({\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{f}(\!(z)\!)})[t^{m}]=r\ell m
. Thus
\overline{\mathbb{E}}({\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{f}(\!(z)\!)})[t^{m}]
has full dimension. Moreover, Corollary
3.3.8
implies

\overline{\mathbb{E}}({\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{f}(\!(z)\!)})[t^{m}]\cong\biggl(\frac{{\mathbb{F}_{q}(\!(z)\!)}[t]}{t^{m}{\mathbb{F}_{q}(\!(z)\!)}[t]}\biggr)^{\oplus\ell},

and each of the maps in (
4.2.5
) is an isomorphism of
{\mathbb{F}_{q}(\!(z)\!)}[t]
-modules. Since
\phi
and
\psi
are defined over
A
and
\mathbb{E}
is defined over
\mathbb{A}
, it follows that these maps also commute with the
\Gal(\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{f}/\mathbb{F}_{f})
-action.

Proof of Theorem
4.2.2
. Case
f\neq\theta
.

By the discussion above,
\overline{\mathbb{E}}({\overline{\mathbb{F}}(\!(z)\!)})[t^{m}]
has full dimension for each
m\geqslant 1
. Therefore, Theorem
3.7.4
implies that

{\left[\mkern 1.0mu\overline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathbb{E}}({\mathbb{F}_{f}(\!(z)\!)})\right]}_{{\mathbb{F}_{q}(\!(z)\!)}[t]}=\gamma\cdot\Char(\tau^{d},T_{t}(\overline{\mathbb{E}}),1),

where
\gamma\in{\mathbb{F}_{q}(\!(z)\!)}^{\times}
is chosen to make the expression monic in
t
. On the other hand, because the maps in (
4.2.5
) commute with the Galois action, if we let
\alpha_{f}\in\Gal(K^{\mathrm{sep}}/K)
be a Frobenius element, we have

\Char(\tau^{d},T_{t}(\overline{\mathbb{E}}),X)=\Char(\alpha_{f},T_{t}(\mathbb{E}),X).

Combining these findings with Proposition
4.1.17
, we see that

(4.2.6)

{\left[\mkern 1.0mu\overline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathbb{E}}({\mathbb{F}_{f}(\!(z)\!)})\right]}_{{\mathbb{F}_{q}(\!(z)\!)}[\theta]}=\gamma\cdot\mathbf{P}_{f}(1).

Now as
\mathbf{P}_{f}(X)=(P_{\phi,f}\otimes P_{\psi,f(z)}^{\vee})(X)
, it follows from (
2.3.7
) and Definition
2.5.4
that the constant term of
\mathbf{P}_{f}(X)
is

(4.2.7)

\mathbf{P}_{f}(0)=(-1)^{r\ell}\bigl(\overline{\chi}_{\phi}(f)f\bigr)^{\ell}\cdot\biggl(\frac{\chi_{\psi}(f)}{f(z)}\biggr)^{r}.

Writing
\mathbf{P}_{f}(X)=\sum_{i=0}^{r\ell}b_{i}X^{i}
,
b_{i}\in\mathbf{A}[X]
and
letting
c_{0},\dots,c_{r-1}\in A
be given as in (
2.3.1
), Definition
2.5.4
implies that, for
0\leqslant m\leqslant r\ell-1
, each
b_{m}
is a polynomial in
c_{0},\dots,c_{r-1}
with coefficients in
\mathbb{F}_{q}(z)
.
Assigning the weight
r-i
to each
c_{i}
, then as formal expressions, each monomial in
c_{0},\dots,c_{r-1}
in
b_{m}
has the same total weight
r\ell-m
. That is, if
c_{0}^{n_{0}}\dots c_{r-1}^{n_{r-1}}
is a monomial in
b_{m}
, then

\sum_{i=0}^{r-1}(r-i)n_{i}=r\ell-m
, and so by §
2.3.2
,

\deg_{\theta}\bigl(c_{0}^{n_{0}}\cdots c_{r-1}^{n_{r-1}}\bigr)\leqslant\sum_{i=0}^{r-1}\frac{d}{r}\cdot n_{i}(r-i)=\frac{d}{r}(r\ell-m)=d\ell-\frac{dm}{r}.

From (
4.2.7
), this is an equality if
m=0
. On the other hand, this inequality implies,

0<m\leqslant r\ell-1\quad\Rightarrow\quad\deg_{\theta}b_{m}<d\ell.

Therefore from (
4.2.7
),
\gamma=(-1)^{r\ell}\chi_{\phi}(f)^{\ell}\overline{\chi}_{\psi}(f)^{r}\cdot f(z)^{r}
. By (
4.2.6
) it remains to verify that
{[\mkern 1.0mu\overline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathbb{E}}({\mathbb{F}_{f}(\!(z)\!)}]}_{{\mathbb{F}_{q}(\!(z)\!)}[t]}={[\mkern 1.0mu\overline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathbb{E}}(\mathbb{F}_{f}(z))]}_{\mathbf{A}}
. However, suppose
\overline{\mathbb{E}}(\mathbb{F}_{f}(z))\cong\mathbf{A}/h_{1}\mathbf{A}\oplus\cdots\oplus\mathbf{A}/h_{s}\mathbf{A}
for monic
h_{1},\dots,h_{s}\in\mathbf{A}
. Since
{\mathbb{F}_{f}(\!(z)\!)}\cong{\mathbb{F}_{q}(\!(z)\!)}\otimes_{\mathbb{F}_{q}}\mathbb{F}_{f}\cong{\mathbb{F}_{q}(\!(z)\!)}\otimes_{\mathbb{F}_{q}(z)}\mathbb{F}_{f}(z)
as
{\mathbb{F}_{q}(\!(z)\!)}
-vector spaces, we have an isomorphism of
{\mathbb{F}_{q}(\!(z)\!)}[t]
-modules,

\overline{\mathbb{E}}({\mathbb{F}_{f}(\!(z)\!)})\cong{\mathbb{F}_{q}(\!(z)\!)}\otimes_{\mathbb{F}_{q}(z)}\overline{\mathbb{E}}(\mathbb{F}_{f}(z)).

It follows that

\overline{\mathbb{E}}({\mathbb{F}_{f}(\!(z)\!)})\cong\frac{{\mathbb{F}_{q}(\!(z)\!)}[t]}{h_{1}{\mathbb{F}_{q}(\!(z)\!)}[t]}\oplus\cdots\oplus\frac{{\mathbb{F}_{q}(\!(z)\!)}[t]}{h_{s}{\mathbb{F}_{q}(\!(z)\!)}[t]},

as desired.
∎

In the case that
f=\theta
, the previous argument does not work because among other issues, (a) the coefficients of the Anderson generating functions
g_{1},\dots,g_{r}
in Example
2.4.12
may have some coefficients that are not
\theta
-integral and (b) furthermore
\omega_{z}\notin{O^{\mathrm{nr}}_{\theta}[\![z]\!]}^{\times}
. However, this case can be checked directly.

Proof of Theorem
4.2.2
. Case
f=\theta
.

In this case,
\mkern 2.5mu\overline{\mkern-2.5mu\theta}=0
and so

\overline{\Gamma}_{\phi}=\begin{pmatrix}0&1&\cdots&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
0&0&\cdots&1\\
\overline{\kappa}_{r}^{-1}t&-\overline{\kappa}_{1}\overline{\kappa}_{r}^{-1}&\cdots&-\kappa_{r-1}\overline{\kappa}_{r}^{-1}\end{pmatrix},\quad\overline{\Theta}_{\psi,z}=\begin{pmatrix}0&\cdots&0&z\overline{\eta}_{\ell}^{-1}\\
1&\cdots&0&-\overline{\eta}_{1}\overline{\eta}_{\ell}^{-1}\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots\\
0&\cdots&1&-\overline{\eta}_{\ell-1}\overline{\eta}_{\ell}^{-1}\end{pmatrix},

which are in
\Mat_{\ell}(\mathbb{F}_{q}[t])
and
\Mat_{\ell}(\mathbb{F}_{q}[z])
respectively. By Corollary
3.7.3
,

P_{\phi,t}(X)=\Char(\overline{\Gamma}_{\phi},X),\quad P_{\psi,z}=\Char(\overline{\Theta}_{\psi,z},X),

where
P_{\phi,t}(X)=P_{\phi,\theta}(X)|_{\theta=t}
. Since
\overline{\Theta}_{\psi,z}^{(1)}=\overline{\Theta}_{\psi,z}
,
we have
\overline{\Theta}_{\psi,z}\smash{\overline{\Theta}}_{\psi,z}^{(1)}\cdots\smash{\overline{\Theta}}_{\psi,z}^{(i-1)}=\smash{\overline{\Theta}}_{\psi,z}^{i}
for each
i\geq 1
, and so by
[
54
]
*Eq. (4.6.20),
if we let

\Gamma\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\begin{pmatrix}0&\mathrm{I}_{\ell}&\cdots&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
0&0&\cdots&\mathrm{I}_{\ell}\\
\overline{\kappa}_{r}^{-1}t\overline{\Theta}_{\psi,z}^{-r}&-\overline{\kappa}_{1}\overline{\kappa}_{r}^{-1}\overline{\Theta}_{\psi,z}^{-r+1}&\cdots&-\overline{\kappa}_{r-1}\overline{\kappa}_{r}^{-1}\overline{\Theta}_{\psi,z}^{-1}\end{pmatrix},

then
\Gamma
represents multiplication by
\tau
on
\mathcal{M}_{\overline{\mathbb{E}}}(\mathbb{F}_{q}(z))
as in §
4.1.8
. Furthermore, since
d=1
, we have
\mathrm{G}=\Gamma
, and so by Corollary
3.6.12
and equations (
3.7.5
) and (
3.7.6
),

{\left[\mkern 1.0mu\overline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathbb{E}}(\mathbb{F}_{q}(z))\right]}_{\mathbf{A}}=\gamma\cdot\Char(\Gamma,1),

where
\gamma\in\mathbb{F}_{q}(z)^{\times}
is chosen to make this expression monic in
t
. If we take the block diagonal matrix
B=\diag(\mathrm{I}_{\ell},\overline{\Theta}_{\psi,z},\dots,\overline{\Theta}_{\psi,z}^{r-1})\in\GL_{r\ell}(\mathbb{F}_{q}(z))
, then one checks

B\Gamma B^{-1}=\begin{pmatrix}0&\overline{\Theta}_{\psi,z}^{-1}&\cdots&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
0&0&\cdots&\overline{\Theta}_{\psi,z}^{-1}\\
\overline{\kappa}_{r}^{-1}t\overline{\Theta}_{\psi,z}^{-1}&-\overline{\kappa}_{1}\overline{\kappa}_{r}^{-1}\overline{\Theta}_{\psi,z}^{-1}&\cdots&-\overline{\kappa}_{r-1}\overline{\kappa}_{r}^{-1}\overline{\Theta}_{\psi,z}^{-1}\end{pmatrix}=\overline{\Gamma}_{\phi}\otimes\overline{\Theta}_{\psi,z}^{-1}.

Thus,
\Char(\Gamma,X)=\Char(\overline{\Gamma}_{\phi}\otimes\overline{\Theta}_{\psi,z}^{-1},X)
,
and so
{[\mkern 1.0mu\overline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathbb{E}}(\mathbb{F}_{q}(z))]}_{\mathbf{A}}=\gamma\cdot(P_{\phi,t}\otimes P_{\psi,z}^{\vee})(1)
. Verifying that
\gamma=(-1)^{r\ell}\chi_{\phi}(f)^{\ell}\overline{\chi}(f)^{r}
is exactly the same as in the
f\neq\theta
case.
∎

5.
Convolutions of Goss and Pellarin
L
-series

In a series of articles
[
39
]

[
40
]

[
41
]

[
44
]
*Ch. 8, Goss defined and investigated function field valued
L
-series attached to Drinfeld modules and
t
-modules defined over finite extensions of
K
. These
L
-functions possess a rich structure of special values, initiated by Carlitz
[
17
]
*Thm. 9.3 for the eponymous Carlitz zeta function and continued by Goss
[
41
]

[
44
]
*Ch. 8. Anderson and Thakur
[
3
]
further revealed the connection between Carlitz zeta values and coordinates of logarithms on tensor powers of the Carlitz module.

Taelman
[
65
,
66
,
67
]
discovered a breakthrough on special
L
-values for Drinfeld modules that related them to the product of an analytic regulator and the
A
-order of a class module. These results have been extended in several directions, including to
t
-modules defined over
\mkern 2.5mu\overline{\mkern-2.5muK}
and more refined special value identities
[
6
,
7
,
12
,
14
,
29
,
18
,
28
,
37
,
32
]
.

In
[
61
]
, Pellarin introduced a new class of
L
-functions that are deformations of the Carlitz zeta function in additional variables and take values in Tate algebras. Results on Pellarin
L
-series and their special values have been investigated extensively
[
5
,
10
,
11
,
8
,
9
,
13
,
35
,
36
,
34
,
45
,
58
,
59
,
62
,
70
]
. Important for the present paper is the work of Demeslay
[
8
,
24
,
25
]
, who extended Taelman’s special value formulas to
L
-series of
t
-modules over Tate algebras. See
[
13
,
8
,
14
,
35
,
36
]
for additional applications of Demeslay’s work.

5.1.
Goss
L
-series

Let
\phi:\mathsf{A}\to A[\tau]
be a Drinfeld module over
A
with everywhere good reduction as in (
4.1.1
). Goss
[
41
]
*§3
[
44
]
*§8.6 associated the Dirichlet series

(5.1.1)

L(\phi^{\vee},s)=\prod_{f\in A_{+},\ \textup{irred.}}Q_{f}^{\vee}\bigl(f^{-s}\bigr)^{-1},\quad L(\phi,s)=\prod_{f\in A_{+},\ \textup{irred.}}Q_{f}\bigl(f^{-s}\bigr)^{-1}.

where as in §
2.3
,
Q_{f}(X)\in A[X]
is the reciprocal polynomial of the characteristic polynomial
P_{f}(X)
of Frobenius acting on the Tate module
T_{\lambda}(\mkern 2.5mu\overline{\mkern-2.5mu\phi})
and
Q_{f}^{\vee}(X)\in K[X]
is the reciprocal polynomial of
P_{f}^{\vee}(X)
arising from
T_{\lambda}(\mkern 2.5mu\overline{\mkern-2.5mu\phi})^{\vee}
.
In the future we will write simply “
\prod_{f}
” to indicate that a product is over all irreducible
f\in A_{+}
.

Remark 5.1.2
.

Corollary
3.7.3
makes the calculation of
P_{f}(X)
and
P_{f}^{\vee}(X)
reasonable (and hence
Q_{f}(X)
and
Q_{f}^{\vee}(X)
also). If we take
\Gamma\in\Mat_{r}(A[t])
as in (
2.4.5
), then for
f\in A_{+}
of degree
d
, we take the reduction
\overline{\Gamma}\in\Mat_{r}(\mathbb{F}_{f}[t])
. Corollary
3.7.3
implies

(5.1.3)

\displaystyle P_{f}(X)

\displaystyle=\Char\bigl(\smash{\overline{\Gamma}}^{(d-1)}\cdots\smash{\overline{\Gamma}}^{(1)}\overline{\Gamma},X\bigr)|_{t=\theta}\in A[X],

\displaystyle P_{f}^{\vee}(X)

\displaystyle=\Char\bigl(\overline{\Gamma}^{-1}(\smash{\overline{\Gamma}}^{(1)})^{-1}\cdots(\smash{\overline{\Gamma}}^{(d-1)})^{-1},X\bigr)|_{t=\theta}\in A[X].

Since we have assumed
\phi
has everywhere good reduction, we have avoided Euler factors at primes of bad reduction. On the other hand, bad primes also greatly complicate our convolution problem, so for the present paper we do not consider them (see Remark
1.4.7
).

The bounds on the coefficients of
P_{f}(X)
from §
2.3.2
imply that
L(\phi,s)
converges in
K_{\infty}
for
s\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
and that
L(\phi^{\vee},s)
converges for
s\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 0}
(e.g., see
[
18
]
*§3). Goss extended the definition of these
L
-series to
s
in a non-archimedean analytic space, but we will not pursue these extensions here. We will henceforth assume
s\in\mathbb{Z}
.

By (
2.3.4
), we find that

(5.1.4)

L(\phi^{\vee},s)=\sum_{a\in A_{+}}\frac{\mu_{\phi}(f)}{a^{s+1}}

(see
[
18
]
*Eqs. (12)–(14)). In particular, for the Carlitz module
P_{\mathsf{C},f}^{\vee}(X)=X-1/f
, so

L(\mathsf{C}^{\vee},s)=\sum_{a\in A_{+}}\frac{1}{a^{s+1}}=\zeta_{\mathsf{C}}(s+1)

is a shift of the Carlitz zeta function.

Taelman
[
67
]
*Thm. 1 proved a special value identity for
L(\phi^{\vee},0)
as follows. First,

(5.1.5)

Q_{f}^{\vee}(1)^{-1}=\frac{f}{(-1)^{r}\,\overline{\chi}(f)\cdot P_{f}(1)}=\frac{{[\mathbb{F}_{f}]}_{A}}{{[\mkern 2.5mu\overline{\mkern-2.5mu\phi}(\mathbb{F}_{f})]}_{A}},

where the first equality follows from (
2.3.8
) and the second from Gekeler
[
31
]
*Thm. 5.1 (and also from Corollary
3.7.8
combined with the definition of
P_{f}(X)
). We then have

(5.1.6)

L(\phi^{\vee},0)=\prod_{f}\frac{{\left[\mathbb{F}_{f}\right]}_{A}}{{\left[\mkern 2.5mu\overline{\mkern-2.5mu\phi}(\mathbb{F}_{f})\right]}_{A}}=\mathrm{Reg}_{\phi}\cdot\mathrm{H}(\phi),

where the first equality follows from (
5.1.5
) and the second is Taelman’s identity.
The formula on the right contains the regulator
\mathrm{Reg}_{\phi}\in K_{\infty}
and the order of the class module
\mathrm{H}(\phi)\in A
(see
[
67
]
for details).
We will use Demeslay’s generalization of Taelman’s formula to
t
-modules over Tate algebras. See Theorem
5.3.10
and Remark
5.3.11
.

Remark 5.1.7
.

In this paper the
L
-function
L(\phi,s)
and is defined using the Galois action on
T_{\lambda}(\mkern 2.5mu\overline{\mkern-2.5mu\phi})
. This is consistent with previous descriptions in
[
18
,
31
,
32
,
67
]
, but it is switched from Goss’s original definition
[
41
]
*§3
[
44
]
*§8.6, where he defined
L(\phi,s)
in terms of
T_{\lambda}(\mkern 2.5mu\overline{\mkern-2.5mu\phi})^{\vee}
. To make following the notation easier we have elected to align

L(\phi,s)\longleftrightarrow T_{\lambda}(\phi),\qquad L(\phi^{\vee},s)\longleftrightarrow T_{\lambda}(\phi)^{\vee}.

However, this leads to a possible notational incongruity in Taelman’s formula (
5.1.6
) and Demeslay’s generalization in Theorem
5.3.10
, where arithmetic invariants of
\phi
are expressed in terms of
L(\phi^{\vee},0)
.

5.2.
Pellarin
L
-series

In
[
61
]
, Pellarin defined the series,

(5.2.1)

L(\mathbb{A},s)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\sum_{a\in A_{+}}\frac{a(z)}{a^{s}}=\prod_{f}\biggl(1-\frac{f(z)}{f^{s}}\biggr)^{-1}\in\mathbb{T}_{z}(K_{\infty}),

which converges in
\mathbb{T}_{z}(K_{\infty})
for
s\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
and is entire as a function of
z
. Among other properties, Pellarin proved the following special value formula.

Theorem 5.2.2

(Pellarin
[
61
]
*Thm. 1)
.

We have

L(\mathbb{A},1)=-\frac{\widetilde{\pi}}{(z-\theta)\omega_{z}}.

We note further that if we take
\mathbf{P}_{f}^{\vee}(X)=\mathbf{P}_{\mathsf{C}\times\mathsf{C},f}^{\vee}(X)=X-f(z)/f
in (
4.1.22
), then

\mathbf{P}_{f}^{\vee}(1)^{-1}=\frac{f}{f-f(z)}=\frac{{\left[\mathbb{F}_{f}(z)\right]}_{\mathbb{A}}}{{\left[\mkern 1.0mu\overline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathbb{E}}(\mathsf{C}\times\mathsf{C})(\mathbb{F}_{f}(z))\right]}_{\mathbb{A}}},

where the calculation that
{[\mkern 1.0mu\overline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathbb{E}}(\mathsf{C}\times\mathsf{C})(\mathbb{F}_{f}(z))]}_{\mathbb{A}}=f-f(z)
follows from
[
8
]
*Lem. 5.8. It also follows from Theorem
4.2.2
, since
\chi_{\mathsf{C}}(f)=1
for all
f
and
\mathbf{P}_{f}(1)=1-f/f(z)
from (
4.1.22
). Letting
\mathbf{Q}_{f}^{\vee}(X)
be the reciprocal polynomial of
\mathbf{P}_{f}^{\vee}(X)
, we also find

(5.2.3)

L(\mathbb{E}(\mathsf{C}\times\mathsf{C})^{\vee},s)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\prod_{f}\mathbf{Q}_{f}^{\vee}\bigl(f^{-s}\bigr)^{-1}=L(\mathbb{A},s+1).

Thus the value
L(\mathbb{E}(\mathsf{C}\times\mathsf{C})^{\vee},0)=L(\mathbb{A},1)
can be obtained through Theorem
5.2.2
. It was this type of calculation that led us to the
L
-series
L(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\phi,\theta}\times\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\psi,z},s)
in §
5.4
.

5.3.
Demeslay’s class module formula

In
[
24
,
25
]
, Demeslay proved an extension of Taelman’s class module formula to abelian and
\mathbf{A}
-finite Anderson
t
-modules defined over
\mathbb{A}
. In fact Demeslay’s formula
[
24
]
*Thm. 2.7 applies over much more general base rings, but we will only require his identity over
\mathbb{A}
.

Let
\mathbf{E}:\mathbf{A}\to\Mat_{\ell}(\mathbb{A}[\tau])
be an abelian and
\mathbf{A}
-finite Anderson
t
-module defined over
\mathbb{A}
.
The exponential series
\Exp_{\mathbf{E}}\in{\mathbb{K}[\![\tau]\!]}
of
\mathbf{E}
induces an
\mathbb{F}_{q}(z)
-linear function

(5.3.1)

\Exp_{\mathbf{E},\mathbb{K}_{\infty}}\colon\Lie(\mathbf{E})(\mathbb{K}_{\infty})\to\mathbf{E}(\mathbb{K}_{\infty})\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\Exp_{\mathbf{E},\mathbb{K}_{\infty}}\colon\mathbb{K}_{\infty}^{\ell}\to\mathbb{K}_{\infty}^{\ell}.

Now
\Lie(\mathbf{E})(\mathbb{K}_{\infty})
has a canonical
\mathbb{K}_{\infty}
-vector space structure, but Demeslay
[
24
]
*p. 5 pointed out that it has another structure of a vector space over
{\mathbb{F}_{q}(z)(\!(t^{-1})\!)}
. Namely we extend
\partial:\mathbf{A}\to\Mat_{\ell}(\mathbb{K}_{\infty})
to an
\mathbb{F}_{q}(z)
-algebra homomorphism,

(5.3.2)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{F}_{q}(z)(\!(t^{-1})\!)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\partial}}{{\longrightarrow}}\Mat_{\ell}(\mathbb{K}_{\infty})\;:\;\sum_{j\geqslant j_{0}}c_{j}t^{-j}\longmapsto\sum_{j\geqslant j_{0}}c_{j}\cdot\partial\mathbf{E}_{t}^{-j}.

Notably the series on the right converges by
[
24
]
*Lem. 2.4. As Demeslay continued,
\Lie(\mathbf{E})(\mathbb{K}_{\infty})
obtains an
{\mathbb{F}_{q}(z)(\!(t^{-1})\!)}
-vector space structure via
\partial
. For any
g\in{\mathbb{F}_{q}(z)(\!(t^{-q^{\ell}})\!)}
, we have
\partial g=g\cdot\mathrm{I}_{\ell}
, and so
\Lie(\mathbf{E})(\mathbb{K}_{\infty})
has dimension
\ell q^{\ell}
as over
{\mathbb{F}_{q}(z)(\!(t^{-q^{\ell}})\!)}
, which implies it has dimension
\ell
over
{\mathbb{F}_{q}(z)(\!(t^{-1})\!)}
.

Since
\mathbb{K}_{\infty}={\mathbb{F}_{q}(z)(\!(\theta^{-1})\!)}\cong{\mathbb{F}_{q}(z)(\!(t^{-1})\!)}
, we will abuse notation and use the map
\partial
to define new
\mathbb{K}_{\infty}
-vector space and
\mathbb{A}
-module structures on
\Lie(\mathbf{E})(\mathbb{K}_{\infty})
that are possibly different from scalar multiplication.
With respect to this
\mathbb{K}_{\infty}
-structure, Demeslay showed
[
24
]
*Prop. 2.5 that
\Lie(\mathbf{E})(\mathbb{A})\subseteq\Lie(\mathbf{E})(\mathbb{K}_{\infty})
is an
\mathbb{A}
-lattice and that the standard basis
\{\mathbf{s}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{s}_{\ell}\}
of
\Lie(\mathbf{E})(\mathbb{K}_{\infty})
, when equated with
\mathbb{K}_{\infty}^{\ell}
, is an
\mathbb{A}
-basis of
\Lie(\mathbf{E})(\mathbb{A})
via
\partial
.
Demeslay further proved
[
24
]
*Prop. 2.6 that

(5.3.3)

\Exp_{\mathbf{E},\mathbb{K}_{\infty}}^{-1}\bigl(\mathbf{E}(\mathbb{A})\bigr)\subseteq\Lie(\mathbf{E})(\mathbb{K}_{\infty})

is an
\mathbb{A}
-lattice as in Definition
3.4.5
. In particular it has rank
\ell
as an
\mathbb{A}
-module via
\partial
.

Remark 5.3.4
.

For applications in the present paper, all
t
-modules will satisfy
\partial\mathbf{E}_{t}=\theta\cdot\mathrm{I}_{\ell}
, and so the
\mathbb{K}_{\infty}
-vector space structure on
\Lie(\mathbf{E})(\mathbb{K}_{\infty})
and the
\mathbb{A}
-module structure on
\Exp_{\mathbf{E},\mathbb{K}_{\infty}}^{-1}(\mathbb{A})
will be induced by the usual scalar multiplication.

Choose an
\mathbb{A}
-basis
\{\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{1},\dots,\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\ell}\}
of
\Exp_{\mathbf{E},\mathbb{K}_{\infty}}^{-1}(\mathbb{A})
via
\partial
, and let
V\in\GL_{\ell}(\mathbb{K}_{\infty})
be chosen so that its columns are the coordinates of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{1},\dots,\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{\ell}
with respect to
\mathbf{s}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{s}_{\ell}
(via
\partial
). Following Taelman
[
66
,
67
]
, Demeslay defined the regulator of
\mathbf{E}
as

(5.3.5)

\mathrm{Reg}_{\mathbf{E}}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\gamma\cdot\det(V)\in\mathbb{K}_{\infty},\quad\gamma\in\mathbb{F}_{q}(z)^{\times},

where
\gamma
is chosen so that
\mathrm{Reg}_{\mathbf{E}}
has sign
1
(leading coefficient as an element of
{\mathbb{F}_{q}(z)(\!(\theta^{-1})\!)}
is
1
).
This value is independent of the choice of
\mathbb{A}
-basis.

Remark 5.3.6
.

If
\partial\mathbf{E}_{t}=\theta\mathrm{I}_{\ell}
and the standard basis vectors
\mathbf{s}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{s}_{\ell}\in\mathbf{E}(\mathbb{K}_{\infty})=\mathbb{K}_{\infty}^{\ell}
fall within the domain of convergence of
\Log_{\mathbf{E}}(\mathbf{z})
, then there is
\gamma\in\mathbb{F}_{q}(z)^{\times}
so that

(5.3.7)

\mathrm{Reg}_{\mathbf{E}}=\gamma\cdot\det\Bigl(\Log_{\mathbf{E}}(\mathbf{s}_{1}),\dots,\Log_{\mathbf{E}}(\mathbf{s}_{\ell})\Bigr)=\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}\gamma\cdot\det\bigl(\Log_{\mathbf{E}}(\mathrm{I}_{\ell})\bigr).

If
\mathbf{E}=\mathbb{E}=\mathbb{E}(\phi\times\psi)
as in §
4.1
, then under these conditions we have

(5.3.8)

\mathrm{Reg}_{\mathbb{E}}=\det\bigl(\Log_{\mathbb{E}}(\mathrm{I}_{\ell})\bigr)=\det\Bigl(\Upsilon_{\psi,z}^{-1}\Log_{\phi}\bigl(\Upsilon_{\psi,z}\bigr)\Bigr).

Indeed, we have that
\mathrm{Reg}_{\mathbb{E}}=\gamma\cdot\Log_{\mathbb{E}}(\mathrm{I}_{\ell})
for some
\gamma\in\mathbb{F}_{q}(z)^{\times}
that forces the expression to be monic. However, in this case
\Log_{\mathbb{E}}(\mathrm{I}_{\ell})=\Upsilon_{\psi,z}^{-1}\Log_{\phi}(\Upsilon_{\psi,z})
, and then we must have that the entries of
\Upsilon_{\psi,z}
all fall within the radius of convergence of
R_{\phi}
of
\Log_{\phi}
as in (
2.2.5
). It is shown in
[
26
]
*Cor. 4.2
[
27
]
*Prop. 6.10
[
49
]
*Cor. 4.5 that in this case the term of
\Log_{\phi}(\Upsilon_{\psi,z})
of greatest
\lVert\,\cdot\,\rVert
-norm is uniquely the first term
\Upsilon_{\psi,z}
. Therefore, the term of
\Log_{\mathbb{E}}(\mathrm{I}_{\ell})
of greatest
\lVert\,\cdot\,\rVert
-norm is uniquely the first term
\mathrm{I}_{\ell}
, forcing
\gamma=1
.
On the other hand, when
\Upsilon_{\psi,z}
is not within the radius of convergence of
\Log_{\phi}(\mathbf{z})
, the determination of
\mathrm{Reg}_{\mathbb{E}}
can be subtle (see §
6.4.4
).

Also following Taelman, Demeslay
[
24
]
*Prop. 2.6 defined the class module of
\mathbf{E}
as

(5.3.9)

\mathrm{H}(\mathbf{E})\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\frac{\mathbf{E}(\mathbb{K}_{\infty})}{\Exp_{\mathbf{E},\mathbb{K}_{\infty}}(\Lie(\mathbf{E})(\mathbb{K}_{\infty}))+\mathbf{E}(\mathbb{A})},

and he proved that
\mathrm{H}(\mathbf{E})
is a finitely generated and torsion
\mathbb{A}
-module. Demeslay’s class module formula is the following.

Theorem 5.3.10

(Demeslay
[
24
]
*Thm. 2.7
[
25
]
*Thm. 2.1.9)
.

Let
\mathbf{E}:\mathbf{A}\to\Mat_{\ell}(\mathbb{A}[\tau])
be an abelian and
\mathbf{A}
-finite Anderson
t
-module. Then

\prod_{f}\frac{{[\Lie(\mkern 1.0mu\overline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathbf{E}})(\mathbb{F}_{f}(z))]}_{\mathbb{A}}}{{[\mkern 1.0mu\overline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathbf{E}}(\mathbb{F}_{f}(z))]}_{\mathbb{A}}}=\mathrm{Reg}_{\mathbf{E}}\cdot{\left[\mathrm{H}(\mathbf{E})\right]}_{\mathbb{A}},

where the left-hand side converges in
\mathbb{K}_{\infty}
.

Remark 5.3.11
.

If
\phi:\mathsf{A}\to A[\tau]
is a Drinfeld module, which we consider to be a constant Drinfeld module over
\mathbb{A}
as in Theorem
3.4.9
. Then
\mathrm{Reg}_{\phi}
and
{[\mathrm{H}(\phi)]}_{\mathbb{A}}
agree with the regulator and class module order of Taelman, and Demeslay’s result provides the same identity as Taelman’s class module formula
[
67
]
*Thm. 1 in (
5.1.6
).

5.4.
The
L
-function of
\mathbb{E}(\phi\times\psi)

Let
\phi
,
\psi:\mathsf{A}\to A[\tau]
be Drinfeld modules defined over
A
of ranks
r
and
\ell
respectively as in (
4.1.1
) and (
4.1.2
). We form the
t
-module
\mathbb{E}=\mathbb{E}(\phi\times\psi):\mathbf{A}\to\Mat_{\ell}(\mathbb{A}[\tau])
defined over
\mathbb{A}
as in (
4.1.6
). For each
f\in A_{+}
, we let
\mathbf{P}_{f}(X)=\mathbf{P}_{\phi\times\psi,f}(X)
and
\mathbf{P}_{f}^{\vee}(X)=\mathbf{P}_{\phi\times\psi,f}^{\vee}(X)
as in Definition
4.1.19
. We further set
\mathbf{Q}_{f}(X)
and
\mathbf{Q}_{f}^{\vee}(X)
to be their reciprocal polynomials. We now consider the
L
-function

(5.4.1)

L(\mathbb{E}^{\vee},s)=L(\mathbb{E}(\phi\times\psi)^{\vee},s)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\prod_{f}\mathbf{Q}_{f}^{\vee}\bigl(f^{-s}\bigr)^{-1},\quad s\geqslant 0.

The following lemma addresses convergence of
L(\mathbb{E}^{\vee},s)
.

Lemma 5.4.2
.

Let
\mathbb{E}=\mathbb{E}(\phi\times\psi):\mathbf{A}\to\Mat_{\ell}(\mathbb{A}[\tau])
be defined as in (
4.1.6
). For a fixed integer
s\geqslant 0
, the value
L(\mathbb{E}^{\vee},s)
converges in
\mathbb{T}_{z}(K_{\infty})
.

Proof.

(cf.
[
18
]
*Cor. 3.6)
We first note by Definition
4.1.19
that
\mathbf{Q}_{f}^{\vee}(X)\in K[z,X]
. Moreover, if we let
\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{r}\in\mkern 2.5mu\overline{\mkern-2.5muK}
be the reciprocals of the roots of
P_{\phi,f}(X)
and
\beta_{1},\dots,\beta_{\ell}\in\overline{\mathbb{F}_{q}(z)}
be the roots of
P_{\psi,f(z)}(X)
, then by definition

\mathbf{Q}_{f}^{\vee}(X)=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leqslant i\leqslant r\\
1\leqslant j\leqslant\ell\end{subarray}}\bigl(1-\alpha_{i}\beta_{j}X\bigr)=\prod_{1\leqslant j\leqslant\ell}Q_{f}^{\vee}(\beta_{j}X).

We note that
\beta_{j}
is integral over
\mathbb{F}_{q}[z]
for each
j
. If we let
Q_{f}^{\vee}(\beta_{j}X)=1+\sum_{i=1}^{r}b_{ij}X^{i}
, then by combining the degree estimates in §
2.3.2
with (
2.3.7
), we see that
\deg b_{ij}\leqslant(r-i)d/r
, where
d=\deg f
. From this we see that

\deg\Bigl(1-\mathbf{Q}_{f}^{\vee}\bigl(f^{-s}\bigr)\Bigr)\leqslant\biggl(1-\frac{1}{r}\biggr)d-(1+s)d=-\biggl(\frac{1}{r}+s\biggr)d.

Since there are only finitely many polynomials of any given degree, this implies that the product defining
L(\mathbb{E}^{\vee},s)
converges when
s\geqslant 0
in the completion of
K[z]
with respect to
\lVert\,\cdot\,\rVert
, i.e.,
\mathbb{T}_{z}(K_{\infty})
as discussed in §
2.1.1
.
∎

For each irreducible
f\in\mathsf{A}_{+}
, Definition
4.1.19
implies that
\mathbf{Q}_{f}^{\vee}(1)=\mathbf{P}_{f}(1)/\mathbf{P}_{f}(0)
. By combining Theorem
4.2.2
and (
5.4.1
), we obtain the following identity for
L(\mathbb{E}^{\vee},0)
, which shows that Demeslay’s class module formula (Theorem
5.3.10
) applies to the special values we are considering.

Proposition 5.4.3
.

Let
\mathbb{E}=\mathbb{E}(\phi\times\psi):\mathbf{A}\to\Mat_{\ell}(\mathbb{A}[\tau])
be defined as in (
4.1.6
). Then in
\mathbb{T}_{z}(K_{\infty})
we have

L(\mathbb{E}^{\vee},0)=\prod_{f}\frac{{\left[\mathbb{F}_{f}(z)^{\ell}\right]}_{\mathbb{A}}}{{\left[\mkern 1.0mu\overline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathbb{E}}(\mathbb{F}_{f}(z))\right]}_{\mathbb{A}}}.

Example 5.4.4
.

Twisting a Drinfeld module by Carlitz
. Using (
5.2.3
) as a guide, we consider
L(\mathbb{E}(\phi\times\mathsf{C})^{\vee},s)
for a Drinfeld module
\phi:\mathsf{A}\to A[\tau]
of rank
r
defined as in (
4.1.1
). We let
\mathbf{Q}_{f}^{\vee}(X)=\mathbf{Q}_{\phi\times\mathsf{C},f}^{\vee}(X)\in K[z][X]
be defined as above. We note that (
4.1.23
) implies
\mathbf{Q}_{f}^{\vee}(X)=Q_{\phi,f}^{\vee}(f(z)X)
, and so

L(\mathbb{E}(\phi\times\mathsf{C})^{\vee},s)=\prod_{f}Q_{\phi,f}^{\vee}\bigl(f(z)f^{-s}\bigr)^{-1}.

It follows from (
2.3.4
) that

(5.4.5)

L(\mathbb{E}(\phi\times\mathsf{C})^{\vee},s)=\sum_{a\in A_{+}}\frac{\mu_{\phi}(a)a(z)}{a^{s+1}},\quad s\geqslant 0.

Gezmiş
[
35
]
*Thm. 1.1, Cor. 1.3 investigated the value when
s=0
, finding that when
\deg\omega_{z}=1/(q-1)<\log_{q}(R_{\phi})
, so that
\Log_{\phi}(\omega_{z})
is well-defined,
then as in (
5.3.8
),

(5.4.6)

L(\mathbb{E}(\phi\times\mathsf{C})^{\vee},0)=\sum_{a\in A_{+}}\frac{\mu_{\phi}(a)a(z)}{a}=\frac{\Log_{\phi}(\omega_{z})}{\omega_{z}}.

By (
2.2.5
),
\deg\omega_{z}<\log_{q}(R_{\phi})\Leftrightarrow\deg\kappa_{i}<q^{i}-(q^{i}-1)/(q-1)
, for all
i
,
1\leqslant i\leqslant r
.

Example 5.4.7
.

Twisting Carlitz by a Drinfeld module
.
We also consider
L(\mathbb{E}(\mathsf{C}\times\phi)^{\vee},s)
. By (
4.1.25
), we find that
\mathbf{Q}_{\mathsf{C}\times\phi,f}^{\vee}(X)=Q_{\phi,f(z)}(X/f)
, and thus by (
5.4.1
)

L(\mathbb{E}(\mathsf{C}\times\phi)^{\vee},s)=\prod_{f}Q_{\phi,f(z)}\bigl(f^{-s-1}\bigr)^{-1}.

Letting
\nu_{\phi,z}:A_{+}\to\mathbb{F}_{q}[z]
be defined by
\nu_{\phi,z}(a)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\nu_{\phi}(a)|_{\theta=z}
, we also find from (
2.3.4
),

(5.4.8)

L(\mathbb{E}(\mathsf{C}\times\phi)^{\vee},s)=\sum_{a\in A_{+}}\frac{\nu_{\phi,z}(a)}{a^{s+1}}.

Proposition 5.4.9
.

For
\phi:\mathsf{A}\to A[\tau]
of rank
r
as in (
4.1.1
), if
\deg\kappa_{i}\leqslant q
for each
i
,
1\leqslant i\leqslant r-1
, then

L(\mathbb{E}(\mathsf{C}\times\phi)^{\vee},0)=\sum_{a\in A_{+}}\frac{\nu_{\phi,z}(a)}{a}=\det\Bigl(\Upsilon_{\phi,z}^{-1}\Log_{\mathsf{C}}\bigl(\Upsilon_{\phi,z}\bigr)\Bigr).

Proof.

As in (
5.3.8
), we show that
\deg\Upsilon_{\phi,z}<\log_{q}(R_{\mathsf{C}})=q/(q-1)
.
By
[
49
]
*Thm. 4.4, if we pick
\xi\in\phi[t]
of maximum degree, then
\deg\Upsilon_{\phi,z}=q^{r-1}\deg\xi>0
.
Initially
[
49
]
*Thm. 4.4 requires a particular choice of
A
-basis for
\Lambda_{\phi}
, but changing to a different basis does not affect the calculation.
By the Newton polygon for
\phi_{t}(X)
(e.g., see
[
49
]
*Fig. 1), since
\kappa_{r}\in\mathbb{F}_{q}^{\times}
it follows that
\deg\xi=\max\{\deg(\kappa_{i})/(q^{r}-q^{i})\}_{i=0}^{r-1}
, where we set
\kappa_{0}=\theta
. It follows that

\deg\Upsilon_{\phi,z}<\frac{q}{q-1}\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\deg\kappa_{i}<q\biggl(1+\frac{1}{q}+\cdots+\frac{1}{q^{r-1-i}}\biggr),\quad\forall\,i,\,0\leqslant i\leqslant r-1.

Because
q^{-1}+\cdots+q^{-r+i}<1/(q-1)
for each
i
, the result follows.
∎

Remark 5.4.10
.

If
\phi=\psi=\mathsf{C}
, then Gezmiş’s result (
5.4.6
) and Proposition
5.4.9
provide the same identity, which is an alternative forumulation of Pellarin’s Theorem
5.2.2
, originally proved by Anglès, Pellarin, and Tavares Ribeiro
[
8
]
*Lem. 7.1.

The
L
-series
L(\mathbb{E}(\phi\times\mathsf{C})^{\vee},s)
and
L(\mathbb{E}(\mathsf{C}\times\phi)^{\vee},s)
in (
5.4.5
) and (
5.4.8
) both provide possible extensions, for the Drinfeld module
\phi
, of Pellarin’s
L
-series
L(\mathbb{A},s)
as in (
5.2.3
). As such we refer to them as
Pellarin
L
-series for
\phi
. In the next section we explore more general convolutions between two Drinfeld modules.

6.
Convolutions and special
L
-values

Throughout this section we fix two Drinfeld modules
\phi
,
\psi:\mathsf{A}\to A[\tau]
with everywhere good reduction as in (
4.1.1
) and (
4.1.2
), together with the convolution
t
-module
\mathbb{E}(\phi\times\psi)
as in (
4.1.6
). Since we have already covered the case that either Drinfeld module is the Carlitz module in §
5.4
, we will assume
r
,
\ell\geqslant 2
. Our first task is to express the Dirichlet series for
L(\mathbb{E}(\phi\times\psi)^{\vee},s)
in terms of Schur polynomials from §
2.5
, following the ideas of Bump
[
16
]
and Goldfeld
[
38
]
*Ch. 7, 12.

6.1.
The functions
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\phi,\theta}
and
\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\phi,\theta}

Let
f\in A_{+}
be irreducible, and let
P_{\phi,f}(X)
and
P_{\phi,f}^{\vee}(X)
be defined as in (
2.3.7
). We let
\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{r}\in\mkern 2.5mu\overline{\mkern-2.5muK}
be the roots of
P_{\phi,f}^{\vee}(X)
. For
k_{1},\dots,k_{r-1}\geqslant 0
, we define

(6.1.1)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\phi,\theta}\bigl(f^{k_{1}},\dots,f^{k_{r-1}}\bigr)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=S_{k_{1},\dots,k_{r-1}}(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{r})\cdot f^{k_{1}+\cdots+k_{r-1}},

(6.1.2)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\phi,\theta}\bigl(f^{k_{1}},\dots,f^{k_{r-1}}\bigr)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=S_{k_{1},\dots,k_{r-1}}\bigl(\alpha_{1}^{-1},\dots,\alpha_{r}^{-1}\bigr),

where
S_{k_{1},\dots,k_{r-1}}
is the Schur polynomial of (
2.5.7
). We note that by (
2.3.8
) and (
2.5.9
),

(6.1.3)

\displaystyle Q_{\phi,f}^{\vee}(fX)

\displaystyle=\begin{aligned} 1&{}-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\phi,\theta}(f,1,\ldots,1)X+\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\phi,\theta}(1,f,1,\ldots,1)fX^{2}\\
&{}+\cdots+(-1)^{r-1}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\phi,\theta}(1,\ldots,1,f)f^{r-2}X^{r-1}+(-1)^{r}\chi_{\phi}(f)f^{r-1}X^{r},\end{aligned}

(6.1.4)

\displaystyle Q_{\phi,f}(X)

\displaystyle=\begin{aligned} 1&{}-\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\phi,\theta}(f,1,\ldots,1)X+\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\phi,\theta}(1,f,1,\ldots,1)X^{2}\\
&{}+\cdots+(-1)^{r-1}\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\phi,\theta}(1,\ldots,1,f)X^{r-1}+(-1)^{r}\overline{\chi}_{\phi}(f)fX^{r}.\end{aligned}

We then extend
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\phi,\theta}
and
\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\phi,\theta}
uniquely to functions on
(A_{+})^{r-1}
, by requiring that if
a_{1},\dots,a_{r-1}
,
b_{1},\dots,b_{r-1}\in A_{+}
satisfy
\gcd(a_{1}\cdots a_{r-1},b_{1}\cdots b_{r-1})=1
, then

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\phi,\theta}(a_{1}b_{1},\dots,a_{r-1}b_{r-1})

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\phi,\theta}(a_{1},\dots,a_{r-1})\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\phi,\theta}(b_{1},\dots,b_{r-1}),

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\phi,\theta}(a_{1}b_{1},\dots,a_{r-1}b_{r-1})

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\phi,\theta}(a_{1},\dots,a_{r-1})\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\phi,\theta}(b_{1},\dots,b_{r-1}).

Proposition 6.1.5
.

For
a
,
a_{1},\dots,a_{r-1}\in A_{+}
, the following hold.

(a)

\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\phi,\theta}(a_{1},\dots,a_{r-1})\in A
and
\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\phi,\theta}(a_{1},\dots,a_{r-1})\in A
.

(b)

\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\phi,\theta}(a,1,\dots,1)=\mu_{\phi,\theta}(a)
and
\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\phi,\theta}(a,1,\dots,1)=\nu_{\phi,\theta}(a)
.

(c)

\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\phi,\theta}(a_{1},\dots,a_{r-1})=\chi_{\phi}(a_{1}\cdots a_{r-1})\cdot\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\phi,\theta}(a_{r-1},\dots,a_{1})
.

(d)

We have

\displaystyle\deg_{\theta}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\phi,\theta}(a_{1},\dots,a_{r-1})

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{1}{r}\bigl((r-1)\deg_{\theta}a_{1}+(r-2)\deg_{\theta}a_{2}+\cdots+\deg_{\theta}a_{r-1}\bigr),

\displaystyle\deg_{\theta}\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\phi,\theta}(a_{1},\dots,a_{r})

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{1}{r}\bigl(\deg_{\theta}a_{1}+2\deg_{\theta}a_{2}+\cdots+(r-1)\deg_{\theta}a_{r-1}\bigr).

Proof.

By the multiplicativity of
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\phi,\theta}
and
\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\phi,\theta}
it suffices to check these identities on powers of an irreducible
f\in A_{+}
. We first verify (c). Let
k_{1},\dots,k_{r-1}\geqslant 0
. By (
2.3.7
) we have
\alpha_{1}\cdots\alpha_{r}=\chi_{\phi}(f)f^{-1}
. Substituting
x_{1}\leftarrow\alpha_{1}^{-1},\dots,x_{r}\leftarrow\alpha_{r}^{-1}
into Lemma
2.5.11
, we find from (
6.1.1
) and (
6.1.2
) that

\overline{\chi}_{\phi}(f)^{k_{1}+\cdots+k_{r-1}}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\phi,\theta}\bigl(f^{k_{1}},\dots,f^{k_{r-1}}\bigr)=\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\phi,\theta}\bigl(f^{k_{r-1}},\dots,f^{k_{1}}\bigr).

This implies the desired result since
\chi_{\phi}:A_{+}\to\mathbb{F}_{q}^{\times}
is completely multiplicative.

As
\alpha_{1}^{-1},\dots,\alpha_{r}^{-1}
are the roots of
P_{\phi,f}(X)
, which is a monic polynomial in
A[X]
, it follows that they are integral over
A
. Therefore, since Schur polynomials are symmetric with integer coefficients, (
6.1.2
) implies that
\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\phi,\theta}(f^{k_{1}},\dots,f^{k_{r-1}})\in A
. By part (c),
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\phi,\theta}(f^{k_{1}},\dots,f^{k_{r-1}})\in A
, and thus (a) is proved.

To prove (b) we combine (
2.3.4
) and (
2.5.2
) and note that for
k\geqslant 0
,

(6.1.6)

\mu_{\phi,\theta}\bigl(f^{k}\bigr)=h_{k}(f\alpha_{1},\dots,f\alpha_{r}),\quad\nu_{\phi,\theta}\bigl(f^{k}\bigr)=h_{k}\bigl(\alpha_{1}^{-1},\dots,\alpha_{r}^{-1}\bigr).

Then (
2.5.10
) implies
\mu_{\phi,\theta}(f^{k})=S_{k,0,\ldots,0}(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{r})f^{k}=\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\phi,\theta}(f^{k},1,\dots,1)
, and likewise
\nu_{\phi,\theta}(f^{k})=\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\phi,\theta}(f^{k},1,\dots,1)
. Finally for (d), we note that since
\deg_{\theta}\alpha_{i}^{-1}=d/r
for each
i
from §
2.3.2
, it follows from (
6.1.2
) that the degree of
\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\phi,\theta}(f^{k_{1}},\dots,f^{k_{r-1}})
in
\theta
is at most
(\deg S_{k_{1},\dots,k_{r-1}})\cdot d/r=(k_{1}+2k_{2}+\cdots+(r-1)k_{r-1})\cdot d/r
. The desired inequality for
\deg_{\theta}\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\phi,\theta}(a_{1},\dots,a_{r-1})
then follows from the multiplicativity of
\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\phi,\theta}
. The inequality for
\deg_{\theta}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\phi,\theta}(a_{1},\dots,a_{r-1})
then follows from (c).
∎

The functions
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\phi,\theta}
and
\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\phi,\theta}
satisfy a number of recursive relations induced by relations on Schur polynomials (cf.
[
38
]
*p. 278). Fix
f\in A_{+}
irreducible. Then Pieri’s rule (
2.5.16
) implies that for
k
,
k_{1},\dots,k_{r-1}\geqslant 0
,

(6.1.7)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\phi,\theta}\bigl(f^{k},

\displaystyle\,1,\dots,1\bigr)\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\phi,\theta}\bigl(f^{k_{1}},\dots,f^{k_{r-1}}\bigr)

\displaystyle=\begin{aligned} \smash{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\begin{subarray}{c}m_{0}+\cdots+m_{r-1}=k\\
m_{1}\leqslant k_{1},\,\ldots,\,m_{r-1}\leqslant k_{r-1}\end{subarray}\end{subarray}}}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\phi,\theta}\bigl(f^{k_{1}+m_{0}-m_{1}},f^{k_{2}+m_{1}-m_{2}},\dots,&\,f^{k_{r-1}+m_{r-2}-m_{r-1}}\bigr)\\
&{}\cdot\chi_{\phi}(f)^{m_{r-1}}f^{k-m_{0}},\end{aligned}

(6.1.8)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\phi,\theta}\bigl(f^{k},

\displaystyle\,1,\dots,1\bigr)\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\phi,\theta}\bigl(f^{k_{1}},\dots,f^{k_{r-1}}\bigr)

\displaystyle=\begin{aligned} \smash{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\begin{subarray}{c}m_{0}+\cdots+m_{r-1}=k\\
m_{1}\leqslant k_{1},\,\ldots,\,m_{r-1}\leqslant k_{r-1}\end{subarray}\end{subarray}}}\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\phi,\theta}\bigl(f^{k_{1}+m_{0}-m_{1}},f^{k_{2}+m_{1}-m_{2}},\dots,&\,f^{k_{r-1}+m_{r-2}-m_{r-1}}\bigr)\\
&{}\cdot\overline{\chi}_{\phi}(f)^{m_{r-1}}f^{m_{r-1}}.\end{aligned}

The dual Pieri rule (
2.5.17
) implies that for
0\leqslant k\leqslant r-1
,

(6.1.9)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\phi,\theta}

\displaystyle\bigl(\underbrace{1,\ldots,1,f,1,\ldots,1}_{\textup{$k$-th place}})\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\phi,\theta}\bigl(f^{k_{1}},\dots,f^{k_{r-1}}\bigr)

\displaystyle=\begin{aligned} \smash{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m_{0}+\cdots+m_{r-1}=k\\
(m_{0},\ldots,m_{r-1})\,\in\,\mathcal{I}_{k_{1},\dots,k_{r-1}}\end{subarray}}}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\phi,\theta}\bigl(f^{k_{1}+m_{0}-m_{1}},f^{k_{2}+m_{1}-m_{2}},\dots,&\,f^{k_{r-1}+m_{r-2}-m_{r-1}}\bigr)\\
&{}\cdot\chi_{\phi}(f)^{m_{r-1}}f^{1-m_{0}},\end{aligned}

(6.1.10)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\phi,\theta}

\displaystyle\bigl(\underbrace{1,\ldots,1,f,1,\ldots,1}_{\textup{$k$-th place}})\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\phi,\theta}\bigl(f^{k_{1}},\dots,f^{k_{r-1}}\bigr)

\displaystyle=\begin{aligned} \smash{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m_{0}+\cdots+m_{r-1}=k\\
(m_{0},\ldots,m_{r-1})\,\in\,\mathcal{I}_{k_{1},\dots,k_{r-1}}\end{subarray}}}\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\phi,\theta}\bigl(f^{k_{1}+m_{0}-m_{1}},f^{k_{2}+m_{1}-m_{2}},\dots,&\,f^{k_{r-1}+m_{r-2}-m_{r-1}}\bigr)\\
&{}\cdot\overline{\chi}_{\phi}(f)^{m_{r-1}}f^{m_{r-1}}.\end{aligned}

In particular for
k\geqslant 1
(cf.
[
38
]
*p. 278),

(6.1.11)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\phi,\theta}(f^{k},1,\dots,1)\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\phi,\theta}(f,1,\ldots,1)

\displaystyle=\begin{aligned} \boldsymbol{\mu}_{\phi,\theta}(f^{k+1},&\,1,\ldots,1)\\
&{}+\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\phi,\theta}(f^{k-1},f,1,\ldots,1)\cdot f,\end{aligned}

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\phi,\theta}(f^{k},1,\dots,1)\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\phi,\theta}(1,f,1,\ldots,1)

\displaystyle=\begin{aligned} \boldsymbol{\mu}_{\phi,\theta}(f^{k},&\,f,1,\ldots,1)\\
&{}+\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\phi,\theta}(f^{k-1},1,f,1,\ldots,1)\cdot f,\end{aligned}

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\phi,\theta}(f^{k},1,\dots,1)\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\phi,\theta}(1,1,f,1,\ldots,1)

\displaystyle=\begin{aligned} \boldsymbol{\mu}_{\phi,\theta}(f^{k},&\,1,f,1,\ldots,1)\\
&{}+\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\phi,\theta}(f^{k-1},1,1,f,1,\ldots,1)\cdot f,\end{aligned}

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\phi,\theta}(f^{k},1,\dots,1)\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\phi,\theta}(1,\ldots,1,f)

\displaystyle=\begin{aligned} \boldsymbol{\mu}_{\phi,\theta}(f^{k},&\,1,\ldots,1,f)\\
&{}+\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\phi,\theta}(f^{k-1},1,\ldots,1)\cdot\chi_{\phi}(f)f,\end{aligned}

and likewise,

(6.1.12)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\phi,\theta}(f^{k},1,\dots,1)\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\phi,\theta}(f,1,\ldots,1)

\displaystyle=\begin{aligned} \boldsymbol{\nu}_{\phi,\theta}(f^{k+1},&\,1,\ldots,1)\\
&{}+\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\phi,\theta}(f^{k-1},f,1,\ldots,1),\end{aligned}

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\phi,\theta}(f^{k},1,\dots,1)\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\phi,\theta}(1,f,1,\ldots,1)

\displaystyle=\begin{aligned} \boldsymbol{\nu}_{\phi,\theta}(f^{k},&\,f,1,\ldots,1)\\
&{}+\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\phi,\theta}(f^{k-1},1,f,1,\ldots,1),\end{aligned}

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\phi,\theta}(f^{k},1,\dots,1)\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\phi,\theta}(1,1,f,1,\ldots,1)

\displaystyle=\begin{aligned} \boldsymbol{\nu}_{\phi,\theta}(f^{k},&\,1,f,1,\ldots,1)\\
&{}+\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\phi,\theta}(f^{k-1},1,1,f,1,\ldots,1),\end{aligned}

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\phi,\theta}(f^{k},1,\dots,1)\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\phi,\theta}(1,\ldots,1,f)

\displaystyle=\begin{aligned} \boldsymbol{\nu}_{\phi,\theta}(f^{k},&\,1,\ldots,1,f)\\
&{}+\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\phi,\theta}(f^{k-1},1,\ldots,1)\cdot\overline{\chi}_{\phi}(f)f.\end{aligned}

6.1.13.
Calculating
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\phi,\theta}
and
\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\phi,\theta}

To calculate
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\phi,\theta}
and
\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\phi,\theta}
one can also use the Jacobi-Trudi identity from §
2.5.18
. Substituting (
6.1.6
) into (
2.5.19
) and using Proposition
6.1.5
(b), for
k_{1},\dots,k_{r-1}\geqslant 0
we have

(6.1.14)

\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\phi,\theta}\bigl(f^{k_{1}},\dots,f^{k_{r-1}}\bigr)=\det\Bigl(\nu_{\phi,\theta}\bigl(f^{k_{i}+\cdots+k_{r-1}-i+j}\bigr)\Bigr)_{i,j=1}^{r-1}.

We can use (
5.1.3
) to determine
P_{\phi,f}(X)
and then use (
2.3.11
) to obtain the entries of this determinant. Then
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\phi,\theta}(f^{k_{1}},\dots,f^{k_{r-1}})
can be found from Proposition
6.1.5
(c).

6.2.
Convolution
L
-series for
r=\ell

In this section we assume that
\phi
and
\psi
from (
4.1.1
) and (
4.1.2
) have the same rank
r=\ell\geqslant 2
, and we investigate

L(\mathbb{E}(\phi\times\psi)^{\vee},s)=\prod_{f}\mathbf{Q}_{f}^{\vee}\bigl(f^{-s}\bigr)^{-1}

from (
5.4.1
). We at first fix
f\in A_{+}
irreducible, and as previously we let
\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{r}\in\mkern 2.5mu\overline{\mkern-2.5muK}
be the roots of
P_{\phi,f}^{\vee}(X)
, and we let
\beta_{1},\dots,\beta_{r}\in\overline{\mathbb{F}_{q}(z)}
be the roots of
P_{\psi,f(z)}(X)
.

As
\mathbf{Q}_{f}^{\vee}(X)
is the reciprocal polynomial of
\mathbf{P}_{f}^{\vee}(X)=P_{\phi,f}^{\vee}(X)\otimes P_{\psi,f(z)}(X)
from Definition
4.1.19
, we can expand
\mathbf{Q}_{f}^{\vee}(f^{-s})^{-1}
using Cauchy’s identity (Theorem
2.5.13
). We note from (
2.3.7
) that
\alpha_{1}\cdots\alpha_{r}=\chi_{\phi}(f)f^{-1}
and
\beta_{1}\cdots\beta_{r}=\overline{\chi}_{\psi}(f)f(z)
. By the definitions of
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\phi,\theta}
and
\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\psi,z}
from (
6.1.1
) and (
6.1.2
), Theorem
2.5.13
implies

\displaystyle\mathbf{Q}_{f}^{\vee}\bigl(

\displaystyle f^{-s}\bigr)^{-1}

\displaystyle=\biggl(1-\frac{\chi_{\phi}(f)\overline{\chi}_{\psi}(f)f(z)}{f^{rs+1}}\biggr)^{-1}\underset{k=(k_{1},\dots,k_{r-1})}{\sum_{k_{1}=0}^{\infty}\cdots\sum_{k_{r-1}=0}^{\infty}}S_{k}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})S_{k}(\boldsymbol{\beta})f^{-s(k_{1}+2k_{2}+\cdots+(r-1)k_{r-1})}

\displaystyle=\begin{aligned} \biggl(1-\frac{\chi_{\phi}(f)\overline{\chi}_{\psi}(f)f(z)}{f^{rs+1}}\biggr)^{-1}\smash{\sum_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{r-1}\geqslant 0}}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\phi,\theta}&\bigl(f^{k_{1}},\dots,f^{k_{r-1}}\bigr)\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\psi,z}\bigl(f^{k_{1}},\dots,f^{k_{r-1}}\bigr)\\
&{}\cdot f^{-k_{1}-k_{2}-\cdots-k_{r-1}-s(k_{1}+2k_{2}+\cdots(r-1)k_{r-1})},\end{aligned}

where
\boldsymbol{\alpha}=(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{r})
and
\boldsymbol{\beta}=(\beta_{1},\dots,\beta_{r})
.
We define the twisted Pellarin
L
-series

(6.2.1)

L(\mathbb{A},\chi_{\phi}\overline{\chi}_{\psi},s)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\sum_{a\in A_{+}}\frac{\chi_{\phi}(a)\overline{\chi}_{\psi}(a)a(z)}{a^{s}},

and finally we define the
L
-series, for
\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\phi,z}(a_{1},\dots,a_{r-1})\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\phi,\theta}(a_{1},\dots,a_{r-1})|_{\theta=z}\in\mathbb{F}_{q}[z]
,

(6.2.2)

L(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\phi,\theta}\times\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\psi,z},s)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\sum_{a_{1}\in A_{+}}\cdots\sum_{a_{r-1}\in A_{+}}\frac{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\phi,\theta}(a_{1},\dots,a_{r-1})\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\psi,z}(a_{1},\dots,a_{r-1})}{a_{1}\cdots a_{r-1}(a_{1}a_{2}^{2}\cdots a_{r-1}^{r-1})^{s}}.

The convergence of this series in
\mathbb{T}_{z}(K_{\infty})
can be deduced from Proposition
6.1.5
(d) for
s\geqslant 0
.
After some straightforward simplification we arrive at the following result.

Theorem 6.2.3
.

Let
\phi
,
\psi:\mathsf{A}\to A[\tau]
be Drinfeld modules both of rank
r\geqslant 2
with everywhere good reduction, as defined in (
4.1.1
) and (
4.1.2
). Then

L(\mathbb{E}(\phi\times\psi)^{\vee},s)=L(\mathbb{A},\chi_{\phi}\overline{\chi}_{\psi},rs+1)\cdot L(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\phi,\theta}\times\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\psi,z},s).

We note that in the case that the leading coefficients
\kappa_{r}
and
\eta_{r}
of
\phi_{t}
and
\psi_{t}
are equal, then by (
2.3.6
) we have
L(\mathbb{A},\chi_{\phi}\overline{\chi}_{\psi},s)=L(\mathbb{A},s)
. We can substitute
s=0
into Theorem
6.2.3
and obtain the following special value identities.

Corollary 6.2.4
.

Let
\phi
,
\psi:\mathsf{A}\to A[\tau]
be Drinfeld modules both of rank
r\geqslant 2
with everywhere good reduction, as defined in (
4.1.1
) and (
4.1.2
).

(a)

If
\kappa_{r}=\eta_{r}
, then

\displaystyle L(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\phi,\theta}\times\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\psi,z},0)

\displaystyle=\sum_{a_{1}\in A_{+}}\cdots\sum_{a_{r-1}\in A_{+}}\frac{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\phi,\theta}(a_{1},\dots,a_{r-1})\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\psi,z}(a_{1},\dots,a_{r-1})}{a_{1}\cdots a_{r-1}}

\displaystyle=(\theta-z)\cdot\frac{\omega_{z}}{\widetilde{\pi}}\cdot\mathrm{Reg}_{\mathbb{E}}\cdot{[\mathrm{H}(\mathbb{E})]}_{\mathbb{A}}.

(b)

If
\kappa_{r}=\eta_{r}
and
\lVert\Upsilon_{\psi,z}\rVert<R_{\phi}
, where
R_{\phi}
is the radius of convergence of
\Log_{\phi}(\mathbf{z})
in (
2.2.5
), then

L(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\phi,\theta}\times\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\psi,z},0)=(\theta-z)\cdot\frac{\omega_{z}}{\widetilde{\pi}}\cdot\det\Bigl(\Upsilon_{\psi,z}^{-1}\Log_{\phi}\bigl(\Upsilon_{\psi,z}\bigr)\Bigr).

(c)

More generally, if we let
\gamma=(\kappa_{r}/\eta_{r})^{1/(q-1)}\in\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{\times}
, then

L(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\phi,\theta}\times\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\psi,z},0)=\frac{1}{\gamma\omega_{z}}\Log_{\mathsf{C}}\bigl(\gamma\omega_{z}\bigr)\cdot\mathrm{Reg}_{\mathbb{E}}\cdot{[\mathrm{H}(\mathbb{E})]}_{\mathbb{A}},

and if additionally
\lVert\Upsilon_{\psi,z}\rVert<R_{\phi}
,

L(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\phi,\theta}\times\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\psi,z},0)=\frac{1}{\gamma\omega_{z}}\Log_{\mathsf{C}}\bigl(\gamma\omega_{z}\bigr)\cdot\det\Bigl(\Upsilon_{\psi,z}^{-1}\Log_{\phi}\bigl(\Upsilon_{\psi,z}\bigr)\Bigr).

Proof.

By Theorem
6.2.3
we have
L(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\phi,\theta}\times\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\psi,z},0)=L(\mathbb{E}(\phi\times\psi)^{\vee},0)/L(\mathbb{A},1)
. Then
using Proposition
5.4.3
together with Pellarin’s identity for
L(\mathbb{A},1)
(Theorem
5.2.2
) and Demeslay’s special value formula (Theorem
5.3.10
), we obtain (a).

For (b), the discussion in Remark
5.3.6
implies that
\mathrm{Reg}_{\mathbb{E}}=\det(\Upsilon_{\psi,z}^{-1}\Log_{\phi}(\Upsilon_{\psi,z}))
, and so by (a) it remains to verify that
{[\mathrm{H}(\mathbb{E})]}_{\mathbb{A}}=1
in this case. Now by construction the product in Demeslay’s Theorem
5.3.10
has
\lVert\,\cdot\,\rVert
-norm
1
(moreover, this can be verified directly from (
5.4.1
)), and so by Proposition
5.4.3
,
\lVert L(\mathbb{E}(\phi\times\psi)^{\vee},0)\rVert=1
. One checks easily that
\lVert L(\mathbb{A},1)\rVert=1
, and so
\lVert L(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\phi,\theta}\times\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\psi,z},0)\rVert=1
. Furthermore, we have
\lVert\det(\Upsilon_{\psi,z}^{-1}\Log_{\phi}(\Upsilon_{\psi,z}))\rVert=1
by Remark
5.3.6
, so both sides of the proposed identity in (b) have
\lVert\,\cdot\,\rVert
-norm
1
. Since
{[\mathrm{H}(\mathbb{E})]}_{\mathbb{A}}
is monic in
\mathbb{A}=\mathbb{F}_{q}(z)[\theta]
, this cannot be the case if
{[\mathrm{H}(\mathbb{E})]}_{\mathbb{A}}\neq 1
.
Part (c) follows in exactly the same way, using the identity

L(\mathbb{A},\chi_{\phi}\overline{\chi}_{\psi},1)=\frac{1}{\gamma\omega_{z}}\Log_{\mathsf{C}}\bigl(\gamma\omega_{z}\bigr),

which is due to Anglès, Pellarin, and Tavares Ribeiro
[
8
]
*Eqs. (18), (25).
∎

6.3.
Convolution
L
-series for
r\neq\ell

We continue with the same situation as in §
6.2
, but here we assume that the ranks of
\phi
and
\psi
are not equal. This splits into two cases,
r<\ell
and
r>\ell
. As we have addressed the cases where either
\phi
or
\psi
is the Carlitz module in Examples
5.4.4
and
5.4.7
, we will assume that
r
,
\ell\geqslant 2
.

6.3.1.
The case
r<\ell

Let
f\in A_{+}
be irreducible, and let
\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{r}\in\mkern 2.5mu\overline{\mkern-2.5muK}
be the roots of
P_{\phi,f}^{\vee}(X)
and
\beta_{1},\dots,\beta_{\ell}\in\overline{\mathbb{F}_{q}(z)}
the roots of
P_{\psi,f(z)}(X)
. Using that
\alpha_{1}\cdots\alpha_{r}=\chi_{\phi}(f)f^{-1}
, we apply Bump’s specialization of Cauchy’s identity (Corollary
2.5.14
), and similar to calculations in §
6.2
, we find

\displaystyle\mathbf{Q}_{f}^{\vee}\bigl(f^{-s}\bigr)^{-1}

\displaystyle=\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}k=(k_{1},\dots,k_{r-1})\\
k^{\prime}=(k_{1},\dots,k_{r},0\dots,0)\end{subarray}}{\sum_{k_{1}=0}^{\infty}\cdots\sum_{k_{r}=0}^{\infty}}S_{k}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})S_{k^{\prime}}(\boldsymbol{\beta})\bigl(\chi_{\phi}(f)f^{-1}\bigr)^{k_{r}}f^{-s(k_{1}+2k_{2}+\cdots+rk_{r})}

\displaystyle=\begin{aligned} \smash{\sum_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{r}\geqslant 0}}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\phi,\theta}\bigl(f^{k_{1}},\dots,f^{k_{r-1}}\bigr)&\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\psi,z}\bigl(f^{k_{1}},\dots,f^{k_{r}},1,\ldots,1\bigr)\\
&{}\cdot\chi_{\phi}\bigl(f^{k_{r}}\bigr)f^{-k_{1}-k_{2}-\cdots-k_{r}-s(k_{1}+2k_{2}+\cdots+rk_{r})}.\end{aligned}

The expression
\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\psi,z}(f^{k_{1}},\dots,f^{k_{r}},1,\ldots,1)
generically has
1
’s in exactly the last
\ell-1-r
places. We thus define the
L
-series when
r<\ell
,

(6.3.2)

L(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\phi,\theta}\times\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\psi,z},s)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\sum_{a_{1},\ldots,a_{r}\in A_{+}}\frac{\chi_{\phi}(a_{r})\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\phi,\theta}(a_{1},\dots,a_{r-1})\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\psi,z}(a_{1},\dots,a_{r},1,\ldots,1)}{a_{1}\cdots a_{r}(a_{1}a_{2}^{2}\cdots a_{r}^{r})^{s}}.

6.3.3.
The case
r>\ell

The case that
r>\ell
is similar to the
r<\ell
case, making use of Corollary
2.5.14
again, with only minor modifications. We skip the details, but when
r>\ell
we define the
L
-series

(6.3.4)

L(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\phi,\theta}\times\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\psi,z},s)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\\
\sum_{a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\in A_{+}}\frac{\overline{\chi}_{\psi}(a_{\ell})a_{\ell}(z)\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\phi,\theta}(a_{1},\dots,a_{\ell},1,\dots,1)\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\psi,z}(a_{1},\dots,a_{\ell-1})}{a_{1}\cdots a_{\ell}(a_{1}a_{2}^{2}\cdots a_{\ell}^{\ell})^{s}}.

After some reasonably straightforward calculation that we omit, we obtain the following theorem that covers both cases.

Theorem 6.3.5
.

Let
\phi
,
\psi:\mathsf{A}\to A[\tau]
be Drinfeld modules of ranks
r
and
\ell
respectively with everywhere good reduction, as defined in (
4.1.1
) and (
4.1.2
). Assume that
r
,
\ell\geqslant 2
and that
r\neq\ell
. Then

L(\mathbb{E}(\phi\times\psi)^{\vee},s)=L(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\phi,\theta}\times\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\psi,z},s).

If we degenerate the expressions in (
6.3.2
) and (
6.3.4
) to the
r=1
or
\ell=1
cases of the Carlitz module, then we recover the identities in (
5.4.5
) and (
5.4.8
).
Just as for Corollary
6.2.4
, we obtain the following result with the same proof, though there is no longer a factor coming from
L(\mathbb{A},\chi_{\phi}\overline{\chi}_{\psi},1)
.

Corollary 6.3.6
.

Let
\phi
,
\psi:\mathsf{A}\to A[\tau]
be Drinfeld modules of ranks
r
and
\ell
respectively with everywhere good reduction, as defined in (
4.1.1
) and (
4.1.2
). Assume that
r
,
\ell\geqslant 2
and that
r\neq\ell
.

(a)

If
r<\ell
, then

\displaystyle L(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\phi,\theta}\times\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\psi,z},0)

\displaystyle=\sum_{a_{1},\ldots,a_{r}\in A_{+}}\frac{\chi_{\phi}(a_{r})\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\phi,\theta}(a_{1},\dots,a_{r-1})\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\psi,z}(a_{1},\dots,a_{r},1,\dots,1)}{a_{1}\cdots a_{r}}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Reg}_{\mathbb{E}}\cdot{[\mathrm{H}(\mathbb{E})]}_{\mathbb{A}}.

(b)

If
r>\ell
, then

\displaystyle L(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\phi,\theta}\times\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\psi,z},0)

\displaystyle=\sum_{a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\in A_{+}}\frac{\overline{\chi}_{\psi}(a_{\ell})a_{\ell}(z)\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\phi,\theta}(a_{1},\dots,a_{\ell},1,\ldots,1)\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\psi,z}(a_{1},\dots,a_{\ell-1})}{a_{1}\cdots a_{\ell}}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Reg}_{\mathbb{E}}\cdot{[\mathrm{H}(\mathbb{E})]}_{\mathbb{A}}.

(c)

If
\lVert\Upsilon_{\psi,z}\rVert<R_{\phi}
, where
R_{\phi}
is the radius of convergence of
\Log_{\phi}(\mathbf{z})
in (
2.2.5
), then

L(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\phi,\theta}\times\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\psi,z},0)=\det\Bigl(\Upsilon_{\psi,z}^{-1}\Log_{\phi}\bigl(\Upsilon_{\psi,z}\bigr)\Bigr).

6.4.
Calculations and examples

In this section we consider computations of the special value formulas in Corollaries
6.2.4
and
6.3.6
. In practice,
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\phi,\theta}
and
\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\psi,z}
can be evaluated using the Jacobi-Trudi identity as described in §
6.1.13
. Unfortunately the convergence of
L(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\phi,\theta}\times\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\psi,z},0)
is slow and yields few digits of accuracy in
\mathbb{T}_{z}(K_{\infty})
. In the first three examples we have
\lVert\Upsilon_{\psi,z}\rVert<R_{\phi}
, and we consider the complementary case in §
6.4.4
and Example
6.4.6
.

Example 6.4.1
.

(
r=\ell=2
).
We let
q=3
and let
\phi
,
\psi:\mathbb{F}_{3}[t]\to\mathbb{F}_{3}[\theta][\tau]
be defined by

\phi_{t}=\theta+\theta^{2}\tau-\tau^{2},\quad\psi_{t}=\theta+\tau-\tau^{2}.

Then for
\mathbb{E}=\mathbb{E}(\phi\times\psi)
,

\mathbb{E}_{t}=\begin{pmatrix}\theta&0\\
0&\theta\end{pmatrix}+\begin{pmatrix}0&-z\theta^{2}+\theta^{3}\\
\theta^{2}&\theta^{2}\end{pmatrix}\tau+\begin{pmatrix}z-\theta&z-\theta\\
-1&z-1-\theta^{3}\end{pmatrix}\tau^{2}.

For example calculations, when
f=\theta^{2}+1
and
g=\theta^{3}-\theta-1
, we find that

\displaystyle P_{\phi,f}(X)

\displaystyle=X^{2}-(\theta+1)X+\theta^{2}+1,

\displaystyle P_{\psi,f}(X)

\displaystyle=X^{2}-(\theta+1)+\theta^{2}+1,

\displaystyle P_{\phi,g}(X)

\displaystyle=X^{2}-(\theta-1)X+\theta^{3}-\theta-1,

\displaystyle P_{\psi,g}(X)

\displaystyle=X^{2}-X+\theta^{3}-\theta-1,

and thus by (
2.3.9
),
\mu_{\phi,\theta}(\theta^{2}+1)=\theta+1
,
\mu_{\phi,\theta}(\theta^{3}-\theta-1)=\theta-1
,
\nu_{\psi,z}(\theta^{2}+1)=z+1
, and
\nu_{\psi,z}(\theta^{3}-\theta-1)=1
. Moreover, Definition
4.1.19
implies

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}_{f}(X)

\displaystyle=\begin{aligned} X^{4}-\frac{(\theta+1)(z+1)}{z^{2}+1}X^{3}&{}-\frac{(z+\theta)(\theta z+1)}{(z^{2}+1)^{2}}X^{2}\\
&{}-\frac{(\theta+1)(\theta^{2}+1)(z+1)}{(z^{2}+1)^{2}}X+\frac{(\theta^{2}+1)^{2}}{(z^{2}+1)^{2}},\end{aligned}

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}_{g}(X)

\displaystyle=\begin{aligned} X^{4}-\frac{\theta-1}{z^{3}-z-1}X^{3}&{}+\frac{(z^{3}-z)\theta^{3}+(z^{3}-z-1)\theta^{2}-\theta-1}{(z^{3}-z-1)^{2}}X^{2}\\
&{}-\frac{(\theta-1)(\theta^{3}-\theta-1)}{(z^{3}-z-1)^{2}}X+\frac{(\theta^{3}-\theta-1)^{2}}{(z^{3}-z-1)^{2}}.\end{aligned}

Theorem
4.2.2
then yields

\displaystyle{[\mkern 1.0mu\overline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathbb{E}}(\mathbb{F}_{f}(z))]}_{\mathbb{A}}

\displaystyle=(z^{2}+1)^{2}\cdot\mathbf{P}_{f}(1)

\displaystyle=\theta^{4}-(z+1)\theta^{3}+(z+1)\theta^{2}-(z^{3}-z^{2}-z)\theta+z^{4}-z^{3}+z^{2},

\displaystyle{[\mkern 1.0mu\overline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathbb{E}}(\mathbb{F}_{g}(z))]}_{\mathbb{A}}

\displaystyle=(z^{3}-z-1)^{2}\cdot\mathbf{P}_{g}(1)

\displaystyle=\begin{aligned} \theta^{6}+(z^{3}-z-1)\theta^{3}&{}+(z^{3}-z+1)\theta^{2}\\
&{}-(z^{3}-z+1)\theta+z^{6}+z^{4}-z^{3}+z^{2}+z-1.\end{aligned}

Now to examine
L(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\phi,\theta}\times\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\psi,z},0)
, we note from
[
49
]
*Eq. (2.11) that
R_{\phi}=\lvert\theta\rvert^{1/2}
, and from
[
49
]
*Thm. 4.4
[
51
]
*Thm. 3.1 it follows that
\lVert\Upsilon_{\psi,z}\rVert=\lvert\theta\rvert^{3/8}
. Thus we are in the situation of Corollary
6.2.4
(b) with
r=\ell=2
. We find through direct calculation that

\displaystyle L(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\phi,\theta}\times\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\psi,z},0)=\sum_{a\in A_{+}}\frac{\mu_{\phi,\theta}(a)\nu_{\psi,z}(a)}{a}=

\displaystyle\>1-\theta^{-1}+z\theta^{-2}-\theta^{-3}+\theta^{-4}+(1-z)\theta^{-5}

\displaystyle{}+z\theta^{-6}+(z+1)^{2}\theta^{-8}-(z+1)\theta^{-9}+O(\theta^{-10}).

The quantity
\det(\Upsilon_{\psi,z}^{-1}\Log_{\phi}(\Upsilon_{\psi,z}))=\det\Log_{\mathbb{E}}(\mathrm{I}_{2})
can be calculated to high precision via (
4.1.10
), and as expected from Corollary
6.2.4
(b), the product
(\theta-z)\cdot\omega_{z}/\widetilde{\pi}\cdot\det(\Upsilon_{\psi,z}^{-1}\Log_{\phi}(\Upsilon_{\psi,z}))
agrees numerically with this value.

Example 6.4.2
.

(
r=3
,
\ell=2
)
Let
\phi
,
\psi:\mathbb{F}_{3}[t]\to\mathbb{F}_{3}[\theta][\tau]
be defined by

\phi_{t}=\theta+\theta^{2}\tau+\theta\tau^{2}+\tau^{3},\quad\psi_{t}=\theta-\tau+\tau^{2}.

We find that for
\mathbb{E}=\mathbb{E}(\phi\times\psi)
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{t}=\begin{pmatrix}\theta&0\\
0&\theta\end{pmatrix}+\theta^{2}\begin{pmatrix}0&z-\theta\\
1&1\end{pmatrix}\tau

\displaystyle{}+\theta\begin{pmatrix}z-\theta&z-\theta\\
1&z+1-\theta^{3}\end{pmatrix}\tau^{2}

\displaystyle{}+\begin{pmatrix}z-\theta&(z+1-\theta^{9})(z-\theta)\\
z+1-\theta^{3}&-z+1-\theta^{3}-\theta^{9}\end{pmatrix}\tau^{3}.

Again
[
49
]
*Eq. (2.11) implies that
R_{\phi}=\lvert\theta\rvert^{1/2}
, and we find from
[
49
]
*Thm. 4.4
[
51
]
*Thm. 3.1 that
\lVert\Upsilon_{\psi,z}\rVert=\lvert\theta\rvert^{3/8}
. We calculate

\displaystyle L(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\phi,\theta}\times\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\psi,z},0)
